import Viz from "../../components/Viz.astro";

<Fragment set:html={"<h2 id=\"s-3\">3. Anatomy of MoE Training</h2><p class=\"full\">The MoE from <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-2-5\">2.5</a> added parts the dense run never had: a router that picks eight experts for each token, experts that can end up with very different amounts of work, and an all-to-all that moves tokens between GPUs. Each of them can slow training or hurt the loss, and a gap against the dense reference does not, on its own, tell you which part caused it.</p><p class=\"full\">The figure below draws one MoE layer at a smaller scale than ours, with 16 experts and top-4 instead of 64 and top-8, plus a shared expert that ours does not have. It shows the tokens of a batch, the router, the experts and the sum that combines their outputs. Each section then opens on its own view of the same drawing, zoomed onto the part it covers, so you can skip to the part you need. A few views also carry a result from their section, such as the rogue experts that Step <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-5\">3.5</a> Flash&#x27;s training loss did not reveal (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-6\">3.6</a>)<sup data-k=\"step35-f4\"><a href=\"#ref-2602.10604\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">15</a></sup>.</p>"} />

<Viz name="hero-anatomy-zoom" height={596} caption="Figure 3" />

<Fragment set:html={"<p class=\"full\">After the monitoring setup in <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-0\">3.0</a>, we start inside the router, with how its scores become the weights of the chosen experts and whether tokens choose experts or experts choose tokens. Then come uneven load across experts, how many experts to use and how large, which layers get experts at all, and how to start a run and keep it stable. We end with where a training step spends its time on the GPUs.</p><h3 id=\"s-3-0\">3.0 Monitoring: turn it on once</h3>\n<p class=\"full\">Before changing any setting, turn on what tells you whether it worked. You do not need to know what every number means yet: each section introduces a metric when it first needs it (z-loss in <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-1\">3.1</a>, MaxVio in <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-2\">3.2</a>, timings and memory in <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-7\">3.7</a>). Appendix <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a> gives the capture commands, calculations and limits of each measurement.</p>\n\n<h4 id=\"s-3-0-what-to-turn-on\">What to turn on</h4><p class=\"full\">The <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-2-5\">2.5</a> run already logs the router losses per layer (<code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-per-layer-logging</span></code>), so you can see which layer misbehaves (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-1\">3.1</a>, <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-3\">3.3</a>, <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>). Keep the <a href=\"https://huggingface.co/docs/trackio\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\">Trackio</a> launcher from <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-2-4\">2.4</a> and add these flags to the <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-2-5\">2.5</a> flags:</p><div class=\"full code-window code-command\"><div class=\"code-toolbar\"><span class=\"window-dots\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><i></i><i></i><i></i></span><button class=\"copy-code\" type=\"button\" aria-label=\"Copy commands\">Copy</button></div><pre class=\"code-content\" tabindex=\"0\" aria-label=\"Commands\"><code><span class=\"syntax-keyword\">--save</span> &lt;dir&gt; <span class=\"syntax-keyword\">--save-interval</span> &lt;m&gt;\n<span class=\"syntax-keyword\">--save-tokens-per-expert-interval</span> &lt;n&gt;\n<span class=\"syntax-keyword\">--log-throughput</span> <span class=\"syntax-keyword\">--log-params-norm</span>\n<span class=\"syntax-keyword\">--log-num-zeros-in-grad</span> <span class=\"syntax-keyword\">--log-max-attention-logit</span></code></pre></div><p class=\"full\">Megatron writes the token counts and the activation dump below under <code><span class=\"nw\">--save</span></code> (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/training/training.py#L3398-L3401\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">training.py#L3398-L3401</span></code></a>) and asks for <code><span class=\"nw\">--save-interval</span></code> beside it (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/training/arguments.py#L1321-L1323\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">arguments.py#L1321-L1323</span></code></a>), so the run also saves checkpoints there. The next flag records how many tokens each expert received (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-3\">3.3</a>, <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>). The other four track the run&#x27;s health: throughput, the parameter norm, the zeros in the gradient and the largest attention logit (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-6\">3.6</a>, <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>). The scalar metrics appear in the same Trackio project as your baseline, while routing captures stay in their own files. The NaN check is already on (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/training/arguments.py#L2825-L2827\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">arguments.py#L2825-L2827</span></code></a>).</p>\n\n<h4 id=\"s-3-0-what-to-capture-when-you-need-it\">What to capture when you need it</h4><p class=\"full\">Two more measurements are bounded captures: run them when a question calls for them.</p><p class=\"full\">The routing trace records which experts each token picked (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-1\">3.1</a>, <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-3\">3.3</a>, <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>). Its hooks on the routers do not fire under CUDA graph replay (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/router_trace.py#L23\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code>router_trace.py#L23</code></a>), and by default it traces every iteration (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/training/config/training_config.py#L363-L367\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">training_config.py#L363-L367</span></code></a>), so turn graphs off and bound it:</p><div class=\"full code-window code-command\"><div class=\"code-toolbar\"><span class=\"window-dots\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><i></i><i></i><i></i></span><button class=\"copy-code\" type=\"button\" aria-label=\"Copy commands\">Copy</button></div><pre class=\"code-content\" tabindex=\"0\" aria-label=\"Commands\"><code><span class=\"syntax-keyword\">--moe-routing-trace-path</span> &lt;dir&gt; <span class=\"syntax-keyword\">--moe-routing-trace-max-training-iters</span> &lt;n&gt;\n<span class=\"syntax-keyword\">--cuda-graph-impl</span> none</code></pre></div><p class=\"full\">The activation dump saves the experts&#x27; activations under the same <code><span class=\"nw\">--save</span></code> directory, so you can compute per-expert activation norms offline (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-4\">3.4</a>, <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>):</p><div class=\"full code-window code-command\"><div class=\"code-toolbar\"><span class=\"window-dots\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><i></i><i></i><i></i></span><button class=\"copy-code\" type=\"button\" aria-label=\"Copy commands\">Copy</button></div><pre class=\"code-content\" tabindex=\"0\" aria-label=\"Commands\"><code><span class=\"syntax-keyword\">--save-activations-interval</span> &lt;n&gt;</code></pre></div>\n<p class=\"full\">With monitoring on, let&#x27;s start where every token starts: the router. It picks the experts, but how much does each one count in the token&#x27;s output?</p>\n<h3 id=\"s-3-1\">3.1 Router scoring: how logits become weights</h3>\n"} />

<Viz name="hero-anatomy-zoom" query="&stage=2&mini=1" w={600} h={347} />

<Fragment set:html={"\n<p class=\"full\">The router turns each token into one logit per expert, and in plain top-k routing the top-k logits pick the experts the token goes to. Between those logits and the weights that mix the experts&#x27; outputs sit four choices. The gating function, the output scale (a constant multiplied onto the weighted sum of the chosen experts&#x27; outputs) and the sharpness (how peaked the router&#x27;s scores are, so how much the top expert dominates) change how much each chosen expert counts in the token&#x27;s output, and how large that output is. In plain top-k routing, for a given token&#x27;s logits, none of them changes which experts win: each keeps the experts in the same order, or applies after the top-k.</p><p class=\"full\">Precision is the exception. A <a href=\"https://huggingface.co/spaces/nanotron/ultrascale-playbook?section=fp16_and_bf16_training\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\">bf16</a> router can round two close logits to the same value and so change the winners, which makes an fp32 router the one setting to always use here.</p><p class=\"full\">The table below shows what ten current open MoEs chose for each of the four, one row per model.</p>\n<p class=\"meta\">how current open MoEs set up their routers, one column per part of this section. Values come from each model&#x27;s <code>config.json</code> and modeling code, training precision from its tech report; \u221ak is computed, and shown wherever the weights sum to 1.</p><table class=\"glance\"><thead><tr><th>Model</th><th>Gating function</th><th>Output scale</th><th>Sharpness</th><th>Router precision: in training; in inference code</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash</td><td>sqrt(softplus)</td><td>renormalized, 1.5 at k = 6 (\u221ak = 2.45)</td><td>a temperature setting, left at 1.0<sup data-k=\"hub-dsv41\"><a href=\"#ref-src-hub-dsv41\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">16</a></sup></td><td>not stated; fp32<sup data-k=\"hub-dsv41\"><a href=\"#ref-src-hub-dsv41\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">16</a></sup></td></tr><tr><td>Gemma 4 26B-A4B</td><td>softmax</td><td>renormalized, then a learned per-expert scale, k = 8 of 128 (\u221ak = 2.83)<sup data-k=\"tf-gemma4\"><a href=\"#ref-src-tf-gemma4\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">17</a></sup></td><td>normalized router input: RMSNorm, a learned scale and 1/\u221ad<sup data-k=\"tf-gemma4\"><a href=\"#ref-src-tf-gemma4\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">17</a></sup></td><td>not stated; bf16, softmax in fp32<sup data-k=\"tf-gemma4\"><a href=\"#ref-src-tf-gemma4\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">17</a></sup></td></tr><tr><td>GLM-5.3</td><td>sigmoid</td><td>renormalized, <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-2-5\">2.5</a> at k = 8 (\u221ak = 2.83)</td><td>none</td><td>not stated; fp32<sup data-k=\"tf-glm-moe-dsa\"><a href=\"#ref-src-tf-glm-moe-dsa\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">18</a></sup></td></tr><tr><td>Inkling</td><td>sigmoid, normalized jointly over the top-6 and its 2 shared experts<sup data-k=\"tf-inkling\"><a href=\"#ref-src-tf-inkling\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">19</a></sup></td><td>8.0 times a learned global scale, over 6 routed plus 2 shared (\u221a8 = 2.83)<sup data-k=\"tf-inkling\"><a href=\"#ref-src-tf-inkling\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">19</a></sup></td><td>none</td><td>not stated; bf16<sup data-k=\"tf-inkling\"><a href=\"#ref-src-tf-inkling\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">19</a></sup></td></tr><tr><td>Kimi K3</td><td>sigmoid</td><td>renormalized, 1.0 at k = 16 of 896 (\u221ak = 4)<sup data-k=\"hub-kimi-k3\"><a href=\"#ref-src-hub-kimi-k3\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">20</a></sup></td><td>none</td><td>above MXFP4 in post-training QAT<sup data-k=\"k3-qat\"><a href=\"#ref-doc-5\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">21</a></sup>; fp32<sup data-k=\"hub-kimi-k3\"><a href=\"#ref-src-hub-kimi-k3\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">20</a></sup></td></tr><tr><td>MiMo-V2.6-Pro-RL</td><td>sigmoid</td><td>renormalized, no factor at k = 8 (\u221ak = 2.83)</td><td>none</td><td>not stated; fp32<sup data-k=\"hub-mimo26\"><a href=\"#ref-src-hub-mimo26\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">22</a></sup></td></tr><tr><td>North-Mini-Code</td><td>sigmoid</td><td>not renormalized: the weights are the raw scores, k = 8 of 128<sup data-k=\"tf-cohere2-moe\"><a href=\"#ref-src-tf-cohere2-moe\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">23</a></sup></td><td>none</td><td>not stated; bf16<sup data-k=\"tf-cohere2-moe\"><a href=\"#ref-src-tf-cohere2-moe\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">23</a></sup></td></tr><tr><td>Qwen3.8-Flash-Next</td><td>softmax<sup data-k=\"tf-qwen4-exp\"><a href=\"#ref-src-tf-qwen4-exp\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">24</a></sup></td><td>renormalized, no factor at k = 10 (\u221ak = 3.16)<sup data-k=\"tf-qwen4-exp\"><a href=\"#ref-src-tf-qwen4-exp\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">24</a></sup></td><td>none</td><td>not stated; bf16, softmax in fp32<sup data-k=\"tf-qwen4-exp\"><a href=\"#ref-src-tf-qwen4-exp\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">24</a></sup></td></tr><tr><td>Step-5-Preview</td><td>sigmoid<sup data-k=\"hub-step5-router\"><a href=\"#ref-src-hub-step5-router\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">25</a></sup></td><td>renormalized, <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-0\">3.0</a> at k = 8 of 352 (\u221ak = 2.83)<sup data-k=\"hub-step5-router\"><a href=\"#ref-src-hub-step5-router\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">25</a></sup></td><td>none</td><td>not stated; fp32<sup data-k=\"hub-step5-router\"><a href=\"#ref-src-hub-step5-router\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">25</a></sup></td></tr><tr><td>ZAYA1-8B</td><td>softmax over an MLP router&#x27;s logits<sup data-k=\"tf-zaya\"><a href=\"#ref-src-tf-zaya\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">26</a></sup></td><td>not renormalized, top-1 of 16 plus a skip expert<sup data-k=\"tf-zaya\"><a href=\"#ref-src-tf-zaya\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">26</a></sup></td><td>none</td><td>softmax in fp32 during RL; bf16<sup data-k=\"tf-zaya\"><a href=\"#ref-src-tf-zaya\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">26</a></sup></td></tr></tbody></table>\n<p class=\"full\">Let&#x27;s start with the gating function. If it never changes the winners, what does it change?</p>\n<h4 id=\"s-3-1-gating-function\">Gating function</h4>\n<p class=\"full\">Softmax, sigmoid and sqrt(softplus) rank the experts the same way, so what differs is the weight each chosen expert gets. The figure below raises one token&#x27;s logits from an unsure router to a confident one: softmax makes the top expert count more and more, while sigmoid saturates at large logits, so confident experts end up with equal weights. Gating functions keep the ranking when selection uses their scores alone. The expert bias of <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-3\">3.3</a> is added to the gated scores before selection (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/moe_utils.py#L936-L938\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">moe_utils.py#L936-L938</span></code></a>), so how far it moves an expert depends on the size of the scores, and a different gating function can change the top-k. Group-limited routing (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-7\">3.7</a>) can too, because it picks groups by summing their best scores (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/moe_utils.py#L712-L715\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">moe_utils.py#L712-L715</span></code></a>).</p>\n"} />

<Viz name="router-score-shape" height={838} caption="Figure 3.1.1" />

<Fragment set:html={"\n\n\n\n<p class=\"full\">In the figure, sqrt(softplus) lands between softmax and sigmoid: for positive logits, the ratio of two weights tends to the square root of the ratio of their logits as the scale grows. DeepSeek-V4 switched to sqrt(softplus) from sigmoid<sup data-k=\"dsv4-sqrtsp\"><a href=\"#ref-2606.19348\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">27</a></sup>. In <a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\">Megatron</a>, the expert bias of <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-3\">3.3</a> needs sigmoid or sqrt(softplus): it refuses the bias with softmax at startup (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/transformer_config.py#L3139-L3147\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">transformer_config.py#L3139-L3147</span></code></a>), and its softmax path never adds it. Wang et al., who proposed the bias<sup data-k=\"lossfree-softmax\"><a href=\"#ref-2408.15664\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">28</a></sup>, explain why it fits softmax badly: softmax&#x27;s normalization makes the gap between two experts depend on every other expert&#x27;s score, so the bias is harder to tune. They still balanced a softmax router with it<sup data-k=\"lossfree-softmax\"><a href=\"#ref-2408.15664\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">28</a></sup>. The auxiliary loss works with all three (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/moe_utils.py#L998-L1014\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">moe_utils.py#L998-L1014</span></code></a>).</p>\n<details><summary>Megatron source</summary><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/moe_utils.py#L913-L927\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">moe_utils.py#L913-L927</span></code></a> (softmax, before or after top-k)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">913</span><span class=\"code\" class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">914</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">if</span> use_pre_softmax:</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">915</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\">scores = torch.softmax(logits, dim=-<span class=\"hl-m\">1</span>, dtype=torch.float32)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">916</span><span topk, num_groups, group_topk)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">921</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">else</span>:</span></span><span class=\"hl-m\">1</span>, index=top_indices)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">936</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">elif</span> expert_bias <span class=\"hl-k\">is</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">not</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">None</span>:</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">937</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\">scores_for_routing = scores + expert_bias.float()</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">938</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\">_, top_indices = compute_topk(scores_for_routing, topk, num_groups, group_topk)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">939</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\">scores = torch.gather(scores, dim=<span class=\"hl-m\">1</span>, index=top_indices)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">940</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">else</span>:</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">941</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\">scores, top_indices = compute_topk(scores, topk, num_groups, group_topk)</span></span></pre></details><p>(sigmoid, sqrt(softplus), bias only selects),</p><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/transformer_config.py#L3139-L3147\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">transformer_config.py#L3139-L3147</span></code></a> (the bias refused with softmax)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">3139</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">if</span> <span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.moe_router_enable_expert_bias <span class=\"hl-k\">and</span> <span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.moe_router_score_function <span class=\"hl-k\">not</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">in</span> (</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">3140</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span><span class=\"hl-s\">sigmoid</span><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span>,</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">3141</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span><span class=\"hl-s\">sqrtsoftplus</span><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span>,</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">3142</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\">):</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">3143</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">raise</span> ValueError(</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">3144</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\"><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span><span class=\"hl-s\">Expert bias for aux-loss-free routing only supports </span><span class=\"hl-s\">&#x27;</span><span class=\"hl-s\">sigmoid</span><span class=\"hl-s\">&#x27;</span> <span class=\"hl-s\">and </span><span class=\"hl-s\">&#x27;</span><span class=\"hl-s\">sqrtsoftplus</span><span class=\"hl-s\">&#x27;</span> <span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">3145</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\"><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span><span class=\"hl-s\">score functions. Please set --moe-router-score-function to </span><span class=\"hl-s\">&#x27;</span><span class=\"hl-s\">sigmoid</span><span class=\"hl-s\">&#x27;</span> <span class=\"hl-s\">or </span><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">3146</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\"><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span><span class=\"hl-s\">&#x27;</span><span class=\"hl-s\">sqrtsoftplus</span><span class=\"hl-s\">&#x27;</span><span class=\"hl-s\">, or unset --moe-router-enable-expert-bias.</span><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">3147</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">)</span></span></pre></details></details>\n<p class=\"full watch\">Sigmoid gives the chosen experts equal weights once all their logits are large and positive, which is where the simulation&#x27;s scaled-up token ends: a confident router with the flat gate that Sharpness returns to. To see whether a run&#x27;s router gets there, record the routing trace with its logits on (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>) and follow two numbers per layer: the top expert&#x27;s weight, and the routing entropy, which is low when one expert takes most of the weight and high when the weights are spread.</p><p class=\"full rec\">Use sigmoid (or sqrt(softplus)) when you balance with the expert bias (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-3\">3.3</a>), since Megatron refuses the bias with softmax. Otherwise use softmax, Megatron&#x27;s default.</p>\n<p class=\"full\">The router&#x27;s weights do two jobs. They pick the experts, and they are also the numbers that mix the experts&#x27; outputs: the layer returns each chosen expert&#x27;s output times its weight, summed. The gating function decides how much each chosen expert counts against the others. It does not decide how much they count together: with softmax, the top-k weights sum to less than 1, by a different amount for each token, so the size of the layer&#x27;s output moves with them. So how big should that combined output be?</p>\n<h4 id=\"s-3-1-output-scale\">Output scale</h4>\n<p class=\"full\">Two settings decide how big the experts&#x27; combined output is. The first is renormalization: rescale the k chosen weights so they sum to 1, and the output&#x27;s size no longer moves with their sum. In Krajewski et al. renormalizing lowered validation loss from 2.219 to 2.183 at granularity 8<span class=\"sn\">Granularity 8: experts 8 times smaller, 8 times as many, and 8 times as many chosen per token, so parameters and compute stay the same; <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-4\">3.4</a> compares granularities.</span><sup data-k=\"krajewski25-t6\"><a href=\"#ref-2506.02890\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">29</a></sup>. Megatron renormalizes by default (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/moe_utils.py#L913-L942\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">moe_utils.py#L913-L942</span></code></a>), and <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-router-pre-softmax</span></code> turns renormalization off. Softmax at top-1 requires that flag: with one expert chosen, softmax over its single logit is always 1, so the router gets no gradient (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/transformer_config.py#L3213-L3221\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">transformer_config.py#L3213-L3221</span></code></a>).</p>\n"} />

<Viz name="router-gate-gain" height={822} caption="Figure 3.1.2" />

<Fragment set:html={"\n\n\n\n<p class=\"full\">The simulation sweeps toy tokens from an unsure router to a confident one, with uncorrelated experts of equal RMS: without renormalization the routed output&#x27;s size follows the top-k probability mass, and renormalizing keeps it between 1/\u221ak of one expert&#x27;s RMS and one expert&#x27;s RMS.</p><p class=\"full\">The second setting is the routed scaling factor, the constant multiplied onto the result. It is needed because, even renormalized, the size of the routed output depends on how much each expert counts. With all the weight on one expert, the output has that expert&#x27;s root mean square (RMS). With equal weights on k uncorrelated experts of equal RMS, it drops to 1/\u221ak of that, and a routed scaling factor near \u221ak restores it. DeepSeek-V3 uses <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-2-5\">2.5</a> at k = 8<sup data-k=\"cfg-dsv3\"><a href=\"#ref-src-cfg-dsv3\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">30</a></sup>, and Kimi K2 2.827 \u2248 \u221a8. Shipped models disagree on its value: in the table above only GLM-5.3 and Step-5-Preview stay near \u221ak, and Kimi went from \u221a8 in K2 to 1.0 at k = 16 in K3<sup data-k=\"hub-kimi-k3\"><a href=\"#ref-src-hub-kimi-k3\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">20</a></sup>. Scaling the routed output also changes its weight against the shared expert&#x27;s output (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-4\">3.4</a>).</p><p class=\"full\">The figure&#x27;s last row scales the expert&#x27;s input instead of its output: Llama 4 sends each token to one expert and multiplies the token by that expert&#x27;s sigmoid score before the expert runs<sup data-k=\"tf-llama4\"><a href=\"#ref-src-tf-llama4\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">31</a></sup>. Its SwiGLU experts have no biases, so the output shrinks roughly as the score squared, and the score also softens the SiLU gate.</p>\n<details><summary>Megatron source</summary><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/moe_utils.py#L942-L947\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">moe_utils.py#L942-L947</span></code></a> (renormalize, then scale)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">942</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">probs = scores / (scores.sum(dim=-<span class=\"hl-m\">1</span>, keepdim=<span class=\"hl-k\">True</span>) + <span class=\"hl-m\">1e-20</span>) <span class=\"hl-k\">if</span> topk &gt; <span class=\"hl-m\">1</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">else</span> scores</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">943</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">else</span>:</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">944</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">raise</span> ValueError(<span class=\"hl-s\">f</span><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span><span class=\"hl-s\">Invalid score_function: </span><span class=\"hl-s\">{</span>score_function<span class=\"hl-s\">}</span><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span>)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">945</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">946</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">if</span> scaling_factor:</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">947</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">probs = probs * scaling_factor</span></span></pre></details><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/transformer_config.py#L3213-L3221\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">transformer_config.py#L3213-L3221</span></code></a> (top-1 needs pre-softmax: softmax over one logit gives a zero gradient)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">3213</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">if</span> (</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">3214</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.moe_router_topk == <span class=\"hl-m\">1</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">3215</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">and</span> <span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.moe_router_score_function == <span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span><span class=\"hl-s\">softmax</span><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">3216</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">and</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">not</span> <span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.moe_router_pre_softmax</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">3217</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">and</span> <span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.moe_router_load_balancing_type != <span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span><span class=\"hl-s\">sinkhorn</span><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">3218</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\">):</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">3219</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-c\"># Requires applying softmax before selecting the top-k when k is 1,</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">3220</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-c\"># since softmax on a [num_tokens, 1] would yield a zero gradient.</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">3221</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">raise</span> ValueError(<span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span><span class=\"hl-s\">Please use --moe-router-pre-softmax when topk is 1.</span><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span>)</span></span></pre></details></details>\n<p class=\"full watch\">Without renormalization, the size of the routed output follows the top-k probability mass from token to token, the setting that gave the higher loss above, so the number to watch is how large the routed output is for each token, the root of the sum of its squared weights. Compute it offline from the routing trace (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>).</p><p class=\"full rec\">Keep renormalization on at top-k &gt; 1, and use <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-router-pre-softmax</span></code> for softmax at top-1, or the router gets no gradient. The routed scaling factor has no settled value: \u221ak restores the routed output&#x27;s RMS only for equal weights on uncorrelated experts, and shipped models disagree on its value.</p>\n<p class=\"full\">Both the weights and the output&#x27;s size depend on how peaked the logits are. A router can get too sharp, with one expert taking all the weight, or too flat, averaging its top-k. What sets how peaked it is?</p>\n<h4 id=\"s-3-1-sharpness\">Sharpness</h4>\n<p class=\"full\">With softmax, a linear router gets sharper as the token&#x27;s norm or the router&#x27;s weights grow: the logits spread apart, and the top expert takes more of the weight. The figure below compares three ways to control that against the linear router. Logit soft-capping compresses mostly the logits near its cap. Normalizing the router input removes the part that comes from the token&#x27;s norm. Gating logit normalization standardizes the logits across experts<sup data-k=\"skywork-eq6\"><a href=\"#ref-2406.06563\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">32</a></sup>, which sets their spread to a chosen scale.</p>\n"} />

<Viz name="router-temperature" height={856} caption="Figure 3.1.3" />

<Fragment set:html={"\n\n\n<p class=\"full\">The plain linear router in the figure sharpens as the token&#x27;s norm or the router matrix grows, and soft-capping, which Grok 2 applies at 30 without renormalizing<sup data-k=\"grok2-softcap\"><a href=\"#ref-src-grok2-softcap\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">33</a></sup>, pulls the large-norm token&#x27;s top weight back only once its logits near the cap. Normalizing the input, as Gemma 4 does<sup data-k=\"tf-gemma4\"><a href=\"#ref-src-tf-gemma4\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">17</a></sup>, routes both tokens alike but still sharpens as the matrix grows, and gating logit normalization is unaffected by both. Skywork-MoE used gating logit normalization against a gate so flat that the layer averages its top-k experts<sup data-k=\"skywork-f2\"><a href=\"#ref-2406.06563\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">34</a></sup>. With it, training loss came out moderately lower<sup data-k=\"skywork-f3\"><a href=\"#ref-2406.06563\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">35</a></sup>. Normalizing the router input was ablated only together with the expert embeddings<sup data-k=\"xmoe-t3\"><a href=\"#ref-2204.09179\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">36</a></sup>.</p>\n<details><summary>Megatron source</summary><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/router.py#L1150-L1152\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">router.py#L1150-L1152</span></code></a> (jitter, then plain linear gate)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1150</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-c\"># Apply input jitter</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1151</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-b\">input</span> = <span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.apply_input_jitter(<span class=\"hl-b\">input</span>)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1152</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\">logits = <span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.gating(<span class=\"hl-b\">input</span>)</span></span></pre></details><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/router.py#L824-L831\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">router.py#L824-L831</span></code></a> (multiplicative input jitter)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">824</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">if</span> <span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.config.moe_input_jitter_eps <span class=\"hl-k\">is</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">not</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">None</span>:</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">825</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">eps = <span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.config.moe_input_jitter_eps</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">826</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">if</span> <span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.input_jitter <span class=\"hl-k\">is</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">None</span>:</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">827</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\"><span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.input_jitter = torch.distributions.uniform.Uniform(</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">828</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:12ch\">torch.tensor(<span class=\"hl-m\">1.0</span> - eps, dtype=<span class=\"hl-b\">input</span>.dtype, device=<span class=\"hl-b\">input</span>.device),</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">829</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:12ch\">torch.tensor(<span class=\"hl-m\">1.0</span> + eps, dtype=<span class=\"hl-b\">input</span>.dtype, device=<span class=\"hl-b\">input</span>.device),</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">830</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\">).rsample</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">831</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">return</span> <span class=\"hl-b\">input</span> * <span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.input_jitter(<span class=\"hl-b\">input</span>.shape)</span></span></pre></details></details>\n<p class=\"full watch\">A flat gate, one that averages its top-k experts and makes the model learn worse, shows up as Max1/Max2, the top expert&#x27;s weight over the second&#x27;s, falling toward 1, as it did in Skywork-MoE without normalization<sup data-k=\"skywork-f3\"><a href=\"#ref-2406.06563\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">35</a></sup>. Follow it per layer together with the top expert&#x27;s weight, both from the routing trace (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>).</p><p class=\"full rec\">If the gate goes flat, gating logit normalization fixed it in Skywork-MoE. On the router input, Megatron offers Switch-style multiplicative input jitter, <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-input-jitter-eps</span></code>.</p>\n<p class=\"full\">Sharpness lives in the gaps between logits, and softmax ignores a shift shared by all of them. bf16 does not: if every logit drifts upward together, the values bf16 can represent get farther apart, and close experts can tie. Do those ties change which experts win?</p>\n<h4 id=\"s-3-1-precision\">Precision</h4>\n<p class=\"full\">Top-k orders tied experts in a way PyTorch does not specify<sup data-k=\"torch-topk\"><a href=\"#ref-src-torch-topk\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">37</a></sup>. The figure below simulates routing the same tokens with 64 experts and top-8 through a bf16 router, with and without z-loss, a penalty on the logits&#x27; logsumexp. Its simulated logits drift up by one shared offset, and it counts the tokens whose experts differ from what an fp32 router picks.</p>\n"} />

<Viz name="router-precision" height={907} caption="Figure 3.1.4" />

<Fragment set:html={"\n\n\n<p class=\"full\">As the simulated offset rises, the bf16 router&#x27;s logits land on a coarser grid and more tokens get different experts than under fp32. Z-loss lowers the logsumexp by pushing the logits negative. At top-8 of 64 the 8th logit sits about <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-5\">3.5</a> below the logsumexp, so in the simulation it ends near \u2212<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-2-2\">2.2</a> with z-loss and +<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-6\">3.6</a> without, two sizes bf16 rounds in steps of the same length, and about as many tokens change experts; only the fp32 router has no flips, since fp32 rounds 65,536 times more finely than bf16<sup data-k=\"stmoe-s34\"><a href=\"#ref-2202.08906\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">38</a></sup>. DeepSeek-V3.2, GLM-4.5 and Kimi K2.5 compute the router in fp32 in their inference code<sup data-k=\"fp32-router-code\"><a href=\"#ref-src-fp32-router-code\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">39</a></sup>. The case for fp32 rests on the training evidence, which is about stability. In Switch Transformers, a bf16 router diverged while an fp32 router trained at bf16 speed<sup data-k=\"switch-t2\"><a href=\"#ref-2101.03961\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">40</a></sup>. In ST-MoE, the router z-loss kept every run stable<sup data-k=\"stmoe-t4\"><a href=\"#ref-2202.08906\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">41</a></sup>. Labs rarely say what precision they train the router in: in the table above, 2 of the 10 reports mention it, and 5 of the 10 run the router&#x27;s matrix multiply in bf16 in their inference code. How far below bf16 the rest of the layer can go, while the router keeps its precision, is the question <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-7-precision\">3.7 Precision</a> returns to.</p>\n<details><summary>Megatron source</summary><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/router.py#L124-L130\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">router.py#L124-L130</span></code></a> (router dtype)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">124</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-c\"># Convert to specified datatype for routing computation if enabled</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">125</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\">router_dtype = <span class=\"hl-b\">input</span>.dtype</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">126</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">if</span> <span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.config.moe_router_dtype == <span class=\"hl-s\">&#x27;</span><span class=\"hl-s\">fp32</span><span class=\"hl-s\">&#x27;</span>:</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">127</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">router_dtype = torch.float32</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">128</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">elif</span> <span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.config.moe_router_dtype == <span class=\"hl-s\">&#x27;</span><span class=\"hl-s\">fp64</span><span class=\"hl-s\">&#x27;</span>:</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">129</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">router_dtype = torch.float64</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">130</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\">logits = router_gating_linear(<span class=\"hl-b\">input</span>, <span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.weight, <span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.bias, router_dtype)</span></span></pre></details><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/moe_utils.py#L1424-L1435\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">moe_utils.py#L1424-L1435</span></code></a> (bf16 operands, fp32 logits)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1424</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">if</span> te_general_gemm <span class=\"hl-k\">is</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">not</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">None</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">and</span> router_dtype != torch.float64:</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1425</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-c\"># cuBLASLt&#x27;s non-FP8 bias epilogue expects bias and output to have the same</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1426</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-c\"># dtype. Router parameters may be BF16 while router logits are FP32, so cast the</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1427</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-c\"># small bias vector before passing it to TE.</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1428</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">gemm_bias = bias.to(router_dtype) <span class=\"hl-k\">if</span> bias <span class=\"hl-k\">is</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">not</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">None</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">else</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">None</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1429</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">output = te_general_gemm(weight, inp, router_dtype, layout=<span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span><span class=\"hl-s\">TN</span><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span>, bias=gemm_bias)[<span class=\"hl-m\">0</span>]</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1430</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">elif</span> bias <span class=\"hl-k\">is</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">None</span>:</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1431</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">output = torch.mm(inp.to(router_dtype), weight.to(router_dtype).t())</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1432</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">else</span>:</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1433</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">output = torch.addmm(</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1434</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\">bias.to(router_dtype), inp.to(router_dtype), weight.to(router_dtype).t()</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1435</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">)</span></span></pre></details><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/moe_utils.py#L171-L184\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">moe_utils.py#L171-L184</span></code></a> (z-loss)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">171</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\">logsum = torch.logsumexp(logits, dim=-<span class=\"hl-m\">1</span>)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">172</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\">z_loss_values = torch.square(logsum)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">173</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">174</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">if</span> padding_mask <span class=\"hl-k\">is</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">not</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">None</span>:</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">175</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-c\"># Invert padding_mask: True (padding) -&gt; 0, False (valid) -&gt; 1</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">176</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">valid_mask = ~padding_mask</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">177</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-c\"># Only compute z_loss for valid (non-padding) tokens</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">178</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">z_loss_values = z_loss_values * valid_mask</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">179</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-c\"># Compute mean over valid tokens only</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">180</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">num_valid_tokens = valid_mask.sum()</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">181</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">z_loss = z_loss_values.sum() / torch.clamp(num_valid_tokens, <span class=\"hl-b\">min</span>=<span class=\"hl-m\">1.0</span>) * z_loss_coeff</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">182</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">else</span>:</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">183</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">z_loss = torch.mean(z_loss_values) * z_loss_coeff</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">184</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">return</span> z_loss</span></span></pre></details><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/router.py#L761-L764\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">router.py#L761-L764</span></code></a> (z-loss coefficient over TP\u00d7CP)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">761</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">if</span> <span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.config.moe_z_loss_coeff <span class=\"hl-k\">is</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">not</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">None</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">and</span> <span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.training <span class=\"hl-k\">and</span> torch.is_grad_enabled():</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">762</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-c\"># Skip Z loss calculations when using torch.no_grad() or checkpointing.</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">763</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">moe_z_loss_coeff = <span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.config.moe_z_loss_coeff / <span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.tp_cp_group.size()</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">764</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">z_loss = z_loss_func(logits, moe_z_loss_coeff, padding_mask=padding_mask)</span></span></pre></details></details>\n<p class=\"full watch\">A bf16 router can make training diverge, as it did in Switch Transformers, so two signals are worth following: the z-loss, which tracks how far the logits drift up, and the flip rate, the share of tokens whose experts change when the router is recomputed in fp32. Megatron logs the z-loss when <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-z-loss-coeff</span></code> is set, already divided by the coefficient (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/router.py#L805-L807\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">router.py#L805-L807</span></code></a>), so its square root is the RMS logsumexp: our run logged 20.9, an RMS logsumexp of about 4.6. The flip rate needs a matched capture of the same inputs and current router state; <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a> provides a bounded live hook, since the trace&#x27;s initial weight snapshot does not track later training updates.</p><p class=\"full rec\">Always set <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-router-dtype</span> fp32</code>. Add <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-z-loss-coeff</span> <span class=\"nw\">1e-3</span></code>, ST-MoE&#x27;s value, to a softmax router whose logits drift up. DeepSeek-V3&#x27;s report never mentions z-loss for its sigmoid router<sup data-k=\"dsv3-noz\"><a href=\"#ref-2412.19437\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">42</a></sup>.</p>\n<h4>What to watch</h4>\n<table class=\"glance\"><thead><tr><th>Part</th><th>Symptom</th><th>Signal to watch</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>Gating function</td><td></td><td>The top expert&#x27;s weight and the routing entropy, per layer, from the routing trace with logits on (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>).</td></tr><tr><td>Output scale</td><td><code>learns badly</code>: without renormalization, the routed output scales with the top-k probability mass. Renormalizing lowered the loss<sup data-k=\"krajewski25-t6\"><a href=\"#ref-2506.02890\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">29</a></sup>.</td><td>How large each token&#x27;s routed output is, \u221a(\u03a3 w\u00b2), from the routing trace (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>).</td></tr><tr><td>Sharpness</td><td><code>learns badly</code>: when the gate is flat, the layer averages its top-k experts<sup data-k=\"skywork-f3\"><a href=\"#ref-2406.06563\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">35</a></sup>.</td><td>The top expert&#x27;s weight and Max1/Max2, its weight over the second expert&#x27;s<sup data-k=\"skywork-f3\"><a href=\"#ref-2406.06563\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">35</a></sup>, per layer, from the routing trace (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>).</td></tr><tr><td>Precision</td><td><code>blows up</code>: a bf16 router diverged in Switch Transformers<sup data-k=\"switch-t2\"><a href=\"#ref-2101.03961\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">40</a></sup>.</td><td>The logged z-loss is divided by its coefficient, so its square root gives RMS logsumexp: 20.9 corresponds to about 4.6. Measure fp32 routing flips with <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>&#x27;s matched live hook using the same inputs and current router state; the trace&#x27;s initial weight snapshot cannot replay later training forwards exactly.</td></tr></tbody></table>\n<p class=\"full\">So far we have shaped how a token&#x27;s logits become weights. The gating function sets how much each chosen expert counts, renormalization and the scaling factor set how big their combined output is, and sharpness and precision set how peaked and how exact the scores are. All of it assumed one setup: a learned router, and the token picking its top k. Both can change: experts can pick their tokens instead, and a fixed table can replace the learned choice. Who should make the choice, the token or the expert, and does the choice need to be learned at all?</p>\n<h3 id=\"s-3-2\">3.2 Routing strategy: who selects, and how</h3>\n"} />

<Viz name="hero-anatomy-zoom" query="&stage=3&mini=1" w={600} h={347} />

<Fragment set:html={"\n<p class=\"full\">Token-choice top-k is the default: each token scores every expert and keeps its k best. This section looks at two ways to change that. The expert can pick its tokens instead of the token picking its experts, or a fixed hash can pick, with no learned router at all.</p>\n<p class=\"meta\">How current open MoEs pick their experts, and which ones look up a table by token id, with Zhou et al.&#x27;s research model as an expert-choice comparison. Current-model settings come from their <code>config.json</code> and modeling code; the expert-choice row comes from the paper<sup data-k=\"zhou22-ec\"><a href=\"#ref-2202.09368\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">43</a></sup>.</p><table class=\"glance\"><thead><tr><th>Model</th><th>Who selects</th><th>Token-id lookup</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>DeepSeek-V4-Pro</td><td>token choice</td><td>hash routing in the first 3 MoE layers<sup data-k=\"hub-dsv4pro-route\"><a href=\"#ref-src-hub-dsv4pro-route\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">44</a></sup></td></tr><tr><td>DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash</td><td>token choice</td><td>Engram n-gram memory (2- to 4-grams) at layers 1 and 14; no hash routing<sup data-k=\"hub-v41-engram\"><a href=\"#ref-src-hub-v41-engram\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">45</a></sup></td></tr><tr><td>EC-CF2 8B/64E (Zhou et al.)<sup data-k=\"zhou22-ec\"><a href=\"#ref-2202.09368\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">43</a></sup></td><td>expert choice</td><td>none</td></tr><tr><td>GLM-5.3</td><td>token choice</td><td>none</td></tr><tr><td>Kimi K3</td><td>token choice</td><td>none</td></tr><tr><td>Llama 4 Maverick</td><td>token choice</td><td>none<sup data-k=\"hub-llama4-route\"><a href=\"#ref-src-hub-llama4-route\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">46</a></sup></td></tr><tr><td>MiMo-V2.6-Pro-RL</td><td>token choice</td><td>none</td></tr><tr><td>Qwen3.8-Flash-Next</td><td>token choice</td><td>n-gram embedding (2- and 3-grams) at layer 1<sup data-k=\"hub-q38-ngram\"><a href=\"#ref-src-hub-q38-ngram\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">47</a></sup></td></tr><tr><td>Step-5-Preview</td><td>token choice</td><td>none<sup data-k=\"hub-step5-route\"><a href=\"#ref-src-hub-step5-route\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">48</a></sup></td></tr></tbody></table>\n<p class=\"full\">Before asking whether the choice needs a learned router, we have to settle who makes it, because with token choice many tokens can want the same experts and loads skew. Should each token keep picking its experts, or should each expert pick its tokens instead?</p>\n<h4 id=\"s-3-2-who-selects\">Who selects</h4>\n<p class=\"full\">With token choice, many tokens can want the same experts, so loads skew. Expert choice flips the roles: each expert takes its best tokens, so every expert gets the same load<sup data-k=\"zhou22-ec\"><a href=\"#ref-2202.09368\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">43</a></sup>. The catch is future token leakage<sup data-k=\"lossfree-s52\"><a href=\"#ref-2408.15664\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">49</a></sup>: whether a token gets an expert depends on the tokens after it. At inference those tokens do not exist yet, so routing differs from training<sup data-k=\"et-ec-causal\"><a href=\"#ref-2603.11535\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">50</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">Expert threshold, the third rule in the figure below, keeps loads near even without looking at future tokens<sup data-k=\"et-ec-causal\"><a href=\"#ref-2603.11535\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">50</a></sup>. In exchange, each token gets a variable number of experts. In a 575M-parameter model it reached a lower loss than token choice, with or without balancing (2.844 against about 2.89)<sup data-k=\"et-t1\"><a href=\"#ref-2603.11535\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">51</a></sup>.</p>\n"} />

<Viz name="who-picks" height={847} caption="Figure 3.2.1" />

<Fragment set:html={"\n\n\n<p class=\"full\">In the figure, the sequence leans toward expert 1, and token choice, drawn with no balancer, overloads it. Expert choice balances the loads, but later tokens push out earlier selections, while expert threshold, whose cutoffs come from earlier batches that lean the same way, keeps the loads near the target without revising any selection, at the price of a varying number of experts per token.</p>\n<details><summary>Megatron source</summary><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/moe_utils.py#L891-L897\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">moe_utils.py#L891-L897</span></code></a> (per-token top-k, token choice only)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">891</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-c\"># Sorting top-k turned off during inference</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">892</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">return</span> torch.topk(</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">893</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">scores,</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">894</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">k=topk,</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">895</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">dim=<span class=\"hl-m\">1</span>,</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">896</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-b\">sorted</span>=torch.is_grad_enabled() <span class=\"hl-k\">or</span> is_batch_invariant_mode_enabled(),</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">897</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\">)</span></span></pre></details></details>\n<p class=\"full watch\">The skew token choice leaves behind makes the step slow. It shows in the token counts, tokens per expert per layer, which <code><span class=\"nw\">--save-tokens-per-expert-interval</span></code> logs (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>) and which read best as MaxVio, how far the busiest expert&#x27;s load sits above the mean<span class=\"sn\">MaxVio, short for maximal violation: (busiest expert&#x27;s load minus the mean load) divided by the mean load. 0 is perfectly even; 1 means the busiest expert gets twice the average. Wang et al., who proposed the expert bias of <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-3\">3.3</a>, define it.</span><sup data-k=\"lossfree-maxvio\"><a href=\"#ref-2408.15664\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">52</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full rec\">Use token choice, the routing <a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\">Megatron</a> trains, with <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-router-topk</span></code> setting k.</p>\n<p class=\"full\">Whether the token or the expert does the picking, the choice so far always comes from a learned router, so the routing itself has to be trained. Does routing really need to be learned, or could a simpler rule pick the experts?</p>\n<h4 id=\"s-3-2-hash-layers\">Hash layers</h4>\n<p class=\"full\">In DeepSeek-V4&#x27;s first 3 MoE layers, a fixed table maps each token id to its experts<sup data-k=\"dsv4-s41\"><a href=\"#ref-2606.19348\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">53</a></sup>, and the router is kept only for the weights<sup data-k=\"dsv4-gate-code\"><a href=\"#ref-src-dsv4-gate-code\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">54</a></sup>. The table is a fixed hash of the token id<sup data-k=\"dsv4-s21\"><a href=\"#ref-2606.19348\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">27</a></sup>, and in our probe it spreads frequent tokens no better than a random table. There is nothing to learn for the selection, and hash layers matched Switch and BASE layers<span class=\"sn\">Lewis et al.&#x27;s balanced assignment of experts, which routes by solving a linear assignment that gives every expert an equal number of tokens.</span> in models up to 4.5B<sup data-k=\"roller-s52\"><a href=\"#ref-2106.04426\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">55</a></sup>. The figure below puts learned and hash routing side by side, above a third lookup by token id: Engram, which hashes each token&#x27;s last 2 to 4 tokens into tables of embeddings and adds the rows it reads to the hidden state<sup data-k=\"engram-s23\"><a href=\"#ref-src-engram-s23\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">56</a></sup>. DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash keeps Engram at two layers, but picks every layer&#x27;s experts with its learned router<sup class=\"fn-multi\"><a data-ks=\"dsv41-s242 dsv41-engram\" href=\"#ref-2609.19969\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">57,58</a></sup>.</p>\n"} />

<Viz name="router-authority" height={852} caption="Figure 3.2.2" />

<Fragment set:html={"\n\n\n<p class=\"full\">The figure&#x27;s two routers treat the word &quot;the&quot; differently: the learned router selects each one by its context, while the hash layer sends every &quot;the&quot; to the six experts its table row names, even past an expert with a higher score. Both rows weight their six experts to sum to V4-Flash&#x27;s routed scaling factor of 1.5<sup data-k=\"cfg-v4flash-rs\"><a href=\"#ref-src-cfg-v4flash-rs\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">59</a></sup>, and the figure measures how alike the four &quot;the&quot;s&#x27; expert sets are by their mean Jaccard similarity. What the hash layer gives up is context: the same token id always selects the same experts, whatever surrounds it. It also scales worse than learned routing. Clark et al. varied how many of a model&#x27;s layers are MoE layers. With a single MoE layer, hash routing reached the lowest loss of the routing methods they compared, but as more layers became MoE layers it stopped improving, while their learned router kept getting better<sup data-k=\"clark-appd\"><a href=\"#ref-2202.01169\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">60</a></sup>. And no bias can rebalance it<sup data-k=\"dsv4-gate-code\"><a href=\"#ref-src-dsv4-gate-code\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">54</a></sup>: frequent tokens can overload their assigned experts, and nothing moves them. V4.1-Flash dropped hash layers and kept only Engram, which picks no experts<sup data-k=\"hub-v41-engram\"><a href=\"#ref-src-hub-v41-engram\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">45</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">In Megatron, <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-num-hash-layers</span></code> makes the first MoE layers hash layers (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/router.py#L304-L308\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">router.py#L304-L308</span></code></a>). They read each token&#x27;s experts from a token-to-expert table (<code>tid2eid</code>) with one row per vocabulary entry, and if you do not load one, Megatron fills it with a round-robin placeholder (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/router.py#L313-L329\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">router.py#L313-L329</span></code></a>). Since the expert bias is off in these layers, nothing corrects a bad table during training, so the table matters. Roller et al. compared ways to build it: a random table and one that greedily spreads the most frequent tokens over the emptiest experts reached about the same perplexity, with the second better balanced, while grouping similar tokens by clustering their embeddings did clearly worse<sup data-k=\"roller-hashfn\"><a href=\"#ref-2106.04426\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">61</a></sup>.</p>\n<details><summary>Megatron source</summary><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/router.py#L304-L308\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">router.py#L304-L308</span></code></a> (first N layers hashed)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">304</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"nw\">router.py#L914-L916</span></code></a> (weights still from the gate)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">914</span><span class=\"code\" group_topk</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">916</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">return</span> scores.gather(<span class=\"hl-m\">1</span>, default_top_indices), default_top_indices</span></span></pre></details><p>plus a few lines that load V4-Flash&#x27;s <code>layers.0.ffn.gate.tid2eid</code> from the Hub and count tokens per expert</p></details>\n<p class=\"full watch\">A table that overloads some experts makes the step slow, and since nothing rebalances it during training, check it early in the run by comparing the tokens per expert in the hash layers with those in the learned layers, from the token counts that <code><span class=\"nw\">--save-tokens-per-expert-interval</span></code> writes (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>).</p><p class=\"full rec\">Use it only to reproduce DeepSeek-V4&#x27;s first layers. Load a fixed hash table as V4 does<sup data-k=\"dsv4-s21\"><a href=\"#ref-2606.19348\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">27</a></sup>, or a frequency-balanced one, instead of Megatron&#x27;s round-robin placeholder.</p>\n<h4>What to watch</h4>\n<table class=\"glance\"><thead><tr><th>Part</th><th>Symptom</th><th>Signal to watch</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>Who selects</td><td><code>slow</code>: with token choice, some experts get more tokens than others. <code>learns badly</code>: with expert choice, routing at inference differs from routing in training.</td><td>MaxVio per layer<sup data-k=\"lossfree-maxvio\"><a href=\"#ref-2408.15664\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">52</a></sup>, how far the busiest expert&#x27;s load sits above the mean, from the token counts (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>).</td></tr><tr><td>Hash layers</td><td><code>slow</code>: frequent tokens can overload the experts their hash assigns them, and no bias can move them.</td><td>Tokens per expert in the hash layers, compared with the learned layers, from the token counts (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>).</td></tr></tbody></table>\n<p class=\"full\">Expert choice and expert threshold keep the load even or close to it, and no bias can move a hash table&#x27;s load. Megatron trains learned token choice, where nothing so far stops many tokens from crowding onto the same few experts. Crowded experts are the first real failure mode of an MoE, so how do we keep tokens spread evenly over the experts while the router keeps learning?</p>\n<h3 id=\"s-3-3\">3.3 Load balancing</h3>\n"} />

<Viz name="hero-anatomy-zoom" query="&stage=4&mini=1" w={600} h={347} />

<Fragment set:html={"\n\n<p class=\"full\">When many tokens pick the same experts, two things go wrong. The GPU holding the busiest experts has the most work, so the other GPUs wait for it and it sets the step time, and if the router keeps sending tokens to the same few experts, the others get few tokens and their parameters go mostly unused. The first cost is the easier one to measure: with routing forced even, the same model ran 1.5x faster at init.</p><p class=\"full\">Forcing the routing even only shows the speed to aim for, since it throws away what the router learned. To get close to it while the router keeps learning, there are two families of fixes, an auxiliary loss and a loss-free bias, and they balance the load in different ways. The aux loss adds a term that rewards an even load, so the router&#x27;s gradient pushes its probabilities toward spreading tokens, at the price of a gradient that competes with the LM loss. The bias changes only which experts are selected. Whichever you pick, the scope it balances over (a sequence, a micro-batch or the global batch) decides what &quot;balanced&quot; means.</p>\n<p class=\"meta\">Balancing recipes reported by model authors. Auxiliary losses and selection biases can be used together. Scope describes the tokens whose loads are balanced; the MiMo row concerns RL rather than pretraining.</p><table class=\"glance\"><thead><tr><th>Model</th><th>Auxiliary loss</th><th>Scope</th><th>Selection bias</th><th>Quantile balancing</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>DeepSeek-V3<sup data-k=\"dsv3-alpha\"><a href=\"#ref-2412.19437\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">62</a></sup></td><td>Small sequence-wise loss, coefficient 0.0001</td><td>Batch-wide bias plus sequence-wise loss</td><td>Fixed-step expert bias</td><td>No, fixed-step updates</td></tr><tr><td>DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash<sup data-k=\"balance-dsv41\"><a href=\"#ref-2609.19969\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">63</a></sup></td><td>Small sequence-wise loss, coefficient 0.0001</td><td>Text and image loads balanced separately, plus sequence-wise loss</td><td>Separate biases per modality, update speed 0.001</td><td>Uses the auxiliary-loss-free bias strategy</td></tr><tr><td>Kimi K3<sup data-k=\"balance-k3\"><a href=\"#ref-2607.24653\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">64</a></sup></td><td>Described as auxiliary-loss-free</td><td>Global batch</td><td>Bias derived from score margins, applied next step</td><td>Yes, global-batch histogram estimation</td></tr><tr><td>LongCat-Flash<sup data-k=\"balance-longcat\"><a href=\"#ref-2509.01322\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">65</a></sup></td><td>Device-level balance loss</td><td>Global-batch FFN targets plus device-level balance within sequences</td><td>PID-controlled expert bias; identity experts excluded from bias updates</td><td>No, PID updates</td></tr><tr><td>MiMo-V2.6-Pro-RL<sup data-k=\"balance-mimo26\"><a href=\"#ref-2610.11959\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">66</a></sup></td><td>Router frozen during RL</td><td>Preserves the pre-RL router</td><td>Report evaluates freezing rather than a new bias update rule</td><td>No new balancing update reported for RL</td></tr><tr><td>Qwen3 MoE<sup data-k=\"balance-qwen3\"><a href=\"#ref-2505.09388\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">67</a></sup></td><td>Global-batch balancing loss</td><td>Global batch</td><td>No selection bias in the published router<sup data-k=\"hf-qwen3moe\"><a href=\"#ref-src-hf-qwen3moe\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">68</a></sup></td><td>No, auxiliary-loss approach</td></tr></tbody></table>\n<p class=\"full\">Before comparing balancers, we need to know how much speed an uneven load costs us, since every balancer below should be judged against the throughput of routing that is forced to be even. Where does that 1.5x come from, and how do we measure it on our own setup?</p>\n<h4 id=\"s-3-3-cost-of-imbalance\">Cost of imbalance</h4>\n<p class=\"full\">To see what imbalance costs, we routed the same model three ways. Forced-even routing, <a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\">Megatron</a>&#x27;s <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-router-force-load-balancing</span></code>, replaces the router&#x27;s logits with random ones so that every expert gets about the same load, which makes it the best case, at 149.4 TFLOP/s.</p><p class=\"full\">Forced-biased routing, <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-router-force-biased</span></code>, adds a shared random bias of growing size to those random logits, so a few experts get more and more of the tokens: a dial for how uneven the load is. At the far end of the dial throughput falls to 100.5 TFLOP/s, and a replay of that routing, whose aux loss matches the logged one, has the busiest expert receive every token, a MaxVio of 7, as uneven as top-8 routing over 64 experts can get. It can get worse: in the replay the busiest experts are spread over several GPUs, so the busiest GPU holds only about twice the mean load, and had they all sat on one GPU, that GPU would have done most of the expert work.</p><p class=\"full\">The learned router, the one you would train with, starts far from that best case: a freshly initialised router ran its first 40 iterations as slowly as the worst forced case. The figure below compares the three. Training has to close that gap without forcing the router&#x27;s choices, and the rest of this section compares the balancing methods by how close they bring a learned router to forced-even throughput.</p>\n"} />

<Viz name="forced-balance-ab" height={439} caption="Figure 3.3.1" />

<Fragment set:html={"\n\n\n\n<p class=\"full\">In the figure, throughput falls with each larger forced bias, as a few experts take more of the tokens, and the learned router starts at the level of the most uneven forced run. The busiest experts set the step because the all-to-all hands each GPU every token routed to its local experts, 8 of 64 in our runs, and the next all-to-all waits for the slowest GPU. A GPU&#x27;s load is the sum over its local experts, so it can be more balanced than the per-expert load. That is why a balance loss can also work per GPU rather than per expert: DeepSeekMoE&#x27;s device-level balance loss pools each GPU&#x27;s experts into one term<sup data-k=\"dsmoe-device\"><a href=\"#ref-2401.06066\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">69</a></sup>, and the figure that follows compares it with the expert-level loss.</p>"} />

<Viz name="device-level-balance-loss" height={853} caption="Figure 3.3.2" />

<Fragment set:html={"<p class=\"full\">Trained on one toy batch with two experts per GPU, one of them among the four the LM favours, both losses balance the GPUs, and the device-level loss pulls the router less far from what the LM wants, since it sees only each GPU&#x27;s total, which that placement keeps closer to even. The catch is that the experts within each GPU stay uneven, so one expert can still take most of its GPU&#x27;s tokens. DeepSeekMoE gives the same reason for weighting the two losses unevenly: strict expert-level balance would cost model quality, so it sets a larger device-level factor and keeps a small expert-level one against routing collapse<sup data-k=\"dsmoe-device\"><a href=\"#ref-2401.06066\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">69</a></sup>. DeepSeek-V2 runs them at 0.05 against 0.003<sup data-k=\"dsv2-coeffs\"><a href=\"#ref-2405.04434\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">70</a></sup>. DeepSeek-V3&#x27;s report no longer mentions a device-level loss: it balances each expert with a bias and a small sequence-wise loss, both covered later in this section<sup data-k=\"dsv3-nodevice\"><a href=\"#ref-2412.19437\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">42</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">The same imbalance costs time in serving too, so a frontier MoE in production keeps it small: when DeepSeek serves DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash, its slowest GPU sits at most 1.09 times the mean<sup data-k=\"deepinfer-eplb\"><a href=\"#ref-doc-1\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">71</a></sup>.</p>\n<details><summary>Megatron source</summary><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/router.py#L1185-L1193\" class=\"hl-k\">return</span> logits + bias</span></span></pre></details></details>\n<p class=\"full watch\">Imbalance shows up as a slow step, so the number to watch is TFLOP/s against the forced-even control run. To see where the lost time goes, look at the step profiled in <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-7\">3.7</a>: it ran on an unbalanced router, and <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-7\">3.7</a> shows how to tell whether imbalance is what slows its all-to-all.</p><p class=\"full rec\">Use it once per setup, as a control run: <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-router-force-load-balancing</span></code> gives the balanced throughput to compare every balancer against. Never train with it.</p>\n<p class=\"full\">Forced routing shows how fast an even load runs, but it replaces the router&#x27;s logits with random ones, so it can only serve as a control run, never as a way to train. How do we steer a learned router toward an even load while it trains, starting with the oldest fix, a loss term?</p>\n<h4 id=\"s-3-3-aux-loss\">Aux loss</h4>\n<p class=\"full\">The oldest fix adds a loss term, the aux loss: the coefficient times the expert count times a sum over experts, each expert&#x27;s share of the micro-batch&#x27;s tokens times its mean router probability (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/moe_utils.py#L148-L152\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">moe_utils.py#L148-L152</span></code></a>). Its gradient pulls router probabilities away from what the LM wants, toward an even load. Below, four settings route one batch: no balancing, the aux loss at a small and at a large coefficient, and the loss-free bias.</p>\n"} />

<Viz name="aux-load-balancing-loss" height={957} caption="Figure 3.3.3" />

<Fragment set:html={"\n\n\n\n<p class=\"full\">Both balancers spread the load in the figure, in different ways: the aux loss pulls the router&#x27;s probabilities off what the LM loss wants, further at the larger coefficient, while the loss-free bias only changes which experts are picked and balances best, though it reroutes the most selections. The aux loss&#x27;s pull makes its coefficient a tradeoff: too large a coefficient costs LM loss, and too small a coefficient leaves the load uneven. Yuan3.0&#x27;s ablation of DeepSeek-V3&#x27;s sequence-wise aux loss on a 10B model, plotted in the next figure, shows both sides: test loss rose with the coefficient from 1e-3 to 0.1, while the smallest coefficient, 1e-4, did slightly better than no aux loss at all but still left the busiest expert in a layer with about 13.6 times the tokens of the least used one<sup class=\"fn-multi\"><a data-ks=\"yuan3-t1 yuan3-l144\" href=\"#ref-2601.14327\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">72,73</a></sup>.</p>"} />

<Viz name="aux-coeff-sweep" height={533} caption="Figure 3.3.4" />

<Fragment set:html={"<p class=\"full\">The smallest coefficient in the figure leaves the load uneven, and the one that balances it, 1e-2, raises the test loss. Megatron sets the coefficient with <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-aux-loss-coeff</span></code> next to <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-router-load-balancing-type</span> aux_loss</code>, one coefficient per loss type, applied in every layer. Motif 3 found its first MoE layers more susceptible to imbalance and gives them stronger auxiliary loss weights<sup data-k=\"motif3-aux\"><a href=\"#ref-2608.09119\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">74</a></sup>. We come back to the first layers later in the chapter.</p>\n<details><summary>Megatron source</summary><details class=\"src\"><summary><a
