class=\"full\">The measured row under the four-column figure above comes from Wang et al., who proposed the loss-free bias and compared it with a per-sequence aux loss on a 1B MoE with 64 routed experts and top-6. At their baseline coefficient of 1e-3, MaxVio over the validation set was 0.72 at a perplexity of 9.56, against 0.04 and 9.50 with the bias<sup data-k=\"lossfree-t2\"><a href=\"#ref-2408.15664\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">75</a></sup>; with no aux loss MaxVio rose to about 5.1, and at 1e-2 it fell to about 0.1 while perplexity rose to about 9.69<sup data-k=\"lossfree-f2\"><a href=\"#ref-2408.15664\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">76</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">This aux loss, at a coefficient of 1e-3, is the learned router of Cost of imbalance, and by the end of its 120 iterations it had climbed to 125 TFLOP/s.</p><p class=\"full watch\">A coefficient set too high makes the model learn worse, and one set too low leaves the load uneven, so watch the LM loss and the balance together. Balance is best read from MaxVio rather than from the aux loss itself. The loss multiplies each expert&#x27;s share of the tokens by its mean router probability, so when the router&#x27;s probabilities are flat the loss reads the same as for an even load, whatever the token shares are, and a perfectly balanced reading can hide a skewed load. Sigma-MoE-Tiny hit this case: in its lower layers the loss went to its minimum by flattening the probabilities while the load stayed uneven<sup data-k=\"sigma-early\"><a href=\"#ref-2512.16248\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">77</a></sup>. <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-per-layer-logging</span></code> logs this value per layer as <code>load_balancing_loss</code>, divided by the coefficient.</p><p class=\"full rec\">Use it when the simplest balancer will do: <code>aux_loss</code> at a coefficient around 1e-3, as in our first MoE run in <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-2-5\">2.5</a>. Raise it if the load stays uneven, lower it if the LM loss suffers<sup data-k=\"yuan3-t1\"><a href=\"#ref-2601.14327\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">72</a></sup>.</p>\n<p class=\"full\">The aux loss we used so far counts each expert&#x27;s load within one micro-batch, which leaves a question we have not asked yet: over which tokens should the load be even? You could balance it over each sequence, over each micro-batch, or over the whole global batch, and the next part compares the three.</p>\n<h4 id=\"s-3-3-scope\">Scope</h4>\n<p class=\"full\">Think about what one micro-batch holds. Pretraining pipelines often pack each micro-batch from a single domain, say all code, so a loss that asks for an even load inside that micro-batch spreads the code tokens over every expert, and no expert gets to specialize in code<sup data-k=\"qiu-l57\"><a href=\"#ref-2501.11873\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">78</a></sup>. Balance over the whole global batch instead, which mixes many domains, and the code tokens may crowd onto a few experts as long as tokens from other domains fill the rest. Qiu et al. saw experts specialize by domain only in that second setting, and it also lowered their perplexity<sup data-k=\"qiu-t1\"><a href=\"#ref-2501.11873\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">79</a></sup>. The GPUs pay for it: inside each micro-batch the load is no longer even, and their 43B model&#x27;s step ran about 6% slower, about 1% of that slowdown from communication overhead and the rest mainly from the uneven load<sup class=\"fn-multi\"><a data-ks=\"qiu-s42 qiu-t5\" href=\"#ref-2501.11873\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">80,81</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">A single sequence is the tightest scope of all. Balancing each sequence on its own spreads every sequence over all the experts, even when a micro-batch mixes domains; DeepSeek-V2-Lite counted its only balance loss, an expert-level one, this way<sup data-k=\"dsv2-seq\"><a href=\"#ref-2405.04434\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">82</a></sup>, and DeepSeek-V3 keeps a small one to stop a single sequence from piling onto a few experts while the batch as a whole looks balanced<sup data-k=\"dsv3-seqwhy\"><a href=\"#ref-2412.19437\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">42</a></sup>. In the figure below the three scopes run side by side, on GPUs that each hold two sequences from two domains: the tighter the scope, the less the experts can concentrate on one domain, and the looser it is, the more uneven the load each GPU carries.</p>\n"} />

<Viz name="global-batch-balance-loss" height={901} caption="Figure 3.3.5" />

<Fragment set:html={"\n\n\n\n<p class=\"full\">In that simulation, the sequence-wise loss spreads the code sequence over all the experts, the micro-batch loss lets each GPU&#x27;s two domains split its experts between them, and the global-batch loss leaves the code tokens mostly on their two favoured experts, at the price of the most uneven load per GPU. Megatron picks the scope through the load-balancing type: <code>aux_loss</code> balances per micro-batch, <code>seq_aux_loss</code> per sequence and <code>global_aux_loss</code> over the global batch. To combine scopes, pass several types and one coefficient for each, in the same order, for example <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-router-load-balancing-type</span> aux_loss seq_aux_loss <span class=\"nw\">--moe-aux-loss-coeff</span> <span class=\"nw\">1e-3</span> <span class=\"nw\">1e-4</span></code>.</p>\n<details><summary>Megatron source</summary><details class=\"src\"><summary><a class=\"hl-s\">&#x27;</span>,</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">237</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\">torch.zeros(</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:12ch\">dtype=torch.float32,</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">240</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:12ch\">device=torch.cuda.current_device(),</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">241</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\">),</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"hl-s\">&#x27;</span>,</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">246</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\">torch.tensor(<span class=\"hl-m\">0</span>, dtype=torch.float32, device=torch.cuda.current_device()),</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">247</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\">persistent=<span class=\"hl-k\">False</span>,</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">248</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">)</span></span></pre></details><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/router.py#L1121-L1125\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">router.py#L1121-L1125</span></code></a> (reset per global batch)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1121</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">def</span> <span class=\"hl-f\">reset_global_aux_loss_tracker</span>(<span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>):</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1122</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;&quot;&quot;Reset the global aux loss tracker.&quot;&quot;&quot;</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1123</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">if</span> <span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.global_tokens_per_expert <span class=\"hl-k\">is</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">not</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">None</span>:</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1124</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\"><span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.global_tokens_per_expert.zero_()</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1125</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\"><span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.ga_steps.zero_()</span></span></pre></details></details>\n<p class=\"full watch\">Each scope fails in its own way. Balancing per sequence or per micro-batch can keep domain experts from forming, so the model learns worse, while balancing over the global batch leaves the load on each GPU uneven, so the step gets slower. Whichever scope you pick, read balance from MaxVio at that scope, computed per micro-batch from the loads <code><span class=\"nw\">--save-tokens-per-expert-interval</span></code> saves, per global batch from the sum of those loads, and per sequence offline from the routing trace (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>).</p><p class=\"full rec\">Use micro-batch (<code>aux_loss</code>) by default. Use <code>global_aux_loss</code> only for domain-specialized experts, at +5.8% step time in Qiu et al.<sup data-k=\"qiu-t5\"><a href=\"#ref-2501.11873\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">81</a></sup>.</p>\n<p class=\"full\">Whichever scope you pick, a balancing loss still steers the router through its gradient, and that gradient competes with the one from the LM loss. Could you balance the load without touching the gradient at all?</p>\n<h4 id=\"s-3-3-loss-free-bias\">Loss-free bias</h4>\n<p class=\"full\">The aux loss fixes an overloaded expert by pulling the router&#x27;s probabilities away from what the LM wants. The loss-free bias leaves the probabilities alone and moves the load with no gradient. Each expert gets a bias, added to its score before the top-k and used only to pick experts, while the weights still come from the scores, and once per step Megatron moves each bias by a fixed amount, the update rate: up for underloaded experts, down for overloaded ones. The next figure runs that loop. In DeepSeek-V3&#x27;s ablation it matched a batch-wise aux loss in validation loss, at 1B and at 3B<sup data-k=\"dsv3-s453\"><a href=\"#ref-2412.19437\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">83</a></sup>. Like the aux loss, it reroutes some selections away from the LM&#x27;s own top-k, and in the four-column toy of the Aux loss part it reroutes the most.</p>"} />

<Viz name="aux-loss-free-bias" height={715} caption="Figure 3.3.6" />

<Fragment set:html={"<p class=\"full\">The figure runs 16 toy experts with a step far larger than Megatron&#x27;s default, so a few rounds stand in for many: overloaded experts lower their bias and underloaded ones raise it until the load is close to even, and the back-and-forth that the large step leaves is damped once the toy cuts its step tenfold. In real training the catch is the fixed step, which must suit the expert count. Li et al. swept the expert count of small MoEs, and with an update rate of 1e-3 the busiest expert&#x27;s load grew with it, to about 50 times the mean at 1,024 experts, where an aux loss of 1e-2 alone stayed under about 7 times<sup data-k=\"slicing-f12\"><a href=\"#ref-2605.11689\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">84</a></sup>. Above 128 experts the bias also hurt LM quality, and the authors suspect a step too coarse for that many experts<sup data-k=\"slicing-s33\"><a href=\"#ref-2605.11689\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">85</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">The reference lines on the overload trace in the figure above come from the same 1B runs, measured on each training batch rather than over the validation set: MaxVio settled near 0.72 with the aux loss at 1e-3 and near 0.1 with the bias<sup data-k=\"lossfree-f3\"><a href=\"#ref-2408.15664\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">86</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">Megatron&#x27;s rule is the simplest one you could write: every step, each bias moves up or down by the same fixed amount, however far its expert sits from the mean load. That is the sign update, and it is not the only way to run the loop. A linear update moves each bias in proportion to its expert&#x27;s distance from the mean load, so a badly overloaded expert gets a large correction and a nearly balanced one a small one, while an AdamW update adapts the step for each expert. LongCat-Flash runs a linear update, aimed at the expert loads its compute budget calls for<sup data-k=\"balance-longcat\"><a href=\"#ref-2509.01322\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">65</a></sup>, and ZAYA1-8B feeds each expert&#x27;s load error to AdamW<sup data-k=\"zaya1-adamw\"><a href=\"#ref-src-zaya1-adamw\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">87</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">The rules also differ in a subtler way: when an update&#x27;s steps do not sum to zero, the mean of all the biases drifts. That drift changes no selection, since adding the same number to every expert&#x27;s score leaves the top-k as it was, but it costs precision where the bias is stored in bf16; Megatron keeps it in fp32. Moonlight and Trinity Mini avoid the drift by subtracting the mean of the steps<sup class=\"fn-multi\"><a data-ks=\"moonlight-centered trinity-centered\" href=\"#ref-2502.16982\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">88,89</a></sup>. The released weights differ the same way: DeepSeek-V3&#x27;s biases sit at a per-layer mean of 2.2 to 8.0, while Moonlight&#x27;s and ZAYA1-8B&#x27;s average 0 and LongCat-Flash&#x27;s sit just below it<sup data-k=\"ckpt-bias-means\"><a href=\"#ref-src-ckpt-bias-means\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">90</a></sup>. Below, the sign update, a centered sign update, the linear update and the AdamW update run on the same batch.</p>"} />

<Viz name="bias-update-rules" height={614} caption="Figure 3.3.7" />

<Fragment set:html={"<p class=\"full\">The figure&#x27;s toy batch has a few hot experts and many just under the mean load, so most sign steps point up: the sign and AdamW updates lift the mean of the biases while the centered sign and linear updates keep it at zero, yet the sign update balances as well as the centered one at every step, since top-k ignores a shift shared by all experts. Whatever rule moves it, the bias can only balance the tokens it counts. A batch-wide bias is fitted on one batch and applied to the next, so it can make the batch as a whole look even without guaranteeing that each sequence is: a single sequence can still pile onto a few experts. The obvious fix, a second bias computed over each whole sequence, would use tokens that come later in that sequence, and so leak the future into the routing of the earlier ones.</p><p class=\"full\">Chang&#x27;s causal bias avoids the leak by building that second bias only from what the sequence has already routed. It resets at the start of every sequence, and after each token it lowers the bias of the experts just picked and raises the others&#x27;, so each expert&#x27;s bias tracks how far its picks so far sit above or below its share, k/N per token, and an expert this sequence has overused is pushed down for the tokens that follow. Like the batch bias, it only changes which experts are picked and is never trained by the gradient<sup data-k=\"chang-cdb\"><a href=\"#ref-doc-4\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">91</a></sup>. The next figure routes the same sequence with the global-batch bias alone and with the causal bias from this sequence&#x27;s past added on top.</p>"} />

<Viz name="sequence-level-balance" height={880} caption="Figure 3.3.8" />

<Fragment set:html={"<p class=\"full\">The sequence shown switches to code halfway. With only the global-batch bias, its code tokens pile onto one expert, even though the whole batch stays balanced; with the causal bias added, those code tokens spread over more experts and the sequence&#x27;s load stays much closer to even. On the other hand, the simulation shows the causal bias sending more of the picks to experts outside the router&#x27;s own top-k, away from what the router itself prefers, and the evidence for it is small-scale, from Chang&#x27;s experiments on nanochat and TinyStories<sup data-k=\"chang-cdb\"><a href=\"#ref-doc-4\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">91</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">Released models handle the single sequence differently. DeepSeek-V3 pairs its bias with the sequence-wise aux loss we saw earlier, at a tiny coefficient of 1e-4<sup data-k=\"dsv3-alpha\"><a href=\"#ref-2412.19437\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">62</a></sup>: the bias keeps the batch even, and the small loss guards against extreme imbalance within a single sequence. The figure that follows compares no such loss, a small one and a large one, on an ordinary sequence and on an extreme one.</p>"} />

<Viz name="sequence-wise-aux-complement" height={1032} caption="Figure 3.3.9" />

<Fragment set:html={"<p class=\"full\">In that toy, whose two coefficients are illustrative rather than DeepSeek-V3&#x27;s, a small coefficient brings an ordinary sequence closer to even while moving the router only a little, but leaves an extreme sequence piled onto one expert; spreading that one takes a large coefficient, which pulls the router much further off the routing the LM loss prefers. A sequence is one way a batch-wide count can hide an imbalance, and a modality is another.</p><p class=\"full\">When a batch mixes modalities, a bias counted over all the tokens can balance their sum while the tokens of one modality, taken alone, still crowd onto a few experts. DeepSeek-VL2 and Kimi-VL keep one bias for all tokens<sup class=\"fn-multi\"><a data-ks=\"dsvl2-bias kimivl-bias\" href=\"#ref-src-dsvl2-bias\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">92,93</a></sup>, and DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash keeps one per modality<sup data-k=\"balance-dsv41\"><a href=\"#ref-2609.19969\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">63</a></sup>. The figure below compares one shared bias with one bias per modality, each counting only its own modality&#x27;s tokens.</p>\n"} />

<Viz name="modality-split-bias" height={802} caption="Figure 3.3.10" />

<Fragment set:html={"\n\n\n\n<p class=\"full\">The toy scripts text to lean toward four of its eight experts and images toward the other four, so a shared bias balances the total while each modality&#x27;s surplus cancels the other&#x27;s, and a bias per modality balances each modality on its own. The price of the split is that the bias then pushes every expert to take its share of each modality, away from specializing in images.</p><p class=\"full\">To run it in Megatron, turn the bias on with <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-router-enable-expert-bias</span></code> and set its fixed step with <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-router-bias-update-rate</span></code>, with sigmoid scores (<code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-router-score-function</span> sigmoid</code>), since Megatron accepts the bias only with sigmoid or sqrt(softplus) scores (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/transformer_config.py#L3139-L3147\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">transformer_config.py#L3139-L3147</span></code></a>).</p>\n<details><summary>Megatron source</summary><details class=\"src\"><summary><a class=\"nw\">moe_utils.py#L936-L939</span></code></a> (bias in selection only)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">936</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\">torch.distributed.all_reduce(tokens_per_expert, group=tp_dp_cp_group)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1255</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\">num_experts = tokens_per_expert.shape[-<span class=\"hl-m\">1</span>]</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1256</span><span class=\"code\" class=\"ln\">1257</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-c\"># Compare each tokens_per_expert value with the row average without converting the integer</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1258</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-c\"># counts to floating point: tokens_per_expert &lt; total / num_experts iff</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1259</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-c\"># tokens_per_expert * num_experts &lt; total.</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1260</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\">update_direction = torch.sign(total_tokens - tokens_per_expert * num_experts)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1261</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\">updated_expert_bias = (</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1262</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">expert_bias + update_direction.to(dtype=expert_bias.dtype) * expert_bias_update_rate</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1263</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\">)</span></span></pre></details><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/distributed/finalize_model_grads.py#L726-L731\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">finalize_model_grads.py#L726-L731</span></code></a> (update once per step)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">726</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">if</span> config.moe_router_enable_expert_bias:</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">727</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">if</span> pg_collection <span class=\"hl-k\">is</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">None</span>:</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">728</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\">tp_dp_cp_group = parallel_state.get_tensor_and_data_parallel_group(</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">729</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:12ch\">with_context_parallel=<span class=\"hl-k\">True</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">730</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\">)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">731</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">_update_router_expert_bias(model, config, tp_dp_cp_group=tp_dp_cp_group)</span></span></pre></details></details>\n<p class=\"full watch\">When the update rate is too coarse for your expert count, the load stays uneven, which slows the step as Cost of imbalance showed, and above 128 experts Li et al.&#x27;s sweep also saw LM quality suffer. You can catch it in two places. Every checkpoint saves <code>expert_bias</code>, so you can follow how far the biases of each layer spread from one checkpoint to the next, and the token counts that <code><span class=\"nw\">--save-tokens-per-expert-interval</span></code> writes give you MaxVio per global batch (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>).</p><p class=\"full rec\">Use it when the balancer must barely pull against the LM loss: the bias adds no gradient at all, and DeepSeek-V3&#x27;s sequence-wise loss at \u03b1 = 1e-4<sup data-k=\"dsv3-alpha\"><a href=\"#ref-2412.19437\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">62</a></sup> adds very little. Run it with sigmoid scores, and tune the update rate to your expert count, since 1e-3 can be too coarse above 128 experts<sup data-k=\"slicing-s33\"><a href=\"#ref-2605.11689\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">85</a></sup>.</p>\n<p class=\"full\">The bias moves by the same small step whatever the load looks like, so it can take many steps to catch up with a router that keeps changing. Instead of stepping toward the right bias, could you compute it directly from the scores of the current batch?</p>\n<h4 id=\"s-3-3-quantile\">Quantile</h4>\n<p class=\"full\">Quantile balancing keeps the bias but solves for it. For each expert, it ranks the micro-batch&#x27;s tokens by their score for that expert and reads off a quantile: the score that only the expert&#x27;s target share of tokens beats. The bias is set from that quantile instead of moved by a fixed step. Jin et al. compared balancers on a 19B-parameter MoE with 768 experts, and quantile balancing balanced sooner than the sign update at about the same LM loss, halving the average MaxVio, though over the last 1k steps its MaxVio ended slightly higher<sup data-k=\"idbal-t1\"><a href=\"#ref-2609.39137\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">94</a></sup>. That precision has a price: in every micro-batch, each expert has to rank all of the micro-batch&#x27;s tokens by its score to find its quantile, a top-k per expert on top of the usual top-k per token. Side by side below, the sign update and quantile balancing run on the same batch.</p>\n"} />

<Viz name="quantile-balancing" height={686} caption="Figure 3.3.11" />

<Fragment set:html={"\n\n\n\n<p class=\"full\">The toy routes the same batch at every step, so each solved bias routes the very tokens it was solved from: after the last step the sign update still leaves the load uneven, while quantile balancing brings it close to even within a few steps. In Megatron, each micro-batch solves for its own quantiles, and the solutions are averaged over the global batch to route the next one, optionally smoothed by an exponential moving average (EMA, <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-router-quantile-balancing-ema</span></code>).<span class=\"sn\">An EMA keeps most of the previous bias and mixes in only a share of each new solution, so the bias changes slowly from step to step.</span></p>\n<details><summary>Megatron source</summary><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/moe_utils.py#L233-L247\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">moe_utils.py#L233-L247</span></code></a> (top-k and per-expert quantile)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">233</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\">topk_result = (scores - beta).topk(k + <span class=\"hl-m\">1</span>, dim=<span class=\"hl-m\">1</span>)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">234</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\">indices = topk_result.indices[:, :-<span class=\"hl-m\">1</span>]</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">235</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">236</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">if</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">not</span> update_beta:</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">237</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">return</span> indices, beta</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">238</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">239</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">241</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-s\">f</span><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span><span class=\"hl-s\">(</span><span stacked_new_beta.mean(dim=-<span class=\"hl-m\">1</span>, keepdim=<span class=\"hl-k\">True</span>)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">455</span><span class=\"code\" class=\"hl-k\">in</span> <span class=\"hl-b\">zip</span>(qb_beta_list, stacked_new_beta):</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">457</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">qb_beta.copy_(new_beta)</span></span></pre></details><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/router.py#L256-L259\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">router.py#L256-L259</span></code></a> (aux losses must be 0)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">256</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-s\">Quantile balancing handles load balance via the bias update; </span><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">259</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\"><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span><span class=\"hl-s\">aux losses must be disabled (set moe_aux_loss_coeff to 0).</span><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span></span></span></pre></details><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/router.py#L398-L403\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">router.py#L398-L403</span></code></a> (no fusion, no group limit)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">398</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">assert</span> (</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">399</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">not</span> <span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.config.moe_router_fusion</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">400</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\">), <span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span><span class=\"hl-s\">Quantile balancing routing does not support moe_router_fusion.</span><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">401</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">assert</span> (</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">402</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.config.moe_router_num_groups <span class=\"hl-k\">is</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">None</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">and</span> <span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.config.moe_router_group_topk <span class=\"hl-k\">is</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">None</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">403</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\">), <span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span><span class=\"hl-s\">Quantile balancing routing does not support group-limited routing.</span><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span></span></span></pre></details></details>\n<p class=\"full\">Kimi K3 runs quantile balancing over its whole global batch, reading each expert&#x27;s quantile from a histogram summed across GPUs<sup data-k=\"balance-k3\"><a href=\"#ref-2607.24653\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">64</a></sup>. It left the fixed-step rule because balancing grew harder once its routed pool reached 896 experts per layer<sup data-k=\"k3-qb-why\"><a href=\"#ref-2607.24653\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">64</a></sup>, and Su Jianlin, who proposed quantile balancing<sup data-k=\"su-qb\"><a href=\"#ref-src-su-qb\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">95</a></sup>, writes that Kimi K2 had used the sign update<sup data-k=\"su-k3\"><a href=\"#ref-src-su-k3\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">96</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">Averaging has a price: a bias shared by the whole global batch can balance that batch while each GPU&#x27;s micro-batch stays uneven. Neitemeier et al. measured how wide that gap gets in a 7.5B run (one seed). The rank-averaged quantile that Megatron uses balanced the global batch to a MaxVio of 0.92, while in the worst layer the busiest expert of an expert-parallel micro-batch still took about 7 times its fair share, a local MaxVio of 5.96. Solving for the exact global-batch quantile lowered both<sup data-k=\"eqb-t1\"><a href=\"#ref-2609.28053\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">97</a></sup>. They read local MaxVio as a proxy for dispatch cost rather than as measured throughput<sup data-k=\"eqb-lim\"><a href=\"#ref-2609.28053\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">98</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">How do the balancers compare in throughput? We ran five router configurations from init on the same model, with one seed, and compared their median throughput over the last 20 of 120 iterations. Quantile balancing reached 139 TFLOP/s, against 125 for the aux loss and 99 for the tested bias-only configuration. The bias-only run used sigmoid scores and an update rate of 1e-3, while the other four used softmax.<span class=\"sn\">These runs used an older Megatron commit, which accumulated the bias&#x27;s token counts in bf16, so a count could round and flip the direction of its bias update; at <code>d6316eb45</code> the counts are exact.</span> With an EMA of 0.9, quantile balancing reached 121 TFLOP/s. That setting gives each new solution 10% of the weight in the bias update (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/transformer_config.py#L910-L913\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">transformer_config.py#L910-L913</span></code></a>).</p><p class=\"full\">Jin et al. added an EMA to quantile balancing: stronger smoothing kept the bias steadier between checkpoints but corrected the load more slowly early in training<sup data-k=\"idbal-f5\"><a href=\"#ref-2609.39137\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">99</a></sup>. All five runs are plotted below.</p>"} />

<Viz name="router-mode-5way" height={891} caption="Figure 3.3.12" />

<Fragment set:html={"<p class=\"full\">Of the five runs in the figure, the bias-only one stays flat while the other four climb, with quantile balancing fastest over the final 20 iterations. Throughput is still rising at the end, so the ranking describes these first 120 iterations. Quantile balancing also ends with the highest training LM loss of the five, so its throughput lead alone does not establish faster training to the same quality.</p><p class=\"full\">To try quantile balancing, set <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-router-load-balancing-type</span> quantile_balancing</code>.<span class=\"sn\">The same flag also accepts <code>sinkhorn</code>: Sinkhorn uses a balanced-assignment procedure to choose experts for each token during training.</span></p><p class=\"full watch\">The risk is a global batch that looks balanced while each GPU&#x27;s micro-batch is not, which Neitemeier et al. read as a higher dispatch cost. To see the gap, compute MaxVio twice from the token counts that <code><span class=\"nw\">--save-tokens-per-expert-interval</span></code> writes, once per micro-batch and once per global batch, and compare the two (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>).</p><p class=\"full rec\">Use it when the sign-update bias balances too slowly: solving for the bias reached a lower average MaxVio than stepping toward it, at about the same LM loss, with 768 experts<sup data-k=\"idbal-t1\"><a href=\"#ref-2609.39137\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">94</a></sup>, and it had the highest median throughput over the last 20 of 120 iterations in our five-configuration comparison (one seed). Plan around its limits, since Megatron currently refuses it next to any aux loss, <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-router-fusion</span></code> or group-limited routing.</p>\n<h4>What to watch</h4>\n<table class=\"glance\"><thead><tr><th>Part</th><th>Symptom</th><th>Signal to watch</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>Cost of imbalance</td><td><code>slow</code>: the GPU holding the busiest experts sets the step.</td><td>TFLOP/s against the forced-even control run.</td></tr><tr><td>Aux loss</td><td><code>learns badly</code>: a coefficient set too high makes the model learn worse, and one set too low leaves the load uneven.</td><td>LM loss and MaxVio together: with flat router probabilities, the aux loss can look balanced while the load stays uneven<sup data-k=\"sigma-early\"><a href=\"#ref-2512.16248\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">77</a></sup>.</td></tr><tr><td>Scope</td><td><code>learns badly</code>: balancing per sequence or per micro-batch keeps domain experts from forming. <code>slow</code>: balancing per global batch leaves the load on each GPU uneven; the step took 5.8% longer, mainly from that imbalance and about 1% of that slowdown from communication<sup data-k=\"qiu-t5\"><a href=\"#ref-2501.11873\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">81</a></sup>.</td><td>MaxVio at the scope you balance: per micro-batch or per global batch from the token counts, per sequence from the routing trace (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>).</td></tr><tr><td>Loss-free bias</td><td><code>slow</code>: with a fixed step of 1e-3, the busiest expert still gets about 20 times the mean load at 128 experts<sup data-k=\"slicing-f12\"><a href=\"#ref-2605.11689\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">84</a></sup>. <code>learns badly</code>: above 128 experts, that step is too coarse<sup data-k=\"slicing-s33\"><a href=\"#ref-2605.11689\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">85</a></sup>.</td><td>How far <code>expert_bias</code> spreads in each layer across checkpoints, and MaxVio per global batch from the token counts (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>).</td></tr><tr><td>Quantile</td><td><code>slow</code>: smoothing the bias with a moving average makes it lag the router&#x27;s scores. The bias is shared by the global batch, so each GPU&#x27;s load can stay uneven, as in Scope.</td><td>MaxVio per micro-batch alongside MaxVio per global batch, from the saved token counts (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>), to detect a globally balanced batch hiding local imbalance<sup data-k=\"eqb-t1\"><a href=\"#ref-2609.28053\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">97</a></sup>.</td></tr></tbody></table>\n\n<p class=\"full\">Every balancer in this section spreads tokens over a set of experts that was fixed before training started, and we have not yet asked what that set should look like. How many experts should a layer have, how wide should each one be, and should some of them see every token?</p>\n<h3 id=\"s-3-4\">3.4 Experts: how many, how big, shared or not</h3>\n"} />

<Viz name="hero-anatomy-zoom" query="&stage=5&mini=1" w={600} h={347} />

<Fragment set:html={"\n<p class=\"full\">Once the router has picked a token&#x27;s experts, what those experts look like decides both what the layer can learn and what it costs to run. You choose how many experts a layer has and how wide each one is, how to keep the network traffic of many small experts in check, whether one expert should see every token, and which activation runs inside each of them.</p>\n<p class=\"meta\">Expert choices in leading open MoEs, plus Nemotron for LatentMoE and ReLU\u00b2 and LongCat-Flash for variable compute. Count and size gives the routed experts each token uses out of the total, then each expert&#x27;s FFN width; shared experts are listed separately. Latent dimensions show token width before and after projection. &quot;No limit specified&quot; refers to the published configuration.</p><table class=\"glance\"><thead><tr><th>Model</th><th>Count and size</th><th>Latent MoE</th><th>Shared experts</th><th>Variable compute</th><th>Activation</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash<sup data-k=\"shape-dsv41\"><a href=\"#ref-2609.19969\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">100</a></sup></td><td>6 of 384, width 2,304</td><td>Full-width experts</td><td>1, width 2,304</td><td>Fixed top-k, equal widths</td><td>SwiGLU, limit 10</td></tr><tr><td>GLM-5.3<sup data-k=\"shape-glm53\"><a href=\"#ref-src-shape-glm53\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">101</a></sup></td><td>8 of 256, width 2,048</td><td>Full-width experts</td><td>1, width 2,048</td><td>Fixed top-k, equal widths</td><td>SiLU gate; no limit specified</td></tr><tr><td>Kimi K3<sup data-k=\"shape-k3\"><a href=\"#ref-2607.24653\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">102</a></sup></td><td>16 of 896, width 3,072</td><td>7,168 to 3,584; norm before up-projection</td><td>2, width 3,072 each</td><td>Fixed top-k, equal widths</td><td>SiTU-GLU, caps 4 and 25</td></tr><tr><td>LongCat-Flash<sup data-k=\"shape-longcat\"><a href=\"#ref-2509.01322\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">103</a></sup></td><td>12 of 768, width 2,048</td><td>Full-width experts</td><td>None</td><td>256 of the 768 are identity experts: 18.6B to 31.3B active parameters per token, 27B on average</td><td>SiLU gate; no limit specified</td></tr><tr><td>MiMo-V2.6-Pro-RL<sup data-k=\"shape-mimo26\"><a href=\"#ref-src-shape-mimo26\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">104</a></sup></td><td>8 of 384, width 2,048</td><td>Full-width experts</td><td>None</td><td>Fixed top-k, equal widths</td><td>SiLU gate; no limit specified</td></tr><tr><td>Nemotron 3 Ultra<sup data-k=\"shape-nemotron-ultra\"><a href=\"#ref-src-shape-nemotron-ultra\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">105</a></sup></td><td>22 of 512, width 5,120</td><td>8,192 to 2,048</td><td>1, width 10,240</td><td>Fixed top-k, equal widths</td><td>ReLU\u00b2</td></tr><tr><td>Qwen3.8 2.4T-A95B<sup data-k=\"shape-qwen38\"><a href=\"#ref-src-shape-qwen38\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">106</a></sup></td><td>10 of 512, width 2,048</td><td>Full-width experts</td><td>1, width 2,048</td><td>Fixed top-k, equal widths</td><td>SiLU gate; no limit specified</td></tr><tr><td>Step 5 Preview<sup data-k=\"shape-step5\"><a href=\"#ref-src-shape-step5\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">107</a></sup></td><td>8 of 352, width 1,536</td><td>Full-width experts</td><td>1, width 1,536</td><td>Fixed top-k, equal widths</td><td>SwiGLU, limit 7</td></tr></tbody></table>\n<p class=\"full\">With the parameter budget fixed, adding more experts means making each one narrower. Which split gives you a better model for the same compute?</p>\n<h4 id=\"s-3-4-count-and-size\">Count and size</h4>\n<p class=\"full\">Picture one MoE layer with a fixed budget of parameters and compute, and cut its experts finer: twice as many experts at half the width, with twice the top-k, keeps both budgets the same. Figure 3.4.1 draws that ladder: coarse, then 16 experts of d/2 with top-4, then 32 of d/4 with top-8. Liew et al. ran the ablation in an 18-layer model with total and active parameters fixed, and loss fell with each finer cut up to 128 experts of d/4 with top-8, from 2.334 to 2.297, while finer cuts did not improve it further<sup data-k=\"liew-t6\"><a href=\"#ref-2601.08215\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">108</a></sup>. In this experiment, then, segmenting stopped paying past 128 experts, while every finer step still ran smaller GEMMs and dispatched more copies of each token.</p>\n"} />

<Viz name="expert-granularity" height={583} caption="Figure 3.4.1" />

<Fragment set:html={"\n\n\n\n<p class=\"full\">Released models sit on different rungs of that ladder. gpt-oss-120b and Trinity Large keep each expert as wide as the model, with top-4 of 128 and top-4 of 256 experts, Mistral Small 4 halves that width with top-4 of 128, and Qwen3.5-35B-A3B quarters it with top-8 of 256<sup data-k=\"cfg-granularity\"><a href=\"#ref-src-cfg-granularity\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">109</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">In the figure, you can see that splitting the experts changes their number and width while keeping the total parameters and compute per token fixed. The total tile area represents the parameters, and the dark area represents the expert computation for one token. What you are changing is the granularity.<span class=\"sn\">Granularity describes how finely you divide the expert capacity: finer granularity means more, narrower experts. To keep the same compute per token, each token uses more of the narrower experts.</span> You can also increase sparsity: add more parameters while keeping the number used by each token fixed. Two ways to spend those extra parameters are compared next: more routed experts, or n-gram embedding tables looked up by token id.</p>"} />

<Viz name="sparse-param-budget" height={707} caption="Figure 3.4.2" />

<Fragment set:html={"<p class=\"full\">The figure plots LongCat&#x27;s comparison at 280M active parameters. There, n-gram tables lowered the loss more than the same parameters spent on more experts (by about 0.01 at 17 times the active parameters) once the experts already held about 12 times the active parameters, while tables added when the experts stopped at about 4 times lost from the start. The gain had a limit: on English validation loss, more experts won again once tables held a little over 40% of the parameters, and LongCat&#x27;s rule, read from that crossing, is to put no more than 50% of the parameters in tables<sup data-k=\"longcat-ngram-f2\"><a href=\"#ref-src-longcat-ngram-f2\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">110</a></sup>. In that comparison, expanding the experts paid off first, and once they were large enough, part of the next parameters did more in lookup tables. Where you spend extra parameters therefore depends on how much expert capacity you already have.</p><p class=\"full\">Both ways of spending ship in released models: Kimi K2 grows its pool to 384 experts with top-8, and LongCat-Flash-Lite puts 31.4B of its 68.5B parameters in n-gram embedding tables<sup data-k=\"flashlite-ngram\"><a href=\"#ref-src-flashlite-ngram\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">111</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">Whether you add more experts or divide the existing capacity into narrower ones, the resulting shape also affects training speed. Narrower experts run smaller GEMMs, which use the GPU less efficiently.</p><p class=\"full\">For a dense model, compute-optimal scaling laws help you choose how to divide a fixed FLOP budget between model size and training tokens: a larger model leaves room for fewer tokens, while a smaller one can train on more<sup data-k=\"chinchilla-allocation\"><a href=\"#ref-2203.15556\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">112</a></sup>. An MoE adds another choice. You can increase its total parameters without increasing the parameters used by each token, so you also need to choose its sparsity. For either kind of model, choosing under a time limit also means accounting for how efficiently each configuration uses your hardware<sup data-k=\"mosaic-f1\"><a href=\"#ref-2608.10605\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">113</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">Sarkar et al. combined a fitted loss scaling law with a training-throughput model to compare the sparsity that minimizes loss at fixed model FLOPs with the sparsity that minimizes loss at fixed cluster time. Their predictions for one cluster and training window are plotted below<sup data-k=\"mosaic-f1\"><a href=\"#ref-2608.10605\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">113</a></sup>.<span class=\"sn\">Sparsity here is the fraction of the model&#x27;s parameters that a token does not use.</span></p>"} />

<Viz name="cluster-optimal-sparsity" height={765} caption="Figure 3.4.3" />

<Fragment set:html={"<p class=\"full\">In their predictions, choosing by model FLOPs alone points you toward the sparsest model in the grid. Once the authors account for training speed on their cluster, the lowest predicted loss moves to a less sparse configuration, 0.031 nats below the sparsest one. As in dense scaling, you are balancing model capacity against how many tokens you can train on. With an MoE, increasing sparsity can lower hardware utilization without increasing model FLOPs per token, so extra capacity can still cost training time<sup data-k=\"mosaic-f1\"><a href=\"#ref-2608.10605\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">113</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\"><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\">Megatron</a> sets the shape with three flags: <code><span class=\"nw\">--num-experts</span></code> for the count, <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-ffn-hidden-size</span></code> for each expert&#x27;s width and <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-router-topk</span></code> for how many experts each token picks.</p>\n<details><summary>Megatron source</summary><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/experts.py#L262-L293\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/router.py#L77-L81\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">router.py#L77-L81</span></code></a> (router weight N x d)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">77</span><span class=\"code\" TODO: Add support for GPU initialization, which requires updating the golden values.</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">79</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.weight = torch.nn.Parameter(</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">80</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">torch.empty((<span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.config.num_moe_experts, <span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.config.hidden_size), dtype=torch.float32)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">81</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\">)</span></span></pre></details></details>\n<p class=\"full watch\">Cut the experts too fine and each expert&#x27;s GEMMs get small enough to lower TFLOP/s per GPU, and with the wrong granularity the model learns worse. To see what a shape costs you, read the grouped-GEMM time per MoE layer from the profiler next to TFLOP/s per GPU from <code><span class=\"nw\">--log-throughput</span></code>. The token counts that <code><span class=\"nw\">--save-tokens-per-expert-interval</span></code> writes also show dead experts that receive no tokens (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>), but an expert can also die while it keeps its tokens, which shows in its output norm instead (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-6\">3.6</a>)<sup data-k=\"step35-dead\"><a href=\"#ref-2602.10604\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">114</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full rec\">Use finer experts at equal total and active parameters, up to a point: in one 18-layer model, the loss fell until 128 experts of d/4 with top-8, and finer splits gained nothing<sup data-k=\"liew-t6\"><a href=\"#ref-2601.08215\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">108</a></sup>. Stop going finer once small GEMMs and token copies start to dominate your step.</p>\n<p class=\"full\">Finer experts help the loss only up to a point, and each finer step sends more copies of every token over the network. Could you keep the many small experts and send less data for each copy?</p>\n<h4 id=\"s-3-4-latent-moe\">Latent MoE</h4>\n<p class=\"full\">Each copy of a token carries its full hidden state, d numbers, sent once for every expert it picked. LatentMoE shrinks that copy. It projects each token down to a smaller latent \u2113 before dispatch, runs the experts on \u2113, and projects back up after combine. Every copy shrinks from d to \u2113, and the savings pay for more experts: at the same expert parameters, expert compute and traffic, the layer can afford \u03b1 = d/\u2113 times more experts and an \u03b1 times larger top-k<sup data-k=\"latentmoe-alpha\"><a href=\"#ref-2601.18089\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">115</a></sup>. Below, a standard layer sits next to its latent counterpart, keeping the parameters and compute inside the experts fixed.</p>\n"} />

<Viz name="latent-moe" height={474} caption="Figure 3.4.4" />

<Fragment set:html={"\n\n\n<p class=\"full\">Drawn as tiles, the standard layer selects four of sixteen experts, each receiving a full-width token vector. The latent layer selects eight of thirty-two, but each receives only half as many values. Both send the same amount of data per token. Their total tile area and dark area are also equal, showing unchanged expert parameters and compute; the two projections add work outside those areas<sup data-k=\"latentmoe-alpha\"><a href=\"#ref-2601.18089\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">115</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">Does the trade pay off in quality? In NVIDIA&#x27;s 73B-A8B ablation, at equal total and active parameters, LatentMoE scored 4.6 points higher on MMLU-Pro than a standard MoE<sup data-k=\"latentmoe-t4\"><a href=\"#ref-2601.18089\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">116</a></sup>, and a small-scale replication also lowered perplexity<sup data-k=\"mhlatent-t1\"><a href=\"#ref-2602.04870\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">117</a></sup>. Nemotron 3 Super, Nemotron 3 Ultra and Kimi K3 ship it<sup data-k=\"cfg-latent\"><a href=\"#ref-src-cfg-latent\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">118</a></sup>. In return, every token runs through two d x \u2113 projections, and serving throughput ranged from 12% lower at concurrency 1 to slightly higher at some concurrencies<sup data-k=\"latentmoe-t5\"><a href=\"#ref-2601.18089\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">119</a></sup>. In Megatron, the latent size is <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-latent-size</span></code>.</p>\n<details><summary>Megatron source</summary><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/moe_layer.py#L304-L335\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.input_size <span class=\"hl-k\">if</span> <span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.config.moe_latent_size <span class=\"hl-k\">is</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">None</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">else</span> <span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.config.moe_latent_size,</span></span></pre></details></details>\n<p class=\"full watch\">A latent MoE can go wrong in two ways: it runs slow, or its activations blow up. When it runs slow, look past the traffic, since the all-to-all now carries \u2113 numbers per copy instead of d, to the two extra projections and the smaller expert GEMMs. LatentMoE&#x27;s NVIDIA authors propose small-GEMM kernels and a separate stream for the shared expert as fixes<sup data-k=\"latentmoe-opt\"><a href=\"#ref-2601.18089\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">120</a></sup>. When it blows up, as Kimi K3 reports for the activations of its routed branch at its 2.8-trillion-parameter scale<sup data-k=\"k3-latent\"><a href=\"#ref-2607.24653\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">102</a></sup>, one place to look is the output norm of the up-projection, <code>fc2_latent_proj</code>, which you can compute per layer offline from the <code><span class=\"nw\">--save-activations-interval</span></code> dumps (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>). K3&#x27;s fix was an RMSNorm before the up-projection, <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-use-norm-before-up-proj</span></code>, which it ships with a routed scaling factor of 1.0<sup data-k=\"cfg-k3\"><a href=\"#ref-src-cfg-k3\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">121</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full rec\">Use a latent when you want more experts and a larger top-k without sending more data per token: shrinking each copy to \u2113 = d/\u03b1 affords \u03b1 times more of both<sup data-k=\"latentmoe-alpha\"><a href=\"#ref-2601.18089\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">115</a></sup>, and it scored higher at matched size<sup data-k=\"latentmoe-t4\"><a href=\"#ref-2601.18089\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">116</a></sup>, at the cost of two projections.</p>\n<p class=\"full\">Every expert so far sits behind the router and sees only the tokens sent to it. What would you gain by putting one expert outside the router, where it sees every token, and is it worth its compute?</p>\n<h4 id=\"s-3-4-shared-experts\">Shared experts</h4>\n<p class=\"full\">A shared expert sits outside the router: it processes every token, next to whichever routed experts the token picked. DeepSeekMoE introduced shared experts to learn common patterns, reducing duplicated knowledge across routed experts and giving them room to specialize<sup data-k=\"dsmoe-shared-purpose\"><a href=\"#ref-2401.06066\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">122</a></sup>. In its ablation at equal compute, adding a shared expert improved results on most benchmarks<sup data-k=\"dsmoe-f3\"><a href=\"#ref-2401.06066\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">123</a></sup>. Qwen&#x27;s models show how open the question is: the Qwen3 MoE models in the Qwen3 report dropped it<sup data-k=\"qwen3-moe\"><a href=\"#ref-2505.09388\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">124</a></sup>, Qwen3-Next brought it back behind a sigmoid gate<sup data-k=\"cfg-qwen3next\"><a href=\"#ref-src-cfg-qwen3next\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">125</a></sup>, and Qwen3.5 kept it<sup data-k=\"cfg-qwen35\"><a href=\"#ref-src-cfg-qwen35\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">126</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">Measured by loss in models trained from scratch at equal compute, the gain is small and not consistent. A shared expert lowered loss by about 0.01 to 0.02 in Pangu Ultra MoE and in Sun et al.&#x27;s expert-threshold models<sup class=\"fn-multi\"><a data-ks=\"pangu-ultra-f2 et-t8\" href=\"#ref-2505.04519\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">127,128</a></sup>, while in OLMoE and in Li et al.&#x27;s sweep of small MoEs it did nothing or slightly worse<sup class=\"fn-multi\"><a data-ks=\"olmoe-f6 slicing-shared\" href=\"#ref-2409.02060\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">129,130</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">Sharing also reduces the router&#x27;s freedom. With 32 experts and 4 active, reserving one slot for a shared expert leaves 4,495 ways to choose the other 3, compared with 35,960 ways to choose 4 from all 32. If you instead add the shared expert without reducing routed top-k, its always-on FFN increases compute per token: Mellum2 added a shared expert without reducing routed top-k and found no quality gain and slower inference<sup data-k=\"mellum2-shared\"><a href=\"#ref-2605.31268\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">131</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">If you do add one, you still have to decide how its output mixes with the routed experts&#x27;. The figure below lines up five designs at equal compute per token: routed only, a shared expert at fixed weight (DeepSeek-V3)<sup data-k=\"cfg-dsv3\"><a href=\"#ref-src-cfg-dsv3\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">30</a></sup>, a gated one (Qwen3-Next)<sup data-k=\"cfg-qwen3next\"><a href=\"#ref-src-cfg-qwen3next\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">125</a></sup>, one normalized jointly with the routed experts (Inkling)<sup data-k=\"tf-inkling\"><a href=\"#ref-src-tf-inkling\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">19</a></sup> and a parallel dense FFN (Grok 2)<sup data-k=\"grok2-parallel\"><a href=\"#ref-src-grok2-parallel\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">132</a></sup>.</p>\n"} />

<Viz name="shared-expert-weight" height={863} caption="Figure 3.4.5" />

<Fragment set:html={"\n\n\n<p class=\"full\">Lined up this way, a shared expert at fixed weight holds the same share of every token&#x27;s weight, Qwen&#x27;s gate moves that share between 0 and 50%, Inkling&#x27;s router learns it per token by scoring the shared experts with the routed ones, and a parallel dense FFN without renormalization holds over half of it whatever the router scores.</p><p class=\"full\">Megatron&#x27;s flags are <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-shared-expert-intermediate-size</span></code>, <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-shared-expert-gate</span></code> (a sigmoid gate on its output) and <code><span class=\"nw\">--use-grouped-gemm-for-shared-expert</span></code>. Without the gate, Megatron adds the shared output at weight 1 (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/moe_layer.py#L668-L669\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">moe_layer.py#L668-L669</span></code></a>), while the routed weights sum to the routed scaling factor when normalized over the selected experts (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/moe_utils.py#L942-L947\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">moe_utils.py#L942-L947</span></code></a>, <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-1\">3.1</a>).<span class=\"sn\">Megatron does this with sigmoid routing, or by applying softmax after selecting the top-k experts. For the latter, leave <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-router-pre-softmax</span></code> disabled.</span> The shared expert therefore takes 1/(1 + factor) of each token&#x27;s output weight, 29% at a factor of <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-2-5\">2.5</a>, and the larger the factor, the smaller its share; GLM-4.5-Air and Mistral Large 3 set the factor to 1, which gives it half<sup class=\"fn-multi\"><a data-ks=\"cfg-glm45air cfg-mistral-large3\" href=\"#ref-src-cfg-glm45air\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">133,134</a></sup>.</p>\n<details><summary>Megatron source</summary><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/shared_experts.py#L128-L128\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">shared_experts.py#L128-L128</span></code></a> (width from shared size)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">128</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\">config.ffn_hidden_size = config.moe_shared_expert_intermediate_size</span></span></pre></details><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/shared_experts.py#L201-L208\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">shared_experts.py#L201-L208</span></code></a> (sigmoid gate on output)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">201</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">def</span> <span class=\"hl-f\">forward</span>(<span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>, hidden_states: torch.Tensor) -&gt; torch.Tensor:</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">202</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;&quot;&quot;Forward function&quot;&quot;&quot;</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">203</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">output, _ = <span class=\"hl-b\">super</span>().forward(hidden_states)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">204</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">if</span> <span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.use_shared_expert_gate:</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">205</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\">logits = torch.nn.functional.linear(hidden_states, <span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.gate_weight)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">206</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\">gate_score = torch.nn.functional.sigmoid(logits)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">207</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\">output = output * gate_score</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">208</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">return</span> output</span></span></pre></details><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/moe_layer.py#L668-L669\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">moe_layer.py#L668-L669</span></code></a> (added at weight 1)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">668</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">if</span> shared_expert_output <span class=\"hl-k\">is</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">not</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">None</span>:</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">669</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">output = output + shared_expert_output</span></span></pre></details></details>\n<p class=\"full watch\">To track how much the shared expert contributes to each token&#x27;s output during training, compare the shared branch after its gate with the routed branch after combine and any latent up-projection, using the sparse forward hook in <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>.</p><p class=\"full rec\">A shared expert is a common starting choice in leading MoEs, including GLM-5.3, Kimi K3 and DeepSeek V4.1 Flash<sup class=\"fn-multi\"><a data-ks=\"shared-glm53 shared-k3 shared-dsv41\" href=\"#ref-2602.15763\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">135,136,137</a></sup>. MiMo-V2.6 ships without one<sup data-k=\"shared-mimo26\"><a href=\"#ref-2610.11959\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">138</a></sup>. To compare with and without one at equal compute, give it the routed experts&#x27; width and replace one routed slot with it.</p>\n<p class=\"full\">Shared or not, every expert so far has the same width and every token gets the same top-k, so every token gets the same compute. Could the router give some tokens less compute and others more, while the average stays fixed?</p>\n<h4 id=\"s-3-4-variable-compute\">Variable compute</h4>\n<p class=\"full\">Two designs let the compute per token vary while the mean stays fixed. LongCat-Flash adds zero-computation experts that pass the input through unchanged. Its router selects 12 experts per token from a pool of 512 FFN experts and 256 of these identity paths. A bias adjusts how often the FFN experts are selected, targeting an average of 8 per token while allowing individual tokens to use more or fewer<sup data-k=\"longcat-budget\"><a href=\"#ref-2509.01322\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">139</a></sup>; the standard deviation of that count rose to about <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-2-5\">2.5</a> during pre-training<sup data-k=\"longcat-f3c\"><a href=\"#ref-2509.01322\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">140</a></sup>. Xiaomi&#x27;s AI Lab takes a different approach in MoDSE (Mixture of Diverse Size Experts): they mix expert widths, letting the router choose larger or smaller experts for each token<sup data-k=\"modse-f2\"><a href=\"#ref-2409.12210\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">141</a></sup>. The figure below simulates the active width per token for homogeneous experts, as in Mixtral<sup data-k=\"cfg-mixtral\"><a href=\"#ref-src-cfg-mixtral\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">142</a></sup>, and both designs.</p>\n"} />

<Viz name="variable-compute-experts" height={732} caption="Figure 3.4.6" />

<Fragment set:html={"\n\n\n<p class=\"full\">In the simulation, where one bias per design holds the mean active width at that of the homogeneous experts, some tokens get more of it and others less, and every token still fills all its top-k slots, since only what lands in them varies. In LongCat-Flash&#x27;s ablation, zero-computation experts reached lower loss at matched compute<sup data-k=\"longcat-f3a\"><a href=\"#ref-2509.01322\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">143</a></sup>. Mixed expert sizes can improve quality, but may cost throughput. Xiaomi&#x27;s AI Lab and Wang et al. each put one router over mixed-size experts in small models and report gains<sup class=\"fn-multi\"><a data-ks=\"modse-f2 hmoe-3b\" href=\"#ref-2409.12210\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">141,144</a></sup>. Li et al. found none, but in their setup the experts sit in two pools, each with a fixed top-k, so every token still gets the same compute<sup data-k=\"slicing-pools\"><a href=\"#ref-2605.11689\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">145</a></sup>. In GroveMoE, the cost showed at inference: its mixed sizes ran about 30% slower than Qwen3-30B-A3B, which its authors attribute to processing the two expert sizes with separate MoE kernels in each layer<sup data-k=\"grove-infer\"><a href=\"#ref-2508.07785\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">146</a></sup>.</p>\n<details><summary>Megatron source</summary><p>none: a grep of Megatron-LM main (megatron/ tree) finds neither zero-computation experts nor mixed expert widths; each grouped linear has one <code>moe_ffn_hidden_size</code></p></details>\n<p class=\"full watch\">Track the mean and spread of compute per token in each layer: the number of selected FFN experts for zero-computation routing, or the sum of selected expert widths for mixed-size routing. Compare the mean with the target budget, and log token counts per expert alongside compute per expert-parallel rank and step time.</p><p class=\"full rec\">Start with routed experts of one width and a fixed top-k, as MiMo-V2.6-Pro-RL, GLM-5.3 and Kimi K3 do<sup class=\"fn-multi\"><a data-ks=\"shared-mimo26 shared-glm53 shared-k3\" href=\"#ref-2610.11959\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">138,135,136</a></sup>. This also fits Megatron&#x27;s grouped linear layers, which use a single <code>moe_ffn_hidden_size</code>.</p>\n<p class=\"full\">With the count, width and compute of the experts settled, one choice remains inside each expert: the function between its two projections. How much does that choice matter for an MoE?</p>\n<h4 id=\"s-3-4-activation\">Activation</h4>\n<p class=\"full\">As in a dense Transformer, each expert uses an activation function such as SwiGLU, as in DeepSeek-V3, Qwen3 and Kimi K2<sup data-k=\"cfg-swiglu\"><a href=\"#ref-src-cfg-swiglu\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">147</a></sup>, or ReLU\u00b2 between its input and output projections. In an MoE, you also need to watch how activations behave across experts: Step <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-5\">3.5</a> Flash found that one or two experts could develop rapidly growing output norms while the others stayed stable and the training loss barely changed<sup data-k=\"step35-f4\"><a href=\"#ref-2602.10604\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">15</a></sup>. The activation function, and any clamp applied to it, helps control that growth. The figure below shows how the output of SwiGLU, ReLU\u00b2 and Expert-Specific PolyNorm grows as the input scale rises.</p>\n"} />

<Viz name="expert-activations" height={785} caption="Figure 3.4.7" />

<Fragment set:html={"\n\n\n<p class=\"full\">Scaled up, SwiGLU and ReLU\u00b2 outputs grow with the square of the input scale, while Expert-Specific PolyNorm normalizes its gate, so its output grows only linearly. A clamp can bound that growth. Two studies found similar quality with and without clipping in their tested settings. In a 7.9B MoE ablation by Ant Group&#x27;s Ling team, SwiGLU with clipping matched plain SwiGLU&#x27;s average over 17 benchmarks<sup data-k=\"powlu-t2\"><a href=\"#ref-2605.25704\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">148</a></sup>. Step <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-5\">3.5</a> Flash&#x27;s activation clipping held its outlier experts&#x27; norms down at indistinguishable loss, although that branch started mid-run from the unclipped run<sup data-k=\"step35-f4\"><a href=\"#ref-2602.10604\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">15</a></sup>. Clipping did not eliminate loss spikes in the Ling team&#x27;s <a href=\"https://huggingface.co/spaces/nanotron/ultrascale-playbook?section=fp8_pretraining\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\">FP8</a> run, the clipped SwiGLU delayed the loss spikes, yet one still came around step 77,000<sup data-k=\"powlu-f4\"><a href=\"#ref-2605.25704\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">149</a></sup>. The next figure sweeps the input scale through plain SwiGLU, a hard clamp and SiTU-GLU&#x27;s smooth cap at the same bound, so you can see how each one bends.</p>"} />

<Viz name="activation-bounds" height={792} caption="Figure 3.4.8" />

<Fragment set:html={"<p class=\"full\">Through that sweep, plain SwiGLU&#x27;s largest hidden unit keeps growing with the square of the gate input up to 6,862, the peak K-EXAONE 2.0 measured at its last layer with its clamp disabled at inference<sup data-k=\"kexaone-peak\"><a href=\"#ref-src-kexaone-peak\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">150</a></sup>, the hard clamp follows it up to the bound and stops there, and SiTU-GLU bends away well before the bound. The sweep sets both to stop a hidden unit at about 100, the clamp at 10 and SiTU-GLU with a gate cap of 4 and an up cap of 25, and from about the same input both pass almost no gradient to those units&#x27; gate weights. Replacing the activation altogether rests on thinner evidence. Zhuo et al.&#x27;s PolyNorm lowered average validation loss by 0.02 against SwiGLU in OLMoE-1B-7B<sup data-k=\"polynorm-t2\"><a href=\"#ref-2411.03884\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">151</a></sup>, while Motif 3 compares its Expert-Specific PolyNorm with SwiGLU only on effective rank<sup data-k=\"motif3-f6a\"><a href=\"#ref-2608.09119\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">152</a></sup>. Kimi K3 states that SiTU-GLU&#x27;s smooth cap gives better training behavior than hard clamping<sup data-k=\"k3-appb\"><a href=\"#ref-2607.24653\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">153</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">In Megatron, <code><span class=\"nw\">--activation-func-clamp-value</span></code> sets the hard-clipping limit for SwiGLU in MoE layers; a value of 10 matches DeepSeek V4.1 Flash&#x27;s limit<sup data-k=\"dsv41-activation\"><a href=\"#ref-2609.19969\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">154</a></sup>, which GigaChat <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-5\">3.5</a> and Hy4-preview also use<sup data-k=\"cfg-clamp10\"><a href=\"#ref-src-cfg-clamp10\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">155</a></sup>. Kimi K3&#x27;s smooth cap, SiTU-GLU, is <code><span class=\"nw\">--activation-func-tanh-clamp-scale</span> 4 --activation-func-tanh-clamp-scale-linear 25</code>, available from <a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/releases/tag/core_v0.20.0\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\">Megatron Core v0.20</a> on, and Nemotron 3&#x27;s ReLU\u00b2 is <code><span class=\"nw\">--squared-relu</span></code><sup data-k=\"cfg-latent\"><a href=\"#ref-src-cfg-latent\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">118</a></sup>.</p>\n<details><summary>Megatron source</summary><details class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">18</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;&quot;&quot;Tanh Soft Clamp to precondition activation inputs.&quot;&quot;&quot;</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">19</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">return</span> (scale * torch.tanh(x.float() / scale)).to(x.dtype)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">20</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">21</span><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;&quot;&quot;Sigmoid-Tanh Unit from Kimi K3: ``s * tanh(x / s) * sigmoid(x)``&quot;&quot;&quot;</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">25</span><span
