class=\"full watch\">Outlier activations in the experts can blow up a run. To catch them, compute Step <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-5\">3.5</a> Flash&#x27;s signal, the max-to-median ratio of expert output norms per layer<sup data-k=\"step35-ratio\"><a href=\"#ref-2602.10604\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">156</a></sup>, offline from the <code><span class=\"nw\">--save-activations-interval</span></code> dumps (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>): one expert pulling away from the others shows as a rising ratio. Each dump keeps only the last micro-batch and copies it to the CPU, so sample sparsely.</p><p class=\"full rec\">Start from your reference model&#x27;s activation recipe. Leading MoEs use different ways to bound activations: Step 5 Preview clips SwiGLU at 7, DeepSeek V4.1 Flash at 10, and Kimi K3 uses SiTU-GLU&#x27;s smooth caps<sup class=\"fn-multi\"><a data-ks=\"step5-activation dsv41-activation k3-appb\" href=\"#ref-src-shape-step5\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">107,154,153</a></sup>. For a new architecture, SwiGLU is a reasonable baseline. If expert norms diverge, test clipping and compare training loss as well as the norms before choosing a limit.</p>\n<h4>What to watch</h4>\n<table class=\"glance\"><thead><tr><th>Part</th><th>Symptom</th><th>Signal to watch</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>Count and size</td><td><code>slow</code>: the expert GEMMs are small. Some experts may also get no tokens.</td><td>Grouped-GEMM time per MoE layer from the profiler, TFLOP/s per GPU (<code><span class=\"nw\">--log-throughput</span></code>), and tokens per expert (<code><span class=\"nw\">--save-tokens-per-expert-interval</span></code>, <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>).</td></tr><tr><td>Latent MoE</td><td><code>slow</code>: the two extra projections and the smaller expert GEMMs add time. <code>blows up</code>: Kimi K3 reports exploding activations in the routed branch<sup data-k=\"k3-latent\"><a href=\"#ref-2607.24653\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">102</a></sup>.</td><td>For speed, profile the two latent projections and smaller expert GEMMs alongside the reduced all-to-all. For instability, one place to look is the up-projection output norm (<code>fc2_latent_proj</code>) from activation dumps (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>); Kimi K3 reports routed-branch activation growth<sup data-k=\"k3-latent\"><a href=\"#ref-2607.24653\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">102</a></sup>.</td></tr><tr><td>Shared experts</td><td><code>slow</code> at inference, when it adds compute: Mellum2 found inference slower<sup data-k=\"mellum2-shared\"><a href=\"#ref-2605.31268\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">131</a></sup>.</td><td>Shared output after its gate against routed output after combine and any latent up-projection, from the sparse forward hook in <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>.</td></tr><tr><td>Variable compute</td><td><code>slow</code>: each expert size runs its own kernels, and GroveMoE reports about 30% slower inference than Qwen3-30B-A3B<sup data-k=\"grove-infer\"><a href=\"#ref-2508.07785\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">146</a></sup>.</td><td>Per layer, mean and spread of selected FFN count or summed expert width against the target compute budget. Log token counts per expert alongside compute per EP rank and step time.</td></tr><tr><td>Activation</td><td><code>blows up</code>: outlier activations.</td><td>The max-to-median ratio of expert output norms per layer, Step <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-5\">3.5</a> Flash&#x27;s signal<sup data-k=\"step35-ratio\"><a href=\"#ref-2602.10604\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">156</a></sup>, from the activation dumps (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>).</td></tr></tbody></table>\n<p class=\"full\">Everything in this section happened inside one MoE layer, while a model stacks many of them, which raises two questions we have not asked yet. Should every layer be an MoE layer, and how should the block around each one be wired?</p>\n<h3 id=\"s-3-5\">3.5 Block layout</h3>\n"} />

<Viz name="hero-anatomy-zoom" query="&stage=6&mini=1" w={600} h={347} />

<Fragment set:html={"\n<p class=\"full\">Picture the model as a stack of blocks, each one attention followed by either a dense MLP or an MoE layer. Every MoE layer sends its tokens out to their experts and back with an all-to-all, so where the MoE layers sit decides how many all-to-alls a step makes, and also which layers end up balancing worst. How each block is wired then decides how much of every all-to-all can hide behind attention.</p>\n<p class=\"meta\">Where models put their MoE layers and how they connect them to attention. Dense-layer counts refer to the backbone, excluding prediction modules.</p><table class=\"glance\"><thead><tr><th>Model</th><th>Dense layers</th><th>Block wiring</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>DeepSeek-V3<sup data-k=\"layout-dsv3\"><a href=\"#ref-2412.19437\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">157</a></sup></td><td>First 3 dense, remaining 58 MoE</td><td>Attention followed by FFN</td></tr><tr><td>DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash<sup data-k=\"layout-dsv41\"><a href=\"#ref-2609.19969\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">158</a></sup></td><td>All 40 blocks use MoE</td><td>Single-Pass mHC residual connections</td></tr><tr><td>GLM-5.3<sup data-k=\"layout-glm53\"><a href=\"#ref-src-shape-glm53\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">101</a></sup></td><td>First 3 dense, remaining 75 MoE</td><td>Attention followed by FFN<sup data-k=\"tf-glm-moe-dsa\"><a href=\"#ref-src-tf-glm-moe-dsa\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">18</a></sup></td></tr><tr><td>Kimi K3<sup data-k=\"layout-k3\"><a href=\"#ref-2607.24653\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">159</a></sup></td><td>First layer dense, remaining 92 MoE</td><td>Attention followed by Stable LatentMoE; Attention Residuals</td></tr><tr><td>Llama 4 Maverick<sup data-k=\"cfg-maverick\"><a href=\"#ref-src-cfg-maverick\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">160</a></sup></td><td>Alternating dense and MoE, starting dense</td><td>MoE every second layer</td></tr><tr><td>LongCat-Flash<sup data-k=\"layout-longcat\"><a href=\"#ref-2509.01322\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">161</a></sup></td><td>Dense FFNs and MoE coexist within each macro-block</td><td>Shortcut-connected MoE overlaps with later attention and FFN work</td></tr><tr><td>MiMo-V2.6-Pro<sup data-k=\"layout-mimo26\"><a href=\"#ref-2610.11959\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">138</a></sup></td><td>First layer dense, remaining 69 MoE</td><td>Hybrid attention blocks followed by FFNs</td></tr></tbody></table>\n<p class=\"full\">The first layout decision is which layers get experts at all, since you can put an MoE in every layer, keep a few layers dense, or alternate the two. Which of these should you pick?</p>\n<h4 id=\"s-3-5-dense-layers\">Dense layers</h4>\n<p class=\"full\">A dense layer has no router and no experts, so it makes no all-to-all. Figure 3.5.1 counts the all-to-alls of three layouts side by side: MoE in every layer, as in Qwen3, gpt-oss, MiniMax M2 and Llama 4 Scout<sup data-k=\"cfg-moe-every\"><a href=\"#ref-src-cfg-moe-every\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">162</a></sup>, leading dense layers, and MoE every other layer. Leading dense layers replace only the earliest MoE layers, as in DeepSeek-V3, which keeps its first three dense<sup data-k=\"dsv3-dense\"><a href=\"#ref-2412.19437\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">163</a></sup>. Putting an MoE in every other layer halves the all-to-alls instead, as in Llama 4 Maverick, which alternates dense and MoE layers<sup data-k=\"cfg-maverick\"><a href=\"#ref-src-cfg-maverick\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">160</a></sup>. Either way, you give up some MoE capacity in exchange for fewer all-to-alls and fewer parameters.</p>\n"} />

<Viz name="dense-layer-placement" height={788} caption="Figure 3.5.1" />

<Fragment set:html={"\n\n\n<p class=\"full\">The figure&#x27;s toy scripts its routing to follow the token id less with depth, so its first layer balances slowest: leading dense layers remove its two slowest layers but cut only 4 of 16 all-to-alls, while MoE every other layer halves the all-to-alls but keeps the second slowest layer. In Ling&#x27;s 60-layer models, leading dense layers cost little: making the first 1 to 3 layers dense barely changed efficiency at matched FLOPs, an efficiency leverage close to 1<sup data-k=\"ling-el\"><a href=\"#ref-2507.17702\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">164</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">The other argument for them is balance, and it rests on less evidence. DeepSeekMoE observed that the first layer&#x27;s balance converges more slowly<sup data-k=\"dsmoe-early\"><a href=\"#ref-2401.06066\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">165</a></sup>, and Ling says leading dense layers ease early-layer imbalance<sup data-k=\"ling-early\"><a href=\"#ref-2507.17702\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">166</a></sup>, though its ablation of those layers reports only loss<sup data-k=\"ling-el\"><a href=\"#ref-2507.17702\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">164</a></sup>. Later reports pull in different directions. Some keep the first layers away from a learned router: Nucleus-Image, an image-generation MoE, diverged without its three leading dense layers<sup data-k=\"nucleus-dense\"><a href=\"#ref-2604.12163\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">167</a></sup>, and DeepSeek-V4 hashes its first 3 MoE layers<sup data-k=\"dsv4-s41\"><a href=\"#ref-2606.19348\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">53</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">Others keep routing there and treat the imbalance instead. Solar Open uses MoE in every layer and fixes its early-layer imbalance with a sequence-wise auxiliary loss<sup data-k=\"solar-early\"><a href=\"#ref-2601.07022\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">168</a></sup>. Sigma-MoE-Tiny routes each token to only 1 of 96 experts, and with an aux loss alone its lower layers collapsed: the loss took a shortcut by making the router&#x27;s probabilities uniform, while some experts still got almost no tokens. Its fix was to activate more experts per token in the first 8 layers early in training, then drop back to one<sup data-k=\"sigma-early\"><a href=\"#ref-2512.16248\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">77</a></sup>. Dense layers do not always end the problem either: Motif 3, whose first two layers are dense, saw its earliest MoE layers balance worst early in training<sup data-k=\"motif3-aux\"><a href=\"#ref-2608.09119\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">74</a></sup>, while ERNIE 5.0 found no severe imbalance in its first layer on text<sup data-k=\"ernie5-first\"><a href=\"#ref-2602.04705\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">169</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\"><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\">Megatron</a> takes the layout as a list in <a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/models/gpt/gpt_layer_specs.py#L635-L645\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-layer-freq</span></code></a>, one entry per layer, 0 for dense and 1 for MoE: <code>&quot;([0]+[1]*15)&quot;</code> gives one leading dense layer, and <code>&quot;([0,1]*8)&quot;</code> a dense-first interleave like Maverick&#x27;s. The flag also takes an integer, and that form puts an MoE at layer 0, so <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-layer-freq</span> 2</code> starts with an MoE rather than with a dense layer as Maverick does. The dense layers take their width from <code><span class=\"nw\">--ffn-hidden-size</span></code>, separately from the expert size.</p>\n<details><summary>Megatron source</summary><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/models/gpt/gpt_layer_specs.py#L635-L645\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">gpt_layer_specs.py#L635-L645</span></code></a> (integer or list pattern)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">635</span><span style=\"--i:4ch\">)</span></span></pre></details><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/models/gpt/gpt_layer_specs.py#L652-L659\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">gpt_layer_specs.py#L652-L659</span></code></a> (dense or MoE per layer)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">652</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\">layer_specs = []</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">653</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">for</span> layer_number <span class=\"hl-k\">in</span> <span class=\"hl-b\">range</span>(config.num_layers):</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">654</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">if</span> moe_layer_pattern[layer_number] == <span class=\"hl-m\">1</span>:</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">655</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\">layer_specs.append(moe_layer_spec)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">656</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">elif</span> moe_layer_pattern[layer_number] == <span class=\"hl-m\">0</span>:</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">657</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\">layer_specs.append(dense_layer_spec)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">658</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">else</span>:</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">659</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">raise</span> ValueError(<span class=\"hl-s\">f</span><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span><span class=\"hl-s\">Invalid layer pattern: </span><span class=\"hl-s\">{</span>moe_layer_pattern<span class=\"hl-s\">}</span><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span>)</span></span></pre></details></details>\n<p class=\"full watch\">The reports above saw imbalance in the early layers, and with dense layers in those places, the first MoE layers after them can still balance worst, as they did in Motif 3. Those first MoE layers are the ones to watch. Follow their aux loss with <a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/moe_logging.py#L382-L405\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-per-layer-logging</span></code></a>, whose per-layer values the launcher from <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-2-4\">2.4</a> sends to <a href=\"https://huggingface.co/docs/trackio\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\">Trackio</a>, and their MaxVio from the routing trace (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>). The dense layers have no router to watch. The <a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/moe_utils.py#L155-L172\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\">z-loss</a> measures how large the router logits grow, not how evenly tokens spread.</p><p class=\"full rec\">Use leading dense layers when you want fewer all-to-alls at little cost: one to three of them barely changed efficiency at matched FLOPs in Ling&#x27;s 60-layer models<sup data-k=\"ling-el\"><a href=\"#ref-2507.17702\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">164</a></sup>. Do not count on them to fix early-layer imbalance: neither DeepSeekMoE nor Ling shows data for it<sup class=\"fn-multi\"><a data-ks=\"dsmoe-early ling-early\" href=\"#ref-2401.06066\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">165,166</a></sup>, and Motif 3 still saw it in its first MoE layers after two dense ones<sup data-k=\"motif3-aux\"><a href=\"#ref-2608.09119\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">74</a></sup>.</p>\n<p class=\"full\">Every MoE layer you keep still sends its tokens out and back, and in an MoE block, where the MoE runs on attention&#x27;s output, the GPUs spend much of that time waiting. Could you rewire the block so that some of that traffic moves while attention computes?</p>\n<h4 id=\"s-3-5-block-wiring\">Block wiring</h4>\n<p class=\"full\">In an MoE block, each all-to-all is tied to compute on both sides. The router reads attention&#x27;s output, so dispatch cannot start until attention is done, and the next layer reads the MoE&#x27;s output, so nothing after the MoE can start until combine is done. The <a href=\"https://huggingface.co/spaces/nanotron/ultrascale-playbook?section=a3:_math_for_compute/communication_overlap\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\">Ultra-Scale Playbook</a> covers how to overlap communication with compute in general.</p>\n"} />

<Viz name="all-to-all-overlap" height={793} caption="Figure 3.5.2" />

<Fragment set:html={"\n\n\n<p class=\"full\">Rewiring the block removes one of these dependencies or both, and the communication it frees can then overlap with compute. A parallel block feeds attention and the MoE the same input, as Cohere&#x27;s North Mini Code and Moondream 3 do<sup data-k=\"parallel-block\"><a href=\"#ref-src-parallel-block\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">170</a></sup>, so dispatch runs while attention computes. Combine is harder to hide, because it can only overlap with work left in the same layer, which in <a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/models/hybrid/shortcut_block.py#L547-L593\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\">Megatron&#x27;s shortcut block</a> is attention&#x27;s output projection and the shared expert; with neither, it stays exposed. A shortcut-connected MoE hides combine too, behind the dense compute that runs beside it, as in LongCat-Flash<sup data-k=\"longcat-scmoe\"><a href=\"#ref-2509.01322\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">171</a></sup> and in Pos-2, the preferred variant of ScMoE, Cai et al.&#x27;s shortcut-connected MoE<sup data-k=\"scmoe-pos\"><a href=\"#ref-2404.05019\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">172</a></sup>. Figure 3.5.2 compares the three wirings inside one micro-batch, and in its illustrative durations the exposed all-to-all share of the layer falls from 33% in the MoE block to 7% with the style=\"--i:4ch\">)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">547</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">combined_output = <span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>._launch_combine(output, async_op=overlap)</span></span></pre></details></details>\n<p class=\"full\">The parallel block in the figure overlaps dispatch with attention while combine stays exposed, and the shortcut-connected MoE covers most of dispatch and combine with the layers around it. The price falls on the router, which now reads the stream from before the compute it overlaps and never sees that compute&#x27;s output.</p><p class=\"full\">Does the model learn worse for it? Cai et al. and the LongCat-Flash team saw it learn about as well. Cai et al. put an MoE in every block of a LLaMA2-style model, wired as Pos-1, the ScMoE variant that Megatron implements, and it averaged slightly higher on zero-shot benchmarks than a standard top-2 MoE<sup data-k=\"scmoe-t2\"><a href=\"#ref-2404.05019\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">173</a></sup>. In LongCat-Flash, the loss curves with and without the shortcut were virtually indistinguishable<sup data-k=\"longcat-shortcut\"><a href=\"#ref-2509.01322\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">174</a></sup>. AMD&#x27;s FarSkip-Collective rewires the block so that computation goes on with a residual that does not yet hold the latest communicated output. Shipped in Instella-MoE, it matched the MoE block&#x27;s benchmark average after 200B training tokens<sup class=\"fn-multi\"><a data-ks=\"instella-t13 farskip-t3\" href=\"#ref-2609.00791\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">175,176</a></sup>, although the tables of both AMD reports show it losing on MMLU and HumanEval+.</p><p class=\"full\">What you get in return is speed. ScMoE trained 1.12x to 1.14x faster on one node of 8 GPUs over NVLink, with the paper&#x27;s standard top-2 MoE as the baseline<sup data-k=\"scmoe-t2\"><a href=\"#ref-2404.05019\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">173</a></sup>, and Instella-MoE gained 12.7% in pre-training throughput<sup data-k=\"instella-tput\"><a href=\"#ref-2609.00791\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">177</a></sup>. LongCat-Flash measured the exposed communication itself: with its shortcut-connected MoE, the share of time spent in dispatch and combine that is not overlapped fell from 25.3% to 8.4%<sup data-k=\"longcat-overlap\"><a href=\"#ref-2509.01322\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">178</a></sup>, in expert-parallel groups of 32 accelerators that use pipelined <a href=\"https://huggingface.co/spaces/nanotron/ultrascale-playbook?section=gather_%26_allgather\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\">all-gather</a> and <a href=\"https://huggingface.co/spaces/nanotron/ultrascale-playbook?section=scatter_%26_reducescatter\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\">reduce-scatter</a> instead of all-to-all.</p><p class=\"full\">In Megatron, the figure&#x27;s parallel row (ScMoE&#x27;s Pos-1) is <a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/transformer_config.py#L796-L813\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-shortcut-connection</span></code></a>, which makes each MoE read the input of the attention layer just before it, so its router no longer waits for that attention. The shared expert still reads attention&#x27;s output, a detail the figure&#x27;s row does not draw, and the routed experts run on the main stream <a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/models/hybrid/shortcut_block.py#L517-L558\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\">after attention&#x27;s core and before its output projection</a>, as the row&#x27;s experts follow its attention. The rewiring alone overlaps nothing: the all-to-alls overlap only once <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-shortcut-parallel</span></code> puts them on a side CUDA stream. An optional extra norm on the merged output comes with <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-shortcut-post-norm</span></code>. All three flags are only on upstream main, and they work only in the hybrid stack, not together with <a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/training/arguments.py#L984-L990\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\">FSDP</a>, CUDA graphs, full recompute or <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-shared-expert-overlap</span></code>. Without a block change, Megatron can also hide the all-to-all behind another micro-batch (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-7-overlap\">3.7 Overlap</a>).</p><p class=\"full watch\">An exposed all-to-all makes the step slow, and a trace shows how much of it is exposed. Take an nsys timeline (<code><span class=\"nw\">--profile</span></code> with <code><span class=\"nw\">--profile-step-start</span></code> and <code><span class=\"nw\">--profile-step-end</span></code>, see <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-7-profiling\">3.7 Profiling</a>) and compare the all-to-all kernels on the side stream with the compute on the main stream: the stretches where the all-to-all runs alone are the exposed share.</p><p class=\"full rec\">Use Megatron&#x27;s shortcut block (the figure&#x27;s parallel row) when dispatch is exposed and you can move to Megatron&#x27;s hybrid stack: ScMoE&#x27;s Pos-1 design trained 1.14x faster than a standard top-2 MoE on one 8-GPU NVLink node<sup data-k=\"scmoe-t2\"><a href=\"#ref-2404.05019\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">173</a></sup>. Set both <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-shortcut-connection</span></code> and <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-shortcut-parallel</span></code>, since only the second makes the all-to-alls overlap, and leave out FSDP, CUDA graphs, full recompute and <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-shared-expert-overlap</span></code>, which do not run with it.</p>\n<h4>What to watch</h4>\n<table class=\"glance\"><thead><tr><th>Part</th><th>Symptom</th><th>Signal to watch</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>Dense layers</td><td><code>learns badly</code>: the first MoE layers balance poorly or their routing collapses. <code>blows up</code>: an image-generation MoE diverged without its leading dense layers<sup data-k=\"nucleus-dense\"><a href=\"#ref-2604.12163\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">167</a></sup>. <code>slow</code>: an MoE in every layer means more all-to-alls.</td><td>Aux loss (<code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-per-layer-logging</span></code>) and MaxVio (routing trace, <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>) in the first MoE layers after the dense block.</td></tr><tr><td>Block wiring</td><td><code>slow</code>: the all-to-all is not hidden behind compute.</td><td>How much of the all-to-all is exposed: in an nsys timeline (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-7-profiling\">3.7 Profiling</a>), compare the all-to-all kernels on the side stream with the compute on the main stream.</td></tr></tbody></table>\n<p class=\"full\">With the router, the balancer, the experts and the block layout chosen, the architecture is complete, and what remains is training it. Where should training start, which optimizer should you use, and how will you know when something goes wrong?</p>\n<h3 id=\"s-3-6\">3.6 Training: start, optimizer, stability</h3>\n"} />

<Viz name="hero-anatomy-zoom" query="&stage=7&mini=1" w={600} h={347} />

<Fragment set:html={"\n<p class=\"full\">Training an MoE adds failure modes that a dense model does not have, and the teams behind large MoEs have run into several of them. A Switch Transformer diverged in bfloat16<sup data-k=\"switch-t2\"><a href=\"#ref-2101.03961\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">40</a></sup>, and two of six baseline ST-MoE runs went unstable<sup data-k=\"stmoe-t4\"><a href=\"#ref-2202.08906\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">41</a></sup>. Routing can also collapse onto a few experts, an imbalance that feeds itself<sup data-k=\"shazeer-collapse\"><a href=\"#ref-1701.06538\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">179</a></sup>. What makes these runs fail, and how would you notice early that yours is about to?</p>\n<p class=\"meta\">Training choices reported by model authors. Initialization and optimizer entries highlight the choice relevant to this section; MTP entries describe training, which can differ from the released inference configuration.</p><table class=\"glance\"><thead><tr><th>Model</th><th>Initialization</th><th>Optimizer</th><th>MTP</th><th>Stability choices</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>DeepSeek-V3<sup data-k=\"train-dsv3\"><a href=\"#ref-2412.19437\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">163</a></sup></td><td>Random initialization</td><td>AdamW</td><td>1 MTP layer</td><td>Gradient clipping at norm 1.0; embedding, output head, MoE gating, normalization and attention kept in BF16 or FP32 during FP8 training</td></tr><tr><td>DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash<sup data-k=\"train-dsv41\"><a href=\"#ref-2609.19969\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">180</a></sup></td><td>From scratch</td><td>Muon for linear weights, AdamW for normalization and non-matrix parameters</td><td>No backbone MTP during pretraining; DSpark trained separately afterward</td><td>SwiGLU clipping at 10; modality-specific balancing</td></tr><tr><td>Kimi K3<sup data-k=\"train-k3\"><a href=\"#ref-2607.24653\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">181</a></sup></td><td>From scratch, language and vision trained jointly from the start</td><td>Muon for matrix parameters, with per-head updates for attention projections</td><td>1 MTP layer</td><td>Weight clipping, attention output in FP32, normalized LatentMoE, SiTU-GLU and quantile balancing</td></tr><tr><td>LongCat-Flash<sup data-k=\"train-longcat\"><a href=\"#ref-2509.01322\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">182</a></sup></td><td>A trained 14-layer model, stacked into 28 layers</td><td>Adam-family, with hyperparameters transferred by Adam&#x27;s scaling rules</td><td>MTP used as an auxiliary objective</td><td>Hidden-state z-loss, router-gradient monitoring, Adam epsilon of 1e-16 and deterministic computation</td></tr><tr><td>Step <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-5\">3.5</a> Flash<sup data-k=\"train-step35\"><a href=\"#ref-2602.10604\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">183</a></sup></td><td>Not reported</td><td>Muon, with its Polar Express iteration in FP16</td><td>3 MTP heads</td><td>Gradient clipping at 1.0; expert activation clipping; monitor expert norms alongside loss</td></tr></tbody></table>\n<p class=\"full\">The first decision is where training starts, from random weights or from the dense model we already trained in <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-2-4\">2.4</a>. What does starting from the dense checkpoint buy you, and what does it cost?</p>\n<h4 id=\"s-3-6-upcycling\">Upcycling</h4>\n<p class=\"full\">Upcycling starts the MoE from a trained dense checkpoint by copying its MLP into the experts. An expert as wide as the dense MLP gets a full copy, and a narrower one gets a slice: in our run, with a dense MLP 8192 wide and experts of 1024, <a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\">Megatron</a> splits the dense MLP into 8 slices and copies each slice into 8 of the 64 experts. Either way, the MoE starts where the dense run stopped.</p><p class=\"full\">Copying the weights leaves a second choice: whether the optimizer state and the learning-rate schedule carry over too. Komatsuzaki et al. continued the dense run&#x27;s schedule where it left off<sup data-k=\"sparse-upcycle-lr\"><a href=\"#ref-2212.05055\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">184</a></sup>, but reused the optimizer state only for vision models, because on language it gave no improvement<sup data-k=\"sparse-upcycle-opt\"><a href=\"#ref-2212.05055\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">185</a></sup>. Megatron&#x27;s upcycling step saves the MoE at iteration 1 without optimizer state (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/training/training.py#L3069-L3083\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">training.py#L3069-L3083</span></code></a>), so the MoE run starts with a fresh optimizer and a new schedule.</p><p class=\"full\">The head start is not free: it is the compute already spent on the dense run, and a run from scratch eventually catches up with the upcycled run. How soon varies a lot. In OLMoE, a run from scratch caught up after 500B tokens, 25% of the 2T tokens the dense model had been trained on<sup data-k=\"olmoe-upcycle\"><a href=\"#ref-2409.02060\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">186</a></sup>, while in Komatsuzaki et al.&#x27;s sparse upcycling runs it took 120% of the dense checkpoint&#x27;s compute<sup data-k=\"sparse-upcycle\"><a href=\"#ref-2212.05055\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">187</a></sup>. The figure below redraws OLMoE&#x27;s comparison from its public training logs: an MoE upcycled from the dense OLMo-1B after 2T tokens of training, against the same MoE trained from scratch, each for 610B tokens.</p>\n"} />

<Viz name="olmoe-upcycling" height={605} caption="Figure 3.6.1" />

<Fragment set:html={"\n\n\n\n<p class=\"full\">In the figure, the upcycled run starts ahead on all four metrics, and the run from scratch narrows the gap only gradually. Its HellaSwag and MMLU accuracies come level with the upcycled run&#x27;s around 500B tokens, where the authors place the catch-up, while its training and validation losses stay slightly higher until about 600B tokens.</p><p class=\"full\">A trained MoE can grow the same way, by copying its own experts instead of a dense MLP. The figure below turns an 8-expert MoE into a 16-expert one and shows what the copies do to routing.</p>"} />

<Viz name="expert-upcycling" height={746} caption="Figure 3.6.2" />

<Fragment set:html={"<p class=\"full\">With top-2 routing and each copy inheriting its original&#x27;s router row, the figure&#x27;s exact copies tie: every token picks an expert and its copy at equal weight, so it runs one distinct expert instead of two. Perturbing the copy gives the router a gradient between the pair, and random new experts avoid the tie but put routing weight on untrained experts. All three have been used to grow MoEs: Expert Upcycling copies each expert with its router row, going from 32 to 64 experts<sup data-k=\"expup-copy\"><a href=\"#ref-src-expup-copy\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">188</a></sup>, K-EXAONE 2.0 adds a random rotation to each copy, going from 128 to 256<sup data-k=\"kexaone-grow\"><a href=\"#ref-src-kexaone-grow\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">189</a></sup>, Expert Upcycling also tested Drop-Upcycling, which re-initializes part of each copy&#x27;s weights, half in the setting its authors found best<sup class=\"fn-multi\"><a data-ks=\"expup-drop dropup-r\" href=\"#ref-src-expup-drop\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">190,191</a></sup>, and EMO draws the new experts and router rows from a Gaussian<sup data-k=\"emo-init\"><a href=\"#ref-src-emo-init\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">192</a></sup>. In Megatron, upcycling from a dense checkpoint is <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-use-upcycling</span></code> with <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-upcycling-granularity</span></code>, and at the pin it needs the two local fixes described in <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-2-5\">2.5</a>.</p>\n<details><summary>Megatron source</summary><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/training/training.py#L3043-L3083\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">def</span> <span class=\"hl-f\">load_checkpoint</span>(</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">2633</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">ddp_model,</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">2634</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">optimizer,</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">2635</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">opt_param_scheduler,</span></span></pre></details><p>Then build the dense model from the arguments with <code>num_experts</code> unset, rather than from the config container that <code>pretrain_gpt.py</code> made once at start-up, so that the dense checkpoint loads into a dense model:</p><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/training/training.py#L2917-L2925\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">training.py#L2917-L2925</span></code></a> (the model built from the config container)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">2917</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">def</span> <span class=\"hl-f\">_build_model_wrapper</span>(wrap_with_ddp: <span class=\"hl-b\">bool</span>):</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">2918</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">if</span> cfg_container <span class=\"hl-k\">is</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">not</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">None</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">and</span> <span class=\"hl-b\">getattr</span>(cfg_container, <span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span><span class=\"hl-s\">model</span><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span>, <span class=\"hl-k\">None</span>) <span class=\"hl-k\">is</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">not</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">None</span>:</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">2919</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">from</span> megatron.training.utils <span class=\"hl-k\">import</span> start_memory_history_recording</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">2920</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">2921</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\">start_memory_history_recording(cfg_container.profiling)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">2922</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">2923</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\">cfg = cfg_container</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">2924</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\">model_config = cfg.model</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">2925</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\">builder_cls = model_config.get_builder_cls()</span></span></pre></details></details>\n<p class=\"full watch\">An upcycle that went wrong loses its head start, so compare its loss with the dense run continued for the same tokens. Since the experts start as identical copies, also watch MaxVio per layer while they drift apart, computed offline from the routing trace (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>).</p><p class=\"full rec\">Use it when a dense checkpoint exists and the MoE run is short, since a run from scratch caught up after 25% of the dense model&#x27;s training tokens in OLMoE and 120% of its compute in Komatsuzaki et al.&#x27;s runs<sup class=\"fn-multi\"><a data-ks=\"olmoe-upcycle sparse-upcycle\" href=\"#ref-2409.02060\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">186,187</a></sup>. Upcycling is an initialization, so set it on the first run only, and at the pin apply the two local fixes from <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-2-5\">2.5</a> first.</p>\n<p class=\"full\">Whether you upcycle or start from random weights only decides where the weights begin; from the first step on, the optimizer decides where they go. Each expert, though, sees only the tokens routed to it, so should your optimizer move an expert that got a handful of tokens as far as a busy one?</p>\n<h4 id=\"s-3-6-optimizer\">Optimizer</h4>\n<p class=\"full\">AdamW, the optimizer DeepSeek-V3 trained with<sup data-k=\"dsv3-adamw\"><a href=\"#ref-2412.19437\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">163</a></sup>, scales each weight&#x27;s update by that weight&#x27;s own gradient history, so how far an expert moves follows the gradients its tokens produced. Muon, an optimizer for weight matrices used by several recent MoEs, sizes the step another way. It orthogonalizes each update, pushing its nonzero singular values toward one, and then multiplies it by a scale set by the matrix&#x27;s shape alone, so the size of the step no longer depends on how large the gradient behind it is.</p><p class=\"full\">Megatron orthogonalizes each expert&#x27;s matrix on its own (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/extensions/transformer_engine.py#L2665-L2668\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">transformer_engine.py#L2665-L2668</span></code></a>, <a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/transformer_config.py#L956\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code>transformer_config.py#L956</code></a>, <a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/optimizer/emerging_optimizers.py#L147-L149\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">emerging_optimizers.py#L147-L149</span></code></a>), as DeepSeek-V4 does<sup data-k=\"dsv4-muon\"><a href=\"#ref-2606.19348\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">193</a></sup>, so an expert the router rarely picks can move about as far as the busiest one, only in its own direction, and it keeps moving even in a step where it got no tokens, along its momentum. The figure below shows what that means over 49 toy experts, plotting update size against tokens per step for AdamW, for Muon applied to each expert separately, and for Muon over all experts as one matrix.</p>\n"} />

<Viz name="muon-expert-steps" height={606} caption="Figure 3.6.3" />

<Fragment set:html={"\n\n\n\n<p class=\"full\">As an expert&#x27;s tokens fall in the toy, its update shrinks under AdamW as the gradient turns to noise and, under Muon over all experts as one matrix, with the size of its gradient, while per-expert Muon keeps the busiest expert&#x27;s step down to a few tokens per step. Below that, the per-expert step shrinks too: each token adds a single direction to the gradient, and with so few tokens even the momentum of recent steps no longer fills the expert&#x27;s matrix.</p><p class=\"full\">The figure shows how Muon moves each expert; whether that trains a better MoE has been measured on whole models. Moonlight, an MoE with 3B active and 16B total parameters, performed significantly better with Muon than its twin trained with AdamW under the same settings, compared at about 1.2T tokens<sup data-k=\"moonlight-t4\"><a href=\"#ref-2502.16982\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">194</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">Once you are on Muon, one choice remains that adopters make differently: which optimizer updates the router. DeepSeek-V4 keeps it on Muon<sup data-k=\"dsv4-muon\"><a href=\"#ref-2606.19348\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">193</a></sup>, while Qwen3.8-Next moved it to AdamW after Muon destabilized the router early in training<sup data-k=\"qwen38-router\"><a href=\"#ref-2608.30320\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">195</a></sup>. Lau and Su compared router optimizers on two small MoEs, one shaped like OLMoE-1B-7B and a downsized gpt-oss: with Muon on the hidden matrices, AdamW on the router ended slightly worse than row-normalized or left-spectral router updates<sup data-k=\"symcompat-router\"><a href=\"#ref-2605.18106\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">196</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">In Megatron, Muon is <code><span class=\"nw\">--optimizer</span> muon</code>, and to put the router on Adam instead, add <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-router-skip-muon</span></code>, available from <a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/releases/tag/core_v0.20.0\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\">Megatron Core v0.20</a> on.</p>\n<details><summary>Megatron source</summary><details class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\">), (</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1795</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span><span class=\"hl-s\">The LayerWise distributed optimizer does not support --moe-single-grouped-weight: </span><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1796</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span><span class=\"hl-s\">Muon semantics for a single grouped [E, N, K] expert weight are not defined. </span><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1797</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span><span class=\"hl-s\">Disable --moe-single-grouped-weight or use Adam/DistributedOptimizer.</span><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1798</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\">)</span></span></pre></details></details>\n<p class=\"full watch\">Under Muon, experts can collapse<sup data-k=\"laguna-collapse\"><a href=\"#ref-2605.27605\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">197</a></sup>, and too large an update scale can make the run diverge<sup data-k=\"mellum2-muon\"><a href=\"#ref-2605.31268\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">198</a></sup>. Track what each expert receives and produces: tokens per expert per step, offline from the routing trace (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>), and, per layer, the largest and the smallest expert output norm, each divided by the layer&#x27;s median, from a forward hook of ours (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>).</p><p class=\"full rec\">Use Muon when you can afford to tune it. Start at <code><span class=\"nw\">--muon-extra-scale-factor</span> 0.2</code>, since Megatron&#x27;s default of 1.0 diverged on a dense 7B in Mellum2&#x27;s tests<sup data-k=\"mellum2-muon\"><a href=\"#ref-2605.31268\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">198</a></sup>, and choose the router&#x27;s optimizer with <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-router-skip-muon</span></code>, a call adopters make both ways. It does not run with Megatron-FSDP or Torch-FSDP2.</p>\n<p class=\"full\">However the optimizer moves the weights, each step still learns only from predicting the next token. Could you get more signal out of each step by predicting further ahead, and what would that cost in step time?</p>\n<h4 id=\"s-3-6-mtp\">MTP</h4>\n<p class=\"full\">Multi-token prediction adds one transformer layer per extra token to predict. That layer takes the main model&#x27;s hidden state together with the next token&#x27;s embedding, and predicts the token after next through the shared output head. DeepSeek-V3 found it better on most benchmarks at both of its test scales, and the extra layer can be discarded at inference<sup data-k=\"dsv3-mtp\"><a href=\"#ref-2412.19437\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">199</a></sup>. The figure below follows one position of the sequence through that wiring.</p>\n"} />

<Viz name="mtp-layer" height={780} caption="Figure 3.6.4" />

<Fragment set:html={"\n\n\n\n<p class=\"full\">On their way in, the two inputs each go through their own RMSNorm, and the MTP layer concatenates them and projects the result from twice the hidden size back to it before its transformer block. Both predictions come out of the same output head, and the MTP loss is weighted and added to the main loss<sup data-k=\"dsv3-mtp\"><a href=\"#ref-2412.19437\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">199</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">The extra layer costs step time, and under <a href=\"https://huggingface.co/spaces/nanotron/ultrascale-playbook?section=pipeline_parallelism\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\">pipeline parallelism</a> that cost lands in one place: unless the layout moves it, Megatron puts the MTP layer on the last stage (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/multi_token_prediction.py#L886-L905\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">multi_token_prediction.py#L886-L905</span></code></a>), which already holds the output head the MTP layer reuses. On our 256-node runs, early steps took about 37% longer with one MTP layer than without, and spreading the model over 8 pipeline stages did not close that gap. Mellum2 reports 7% extra training time for a 14B MoE<sup data-k=\"mellum2-mtp\"><a href=\"#ref-2605.31268\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">200</a></sup>.</p>\n<details><summary>Megatron source</summary><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/multi_token_prediction.py#L1709-L1714\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">multi_token_prediction.py#L1709-L1714</span></code></a> (hidden plus next-token embedding)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1709</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\">hidden_states = apply_module(<span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.hnorm)(hidden_states)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1710</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\">hidden_states = make_viewless_tensor(</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1711</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">inp=hidden_states, requires_grad=<span class=\"hl-k\">True</span>, keep_graph=<span class=\"hl-k\">True</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1712</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\">)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1713</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\">hidden_states = torch.cat((decoder_input, hidden_states), -<span class=\"hl-m\">1</span>)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1714</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\">hidden_states, _ = <span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.eh_proj(hidden_states)</span></span></pre></details><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/multi_token_prediction.py#L1276-L1276\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">multi_token_prediction.py#L1276-L1276</span></code></a> (loss times factor over depth)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1276</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\">mtp_loss_scale = config.mtp_loss_scaling_factor / config.mtp_num_layers</span></span></pre></details><details class=\"src\"><summary><a class=\"ln\">932</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-c\"># without custom PP layout, we only support put all of MTP layers on the last pipeline stage</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">933</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">if</span> mtp_num_layers <span class=\"hl-k\">is</span> <span <span class=\"hl-m\">1</span></span></span></pre></details><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/multi_token_prediction.py#L2598-L2599\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">multi_token_prediction.py#L2598-L2599</span></code></a> (detach cuts trunk gradient)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">2598</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">if</span> <span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.config.mtp_detach_heads:</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">2599</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">hidden_states = hidden_states.detach()</span></span></pre></details></details>\n<p class=\"full\">Much of that gap may come from where the layer sits. A pipeline step waits for its slowest stage, so a layer added to one stage slows the step by its share of that stage&#x27;s work, not by its much smaller share of the whole model. Ling 2.0&#x27;s team ran into the same imbalance: they measured an MTP layer at about 1.7 times the compute of a standard MoE layer, and repartitioning the pipeline around it gained 40% end to end<sup data-k=\"ling2-mtp\"><a href=\"#ref-2510.22115\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">201</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">Megatron turns MTP on with <code><span class=\"nw\">--mtp-num-layers</span></code>, the number of extra depths to add, and <code><span class=\"nw\">--mtp-loss-scaling-factor</span></code> weights the MTP loss: DeepSeek-V3 used 0.3 for its first 10T tokens and 0.1 after<sup data-k=\"dsv3-mtp-lambda\"><a href=\"#ref-2412.19437\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">163</a></sup>, and Mellum2 used 0.1<sup data-k=\"mellum2-mtp\"><a href=\"#ref-2605.31268\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">200</a></sup>. To choose which stage holds the MTP layer, written <code>m</code>, use <code><span class=\"nw\">--pipeline-model-parallel-layout</span></code>. If you want the MTP layer only as a draft model for speculative decoding, <code><span class=\"nw\">--mtp-detach-heads</span></code> detaches its inputs, so the MTP loss trains the MTP layer and leaves the main model alone.</p><p class=\"full watch\">Megatron logs an <code>mtp_1 loss</code> next to the lm loss whenever MTP is on, while the MTP layer&#x27;s cost shows elsewhere, in step time and in the memory of the stage that holds it. To find the slow stage, log forward and backward compute time per pipeline rank with <code><span class=\"nw\">--timing-log-level</span> 2 <span class=\"nw\">--timing-log-option</span> all</code> (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>); the stage holding the MTP layer should show up as the slowest rank.</p><p class=\"full rec\">Use it when the denser training signal DeepSeek-V3 reports<sup data-k=\"dsv3-mtp\"><a href=\"#ref-2412.19437\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">199</a></sup> is worth the step time. Under pipeline parallelism, place the MTP layer with <code><span class=\"nw\">--pipeline-model-parallel-layout</span></code>, or the last stage carries it.</p>\n<p class=\"full\">Each part so far has told you what to watch for its own choice. Some failures, though, belong to no single choice and can start anywhere in the model, so what should every run have in place to catch them?</p>\n<h4 id=\"s-3-6-stability\">Stability</h4>\n<p class=\"full\">An MoE run can fail loudly or quietly. A loud failure shows in the loss, which spikes or turns into NaN, while a quiet one, such as routing collapse<sup data-k=\"lossfree-f2\"><a href=\"#ref-2408.15664\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">76</a></sup> or a rogue expert<sup data-k=\"step35-f4\"><a href=\"#ref-2602.10604\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">15</a></sup>, can leave the loss flat.</p><p class=\"full\">The loudest failure is also the easiest to catch, since Megatron checks every step&#x27;s loss and gradients for a NaN by default (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-0\">3.0</a>). What you want to know next is whether to blame the hardware, so when the check fires, Megatron reruns the step (<code><span class=\"nw\">--rerun-mode</span> validate_results</code>, also on by default). The first rerun, on the same GPU, tells a transient error from one that repeats. If the NaN repeats, Megatron exits with code 16 so that the job restarts from a checkpoint on another GPU, where the second rerun tells a faulty GPU from a NaN that the data or the model really produced.</p><p class=\"full\">Bad hardware and bad batches do not always end in a NaN, because they can also return a finite loss or gradient with a far too large exponent. Megatron catches those with <code><span class=\"nw\">--check-for-spiky-loss</span></code> and <code><span class=\"nw\">--check-for-large-grads</span></code>: each flags a value 10x the largest of the first 100 it has seen, reruns the step once on the same GPU, logs whether the value came back, and keeps training.</p><p class=\"full\">All of these checks react to a step that has already gone wrong. To stop the loss from breaking in the first place, <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-1\">3.1</a> already protects the router, with fp32 math and a z-loss that holds its logits down, and recent large MoEs add guards of their own. Some cap a value before it can grow: DeepSeek-V3 and Step <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-5\">3.5</a> Flash clip the gradient norm<sup class=\"fn-multi\"><a data-ks=\"train-dsv3 train-step35\" href=\"#ref-2412.19437\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">163,183</a></sup>, DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash clamps the experts&#x27; SwiGLU activations<sup data-k=\"train-dsv41\"><a href=\"#ref-2609.19969\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">180</a></sup>, and Step <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-5\">3.5</a> Flash clips expert activations as well<sup data-k=\"train-step35\"><a href=\"#ref-2602.10604\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">183</a></sup>. Others keep the most sensitive operations in higher precision: DeepSeek-V3 kept its embedding, output head, MoE gating, normalization and attention in BF16 or FP32 during FP8 training<sup data-k=\"train-dsv3\"><a href=\"#ref-2412.19437\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">163</a></sup>, and Kimi K3 keeps the attention output in FP32<sup data-k=\"train-k3\"><a href=\"#ref-2607.24653\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">181</a></sup>. LongCat-Flash carries the z-loss idea over to the hidden states<sup data-k=\"train-longcat\"><a href=\"#ref-2509.01322\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">182</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">The quiet failures may not show in the loss at all, so you have to look for them elsewhere. The figure below simulates three of them next to a healthy run: routing collapse, a dead expert and a rogue expert.</p>\n"} />

<Viz name="moe-failure-signals" height={718} caption="Figure 3.6.5" />

<Fragment set:html={"\n\n\n\n<p class=\"full\">Since the simulation keeps the loss flat through all three failures, only the other signals tell them apart from the healthy run. Routing collapse drives MaxVio up. A dead expert pulls the smallest expert output norm far below the layer&#x27;s median, and a rogue expert pushes the largest far above it, from about 14 to about 5,700 times the median as in Step <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-5\">3.5</a> Flash&#x27;s layer 45<sup data-k=\"step35-f4\"><a href=\"#ref-2602.10604\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">15</a></sup>. The loss alarm, 0.1 above the lowest loss so far, borrows the margin of Qwen3.8-Next&#x27;s spike criterion<sup data-k=\"qwen38-spike\"><a href=\"#ref-2608.30320\" class=\"code\" style=\"--i:12ch\"><span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.continue_requested = <span class=\"hl-k\">True</span></span></span><span class=\"cm\">... 13 more lines</span>\n</pre></details></details>\n<p class=\"full watch\">For spikes, watch the router&#x27;s logits through the z-loss (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-1\">3.1</a>). The failures that leave the loss flat need signals of their own: for routing collapse, MaxVio per layer, computed offline from the routing trace (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>), next to the load-balancing loss, and for dead and rogue experts, the largest and the smallest expert output norm in each layer over the layer&#x27;s median, from a hook of ours (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>).</p><p class=\"full rec\">Use it always: keep the default NaN check and <code><span class=\"nw\">--rerun-mode</span> validate_results</code>.</p>\n<h4>What to watch</h4>\n<table class=\"glance\"><thead><tr><th>Part</th><th>Symptom</th><th>Signal to watch</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>Upcycling</td><td><code>learns badly</code>: the loss falls slowly at first.</td><td>Loss against the dense run continued for the same tokens. While the copied experts drift apart, also MaxVio per layer (routing trace, <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>).</td></tr><tr><td>Optimizer</td><td><code>learns badly</code>: experts collapse. <code>blows up</code>: when orthogonalization runs in bf16, or when the update scale is too large.</td><td>Tokens per expert per step (routing trace, <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>). Per layer, the largest and smallest expert output norm, each over the median (forward hook, <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>).</td></tr><tr><td>MTP</td><td><code>slow</code>: one pipeline stage takes longer than the others. <code>OOM</code>: MTP with 53 layers on 4 pipeline stages ran out of memory.</td><td><code>mtp_1 loss</code> alongside LM loss; step time and peak memory; forward and backward compute time per pipeline rank (<code><span class=\"nw\">--timing-log-level</span> 2 <span class=\"nw\">--timing-log-option</span> all</code>, <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>), especially the stage holding MTP<sup data-k=\"ling2-mtp\"><a href=\"#ref-2510.22115\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">201</a></sup>.</td></tr><tr><td>Stability</td><td><code>blows up</code>: NaNs and loss spikes. <code>learns badly</code>: routing collapse, dead experts or rogue experts.</td><td>The router z-loss (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-1\">3.1</a>), for the router logits ST-MoE traced its instabilities to<sup data-k=\"stmoe-s34\"><a href=\"#ref-2202.08906\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">38</a></sup>. Per layer, MaxVio (routing trace) and the largest and smallest expert output norm over the median (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>).</td></tr></tbody></table>\n<p class=\"full\">A run that trains cleanly can still spend a large share of each step waiting on communication rather than computing. Where does the time in an MoE step go, and how much of it can you get back?</p>\n<h3 id=\"s-3-7\">3.7 Systems: where the time goes and how to get it back</h3>\n"} />

<Viz name="hero-anatomy-zoom" query="&stage=8&mini=1" w={600} h={347} />

<Fragment set:html={"\n<p class=\"full\">Every MoE layer sends its tokens to their experts and brings them back, and on a real run that traffic can take much of the step. In the one step we traced on one node, half the time was exposed all-to-all, while the router was still unbalanced. The figure below zooms in on those two transfers, dispatch out to the experts and combine back, which run over NVLink inside a node and over the fabric across nodes.</p><p class=\"full\">You can place the experts so that less traffic crosses the slower network between nodes, and hide the traffic that remains behind other compute. You can keep the memory that costs in check, cap how many nodes each token reaches, and trade dropped tokens or precision for speed. Each part comes from its own setup, so do not add their gains together; test them as alternatives, one at a time.</p>\n<p class=\"meta\">Systems choices documented in model reports. Parallelism and overlap describe training; post-training quantization is labeled separately. These are model-specific implementations, not interchangeable Megatron flag settings.</p><table class=\"glance\"><thead><tr><th>Model</th><th>Interconnect and overlap</th><th>Memory and precision</th><th>Routing constraints</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>DeepSeek-V3<sup data-k=\"systems-dsv3\"><a href=\"#ref-2412.19437\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">203</a></sup></td><td>EP64 across 8 nodes; DualPipe overlaps computation and communication</td><td>FP8 <a href=\"https://huggingface.co/spaces/nanotron/ultrascale-playbook?section=mixed_precision_training\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\">mixed-precision</a> training; recompute RMSNorm, MLA up-projections and SwiGLU outputs</td><td>At most 4 target nodes per token; no token dropping</td></tr><tr><td>DeepSeek-V4<sup data-k=\"systems-dsv4\"><a href=\"#ref-2606.19348\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">204</a></sup></td><td>Fused MoE pipeline schedules expert groups in waves to overlap dispatch, GEMMs and combine</td><td>Tensor-level checkpointing and selective recomputation</td><td>Removes V3&#x27;s target-node limit</td></tr><tr><td>Kimi K3<sup class=\"fn-multi\"><a data-ks=\"systems-k3 k3-qat\" href=\"#ref-2607.24653\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">205,21</a></sup></td><td>Optimizer fetches only locally owned parameter shards; communication pipelined with computation</td><td>Avoids a full-parameter gather buffer; SFT/RL use MXFP4 expert weights and MXFP8 activations</td><td>One expert group in the released config<sup data-k=\"hub-kimi-k3-route\"><a href=\"#ref-src-hub-kimi-k3-route\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">206</a></sup></td></tr><tr><td>LongCat-Flash<sup class=\"fn-multi\"><a data-ks=\"longcat-overlap systems-longcat\" href=\"#ref-2509.01322\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">178,207</a></sup></td><td>EP groups of 32 accelerators; pipelined all-gather/reduce-scatter and shortcut-connected MoE</td><td>BF16 training with deterministic kernels</td><td>Identity experts require no expert communication</td></tr></tbody></table>\n<p class=\"full\">Before reaching for any of these levers, you need to see where your own step spends its time, since each kind of waste calls for a different fix. What does one profiled step on a single node show?</p>\n<h4 id=\"s-3-7-profiling\">Profiling</h4>\n<p class=\"full\"><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\">Megatron</a> can record a trace of any step for you. With <code><span class=\"nw\">--profile</span></code> alone it records an nsys trace, and <code><span class=\"nw\">--use-pytorch-profiler</span></code> switches it to a torch profiler trace (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/training/argument_utils.py#L706-L709\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">argument_utils.py#L706-L709</span></code></a>). In either case, <code><span class=\"nw\">--profile-step-start</span></code>, <code><span class=\"nw\">--profile-step-end</span></code> and <code><span class=\"nw\">--profile-ranks</span></code> pick which steps and ranks to trace. The step we traced comes from a 7B-A1B MoE, with 7B parameters in total and 1B active per token. Its 16 layers hold 64 experts each with top-8 routing, and it trained on one node of 8 H100s. Tracing it cost one short job, which traced iteration 4 and had moved past it within about six minutes of launch. The figures below break that step down and annotate its trace.</p>\n"} />

<Viz name="step-breakdown" height={397} caption="Figure 3.7.1" />

<Viz name="annotated-trace" height={655} caption="Figure 3.7.2" />

<Fragment set:html={"\n\n\n\n<p class=\"full\">The all-to-all took half of that step, but how much of that half was the network actually moving data? When one GPU&#x27;s experts get more tokens than the rest, that GPU finishes its expert compute late, and NVIDIA&#x27;s Megatron Core team reports that the other ranks then wait for it at synchronization points<sup data-k=\"mcore-s437\"><a href=\"#ref-2603.07685\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">208</a></sup>. In an MoE layer the all-to-all can be one of those points, so part of its time in a trace can be waiting rather than moving data. Our step had a load-balancing loss of <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-2\">3.2</a>, where 1 is balanced, so its router was still unbalanced. A separate run of the same config, with routing forced even, ran that same iteration 40% faster.</p>\n<details><summary>Megatron source</summary><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/training/training.py#L4955-L4982\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">training.py#L4955-L4982</span></code></a> (torch profiler window per rank)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">4955</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">if</span> (</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">4956</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">cfg.profiling.use_pytorch_profiler</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">4957</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">and</span> (<span class=\"hl-b\">len</span>(cfg.profiling.profile_ranks) == <span class=\"hl-m\">0</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">or</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">4958</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:9ch\">torch.distributed.get_rank() <span class=\"hl-k\">in</span> cfg.profiling.profile_ranks)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">4959</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\">):</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">4960</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">if</span> class=\"nw\">training.py#L5041-L5053</span></code></a> (nsys start at step)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">5041</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span cfg.profiling.profile_step_start <span class=\"hl-k\">and</span> cfg.profiling.nvtx_ranges:</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">5046</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\">configure_nvtx_profiling(<span class=\"hl-k\">True</span>)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">5047</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">5050</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\">torch.cuda.check_error(torch.cuda.cudart().cudaProfilerStart())</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">5051</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">if</span> cfg.profiling.record_shapes:</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">5052</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:12ch\">nsys_nvtx_context = torch.autograd.profiler.emit_nvtx(record_shapes=cfg.profiling.record_shapes)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">5053</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:12ch\">nsys_nvtx_context.<span class=\"hl-f\">__enter__</span>()</span></span></pre></details></details>\n<p class=\"full\">On your own run, start with the balance signals of <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-2\">3.2</a>. MaxVio, computed from the token counts that <code><span class=\"nw\">--save-tokens-per-expert-interval</span></code> writes, shows how uneven the load is across experts. Summing those counts over each GPU&#x27;s experts shows which rank receives the most tokens, and so which rank the others most likely wait for. To see how much of the all-to-all that imbalance costs, re-trace with <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-router-force-load-balancing</span></code>, which swaps the router&#x27;s logits for random ones so that every expert gets about the same load (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/router.py#L1185-L1187\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">router.py#L1185-L1187</span></code></a>). If the all-to-all shrinks under forced balance, imbalance is costing you time that better balancing (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-3\">3.3</a>) can win back, and what remains is mostly the transfer, which the rest of this section works on.</p><p class=\"full\">Traces also show which MoE kernels are worth fusing. Before dispatch, Megatron reorders the tokens so that each expert&#x27;s tokens sit together (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/token_dispatcher.py#L668-L682\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">token_dispatcher.py#L668-L682</span></code></a>), and after combine it puts them back in order (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/token_dispatcher.py#L893-L900\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">#L893-L900</span></code></a>). With <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-permute-fusion</span></code>, the permute and copy kernels that do this fall from 11.45% to 7.14% of the step, and step time drops <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-3\">3.3</a>%.</p><p class=\"full watch\">Judge each change by step time or TFLOP/s. A trace then tells you why the number moved: read the share of the step that the all-to-all leaves exposed.</p><p class=\"full rec\">Use it once for every new config: trace one step at the node count you will run. If the all-to-all takes a large share of it, check MaxVio and re-trace with <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-router-force-load-balancing</span></code>: fix balancing first (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-3\">3.3</a>) if the all-to-all shrinks, and work on Interconnect and Overlap below if it does not. If permute and copy kernels take a large share, try <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-permute-fusion</span></code>.</p>\n<p class=\"full\">The step we traced kept its all-to-all inside one node, on NVLink, and a larger run cannot always do that. What happens to the step when an expert-parallel group spans two nodes and its all-to-all has to cross the network between them?</p>\n<h4 id=\"s-3-7-interconnect\">Interconnect</h4>\n<p class=\"full\">Between nodes, the all-to-all leaves NVLink for the cluster&#x27;s network fabric, InfiniBand or AWS&#x27;s EFA for instance, so the first thing to check is that <a href=\"https://huggingface.co/spaces/nanotron/ultrascale-playbook?section=nccl\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\">NCCL</a> uses that fabric at all. It can fall back to plain TCP sockets even when the fabric is requested: two of our runs set <code>FI_PROVIDER=efa</code> and named an OFI plugin, NCCL still logged &quot;Using network Socket&quot;, and they ran about 25x slower than our EP16 run on EFA. The next figure shows the settings we tried, in order.</p>"} />

<Viz name="efa-settings" height={438} caption="Figure 3.7.3" />

<Fragment set:html={"<p class=\"full\">In the figure, NCCL reached EFA only once the host&#x27;s RDMA libraries came first on the library path; naming the plugin, by path or by name, left it on sockets. With the fabric working, what is left to choose is the expert-parallel size, <code><span class=\"nw\">--expert-model-parallel-size</span></code>, which decides how far the all-to-all has to travel. With expert tensor parallelism at 1 and the default rank order, Megatron forms each EP group from consecutive ranks (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/parallel_state.py#L921-L929\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">parallel_state.py#L921-L929</span></code></a>). An EP size that divides the number of GPUs in a node then keeps the all-to-all inside that node, on NVLink, and a larger one spreads it over several nodes. Our nodes hold eight H100s each, so the figure below compares EP8 in one node with EP16 across two.</p>\n"} />

<Viz name="ep-placement" height={521} caption="Figure 3.7.4" />

<Fragment set:html={"\n\n\n\n<p class=\"full\">Spread over two nodes, EP16 ran 59% below EP8 in one node, even with EFA working, although the two runs also differ in their nodes and in how many micro-batches each step accumulates.</p>\n<details><summary>Megatron source</summary><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/parallel_state.py#L920-L930\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">parallel_state.py#L920-L930</span></code></a> (expert grid, EP after TP)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">920</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\">expert_order = _inject_gtp_remat_axis(order, after=<span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span><span class=\"hl-s\">ep</span><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span>)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">921</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\">expert_decoder_rank_generator = RankGenerator(</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">922</span><span style=\"--i:4ch\">cp=<span class=\"hl-m\">1</span>,</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">927</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">order=expert_order,</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">928</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">rank_offset=rank_offset,</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">929</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">gtp_remat=expert_gtp_remat_size,</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">930</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\">)</span></span></pre></details><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/parallel_state.py#L1419-L1428\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">parallel_state.py#L1419-L1428</span></code></a> (consecutive ranks form EP)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1419</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">for</span> ranks <span class=\"hl-k\">in</span> expert_decoder_rank_generator.get_ranks(<span class=\"hl-s\">&#x27;</span><span class=\"hl-s\">ep</span><span class=\"hl-s\">&#x27;</span>):</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1420</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">group = create_group(</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1421</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\">ranks,</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1422</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\">timeout=timeout,</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1423</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\">pg_options=get_nccl_options(<span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span><span class=\"hl-s\">ep</span><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span>, nccl_comm_cfgs),</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1424</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\">group_desc=<span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span><span class=\"hl-s\">EXPERT_MODEL_PARALLEL_GROUP</span><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span>,</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1425</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span
