class=\"ln\">1427</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\">_EXPERT_MODEL_PARALLEL_GROUP = group</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1428</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\">_EXPERT_MODEL_PARALLEL_RANKS = ranks</span></span></pre></details></details>\n<p class=\"full watch\">A fallback to sockets slowed our two-node run by the factor above, so check the startup log first. With <code>NCCL_DEBUG=INFO</code>, NCCL prints &quot;Using network&quot; and the transport it picked. You want your fabric&#x27;s transport there, such as Libfabric for the OFI plugin on EFA, while &quot;Using network Socket&quot; means the fallback. Once the fabric is confirmed, compare alltoall bus bandwidth inside and across nodes from <a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/nccl-tests\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\">nccl-tests</a> (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-2-1\">2.1</a>). Then measure TFLOP/s per GPU at an EP size that fits in one node and at one that spans two.</p><p class=\"full rec\">Use <a href=\"https://huggingface.co/spaces/nanotron/ultrascale-playbook?section=expert_parallelism\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\">expert parallelism</a> inside a node when the model fits that way: start there, add nodes as <a href=\"https://huggingface.co/spaces/nanotron/ultrascale-playbook?section=data_parallelism\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\">data-parallel</a> replicas, and time both placements at your node count before you commit.</p>\n<p class=\"full\">Even on a working interconnect, the tokens that cross it still sit on the critical path while the GPUs wait. Is there other work the GPUs could do while the tokens are in flight?</p>\n<h4 id=\"s-3-7-overlap\">Overlap</h4>\n<p class=\"full\">We&#x27;ve already seen in <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-5-block-wiring\">3.5 Block wiring</a> how changing the architecture hides the all-to-all inside one micro-batch. A parallel block can run dispatch while attention computes, and a shortcut-connected MoE can also hide combine behind the dense compute that runs beside it. Rewiring changes what the router sees, however, and you may not want to touch an architecture you have already settled on. Without rewiring, the exposed all-to-all is hard to hide inside one micro-batch. A training step, however, runs many micro-batches, and the work of one does not wait on another until their gradients are summed.</p><p class=\"full\">Megatron borrows that work: <code><span class=\"nw\">--overlap-moe-expert-parallel-comm</span></code> runs one micro-batch&#x27;s forward beside another&#x27;s backward, so the all-to-all of one hides behind the compute of the other (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/model_parallel_config.py#L349-L352\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">model_parallel_config.py#L349-L352</span></code></a>). Pipeline schedules also pair one micro-batch&#x27;s forward with another&#x27;s backward, as the <a href=\"https://huggingface.co/spaces/nanotron/ultrascale-playbook?section=zero_bubble_and_dualpipe\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\">Ultra-Scale Playbook</a> describes, and DeepSeek-V3&#x27;s DualPipe uses that pairing to hide its all-to-alls<sup data-k=\"dsv3-dualpipe\"><a href=\"#ref-2412.19437\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">209</a></sup>. The figure below draws one MoE layer with and without that interleaving.</p>\n"} />

<Viz name="combined-1f1b" height={498} caption="Figure 3.7.5" />

<Fragment set:html={"\n\n\n\n<p class=\"full\">Drawn with one micro-batch after the other, the layer leaves all four all-to-alls exposed. The interleaved drawing also sets <code><span class=\"nw\">--delay-wgrad-compute</span></code>, which Megatron&#x27;s MoE guide pairs with the overlap (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/README.md?plain=1#L434\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code>README.md#L434</code></a>). That flag computes the weight gradients later, so the backward dispatch waits only for the experts&#x27; input gradients and runs under that micro-batch&#x27;s own delayed expert weight gradients (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/models/common/model_chunk_schedule_plan.py#L266-L268\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">model_chunk_schedule_plan.py#L266-L268</span></code></a>), while the other three all-to-alls run under compute from the other micro-batch. On our runs, which set the overlap flag alone, the overlap raised throughput by up to 35% on two nodes. It also costs memory: rank 0 reserved 5 to 10 GB more, while its peak allocated memory barely moved.</p>\n<details><summary>Megatron source</summary><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/pipeline_parallel/combined_1f1b.py#L54-L67\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">combined_1f1b.py#L54-L67</span></code></a> (one forward beside one backward)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">54</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;&quot;&quot;Scheduler for 1f1b with no pipelining.</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">55</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">56</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-s\">This function schedules micro-batches in a way that the forward pass of Transformer layers</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">57</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-s\">for one micro-batch runs in parallel with the backward pass of another.</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">58</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-s\">Each layer&#x27;s forward and backward operations are co-scheduled to maximize the overlap of</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">59</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-s\">their computations and communications.</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">60</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-s\">EP A2A in forward step is hidden by the attention/mlp computation in the backward step,</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">61</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-s\">and vice versa.</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">62</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-s\">Assuming we have 4 microbatches, the schedule is as follows:</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">63</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-s\">Phases 0: 1st microbatch forward</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">64</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-s\">Phases 1: 1st microbatch backward + 2nd microbatch forward</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">65</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-s\">Phases 2: 2nd microbatch backward + 3rd microbatch forward</span></span></span><span TFLOP/s per GPU (<code><span class=\"nw\">--log-throughput</span></code>) before and after turning the overlap on, and keep it only if the step gets faster. Then look at a trace to see which all-to-alls are overlapped with compute.</p><p class=\"full rec\">Use it when the all-to-all is exposed and memory allows: add <code><span class=\"nw\">--overlap-moe-expert-parallel-comm</span> <span class=\"nw\">--delay-wgrad-compute</span></code>, the pair Megatron&#x27;s MoE guide recommends (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/README.md?plain=1#L434\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code>README.md#L434</code></a>), instead of <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-shared-expert-overlap</span></code>. <code><span class=\"nw\">--delay-wgrad-compute</span></code> runs only with the overlap on and with Transformer Engine layers (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/transformer_config.py#L3631-L3635\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">transformer_config.py#L3631-L3635</span></code></a>, <a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/training/arguments.py#L1956-L1958\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">arguments.py#L1956-L1958</span></code></a>). With pipeline parallelism, the overlap also needs virtual pipeline stages (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/transformer_config.py#L3565-L3599\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">transformer_config.py#L3565-L3599</span></code></a>).</p>\n<p class=\"full\">The overlap wins back time but costs memory. How can you keep a run&#x27;s memory in check without giving that time away again?</p>\n<h4 id=\"s-3-7-memory\">Memory</h4>\n<p class=\"full\">An MoE changes memory use in two ways, through its weights and through its activations. Its weights hold every expert, although each token uses only k of them, and expert parallelism spreads those experts so that each GPU keeps only its own. Its activations, the intermediate results a layer keeps for the backward pass, grow with top-k. The dispatcher copies each token&#x27;s hidden state once per selected expert (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/token_dispatcher.py#L559\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code>token_dispatcher.py#L559</code></a>), and every copy keeps its own expert activations, so a GPU whose experts receive more tokens also holds more of them.</p><p class=\"full\">The usual answer is activation recomputation, which discards activations after the forward pass and recreates them when the backward pass needs them. The <a href=\"https://huggingface.co/spaces/nanotron/ultrascale-playbook?section=activation_recomputation\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\">Ultra-Scale Playbook</a> covers its two classic forms, full recompute of every layer and selective recompute of attention. Full recompute runs each layer&#x27;s forward twice, which at micro-batch 2 cost us 24% of throughput, and the overlap from <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-7-overlap\">3.7 Overlap</a> needs it turned off (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/transformer_config.py#L3579-L3581\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">transformer_config.py#L3579-L3581</span></code></a>).</p><p class=\"full\">A finer approach starts from what each module of a layer saves for its backward. Some of those tensors are cheap to recreate, such as the outputs of the normalizations and of the experts&#x27; activation function, which take a few elementwise operations. Others are large and costly to recompute: the query, key and value that enter attention, and the dispatched tokens that enter the experts&#x27; first matrix multiply, k copies of each token. Megatron treats the two kinds differently<sup data-k=\"mcore-fgo-doc\"><a href=\"#ref-doc-6\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">210</a></sup>, and the next figure marks which saved tensor each of its options acts on.</p>"} />

<Viz name="offload-recompute-map" height={707} caption="Figure 3.7.6" />

<Fragment set:html={"<p class=\"full\">The blue labels are recomputation options. Megatron&#x27;s guide recomputes the norms and the expert activation (<code><span class=\"nw\">--recompute-granularity</span> selective <span class=\"nw\">--recompute-modules</span> layernorm moe_act</code>): it discards their cheap outputs and recreates them just before the backward that reads them (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/tensor_parallel/random.py#L1042-L1064\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">random.py#L1042-L1064</span></code></a>). The outlined labels belong to fine-grained activation offloading, and the guide offloads attention&#x27;s inputs and output and the experts&#x27; first input (<code><span class=\"nw\">--fine-grained-activation-offloading</span> <span class=\"nw\">--offload-modules</span> core_attn attn_proj expert_fc1</code>).</p><p class=\"full\">Offloading copies the inputs of those large modules to pinned CPU memory on a separate CUDA stream while later modules compute (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/pipeline_parallel/fine_grained_activation_offload.py#L1262-L1269\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code>fine_grained_activation_offload.py#L1262-L1269</code></a>). Each time the backward finishes an offloaded module, it starts copying back, on a second stream, the tensors of the next one, so they are on the GPU before they are needed (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/pipeline_parallel/fine_grained_activation_offload.py#L1332-L1342\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">#L1332-L1342</span></code></a>). Recomputation pays in compute, while offloading pays in bandwidth between host and GPU, which these copies hide behind compute. The figure below runs three layers through a forward and a backward pass with every saved input kept, with full recompute, and with that split, and tracks the saved inputs each setting holds on the GPU.</p>\n"} />

<Viz name="offload-recompute" height={677} caption="Figure 3.7.7" />

<Fragment set:html={"\n\n\n\n<p class=\"full\">With only three layers, one micro-batch and illustrative durations, full recompute reaches the lowest peak but adds a second forward pass, while the fine-grained split holds more and adds only a few small recomputes, because its copies run under compute. The last layer keeps its large inputs on the GPU, since its backward starts as soon as its forward ends and a reload would stall it (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/pipeline_parallel/fine_grained_activation_offload.py#L630-L637\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code>fine_grained_activation_offload.py#L630-L637</code></a>). Slow the host link in the simulation and the copies to the CPU finish late, so the peak rises; slow it further and the backward waits for its reloads.</p>\n<details><summary>Megatron source</summary><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/experts.py#L319-L322\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">if</span> <span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.config.moe_paged_stash:</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">861</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">permuted_local_hidden_states = paged_stash_group_start(permuted_local_hidden_states)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">862</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">max_num_tokens = permuted_local_hidden_states.shape[<span class=\"hl-m\">0</span>]</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">863</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-c\"># Average/expected tokens is a pre-padding estimate used by paged stashing heuristics.</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">864</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-c\"># moe_expert_rank_capacity_factor is required when moe_paged_stash is enabled.</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">865</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">cap_factor = <span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.config.moe_expert_rank_capacity_factor</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">866</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">avg_num_tokens = (</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">867</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\"><span class=\"hl-b\">int</span>(max_num_tokens // cap_factor)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">868</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">if</span> cap_factor <span class=\"hl-k\">is</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">not</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">None</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">and</span> cap_factor &gt; <span class=\"hl-m\">0</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">869</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">else</span> <span class=\"hl-k\">None</span></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">870</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">871</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">stash_context = get_paged_stash_context(</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">872</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\">name=<span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span><span class=\"hl-s\">grouped_mlp</span><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span>,</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">873</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\">max_num_tokens=max_num_tokens,</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">874</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\">num_tokens_tensor=tokens_per_expert.sum(),</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">875</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\">avg_num_tokens=avg_num_tokens,</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">876</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">)</span></span></pre></details></details>\n<p class=\"full\">NVIDIA measured the trade when it added the feature. On H100s, a 14-layer model with DeepSeek-V3&#x27;s layer shapes, offloading its normalization inputs and both expert inputs, reserved 18% less memory at its peak and lost 2% of throughput<sup data-k=\"mcore-fgo-pr1913\"><a href=\"#ref-doc-6\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">211</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">Offloading saves memory only while its copies stay hidden, and three settings decide how much it moves. <code><span class=\"nw\">--activation-offload-fraction</span></code> offloads only a first share of the eligible groups in forward order, and <code><span class=\"nw\">--min-offloaded-tensor-size</span></code> skips small tensors, under a million elements by default. A later pipeline stage holds fewer micro-batches in flight and needs less memory, so <code><span class=\"nw\">--delta-offload-bytes-across-pp-ranks</span></code> lets each stage offload less than the one before it.</p><p class=\"full\">Recomputation and offloading shrink the activations each layer keeps. A layer can also reserve far more memory than its activations need, and that happens once you capture the step in CUDA graphs, which replay a recorded sequence of kernels without the CPU and so need every buffer&#x27;s size fixed in advance. In a dropless MoE the number of tokens a rank receives changes from step to step, so each layer has to reserve its expert buffers for the largest load you allow: with <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-expert-rank-capacity-factor</span></code> and the HybridEP or NCCL EP backend, that is the factor times a perfectly balanced load. Every layer holds its reservation until its backward runs, yet <a href=\"https://arxiv.org/html/2603.07685v2#S4.SS3.SSS7.Px3\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\">NVIDIA&#x27;s Megatron Core MoE report</a> notes that the activations a layer actually keeps for its backward are often more than ten times smaller<sup data-k=\"mcore-paged-stash\"><a href=\"#ref-2603.07685\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">212</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">Megatron&#x27;s experimental paged stash, <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-paged-stash</span></code>, separates the space a layer computes in from the space it stores. All layers compute in one shared buffer of the reserved size, and after each layer&#x27;s forward a separate stream copies only its real tokens into small fixed-size pages, much as offloading copies tensors to the CPU. It needs <code><span class=\"nw\">--use-transformer-engine-op-fuser</span></code> and FP8 or NVFP4 experts, and since it already holds the expert activations, Megatron refuses to combine it with offloading <code>expert_fc1</code> or <code>moe_act</code><sup data-k=\"mcore-paged-stash\"><a href=\"#ref-2603.07685\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">212</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full watch\">Compare peak memory and step time before and after each change, since a run short of memory fails with an out-of-memory error on its busiest rank, and offload copies that outlast compute show up as a longer backward. The peak memory to watch is therefore the busiest rank&#x27;s. Megatron prints the peak of data-parallel rank 0 (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/training/utils/common_utils.py#L407-L427\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">common_utils.py#L407-L427</span></code></a>) and sends the last rank&#x27;s peak to <a href=\"https://huggingface.co/docs/trackio\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\">Trackio</a> through the launcher from <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-2-4\">2.4</a>. To find the busiest rank, take the maximum of <code>torch.cuda.max_memory_allocated()</code> across ranks at each log interval, or record per-rank <code><span class=\"nw\">--record-memory-history</span></code> snapshots.</p><p class=\"full rec\">Use it when the overlap does not fit in memory: recompute the cheap modules (<code><span class=\"nw\">--recompute-granularity</span> selective <span class=\"nw\">--recompute-modules</span> layernorm moe_act</code>) and offload the large inputs (<code><span class=\"nw\">--fine-grained-activation-offloading</span> <span class=\"nw\">--offload-modules</span> core_attn attn_proj expert_fc1</code>) instead of recomputing every layer, which blocks the overlap. If step time grows, lower <code><span class=\"nw\">--activation-offload-fraction</span></code>. Recomputing <code>layernorm</code> or <code>moe_act</code> does not run with FP8&#x27;s delayed recipe (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/transformer_config.py#L2422-L2433\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">transformer_config.py#L2422-L2433</span></code></a>), and with the <code>ncclep</code> backend the overlap does not run with <code>expert_fc1</code> offloading (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/transformer_config.py#L3606-L3618\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">#L3606-L3618</span></code></a>).</p>\n<p class=\"full\">So far you have tuned the system around the router&#x27;s choices without touching them. What if the router took the network into account, at a price paid in training?</p>\n<h4 id=\"s-3-7-group-limited-routing\">Group-limited routing</h4>\n<p class=\"full\">A token&#x27;s top-k experts can sit anywhere in the expert-parallel group. Once that group spans several nodes, one token may need experts on many of them, and its copies cross the slower network between nodes, both when the token is dispatched and when its outputs are combined. Plain top-k routing ignores where the experts live, so nothing keeps a token&#x27;s experts on few nodes.</p><p class=\"full\">DeepSeek-V3 adds the missing constraint with group-limited routing, which restricts where a token may look before it picks, &quot;to limit communication costs during training&quot;. Its experts sit on 8 nodes, and for every token it scores each node by summing the token&#x27;s top-2 expert scores there, including the balancing bias of <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-3\">3.3</a>. It keeps the best 4 nodes and then takes the top-k among the experts on them, so a token reaches at most 4 nodes<sup data-k=\"dsv3-s212\"><a href=\"#ref-2412.19437\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">42</a></sup>. On devices instead of nodes, the figure below draws the same rule next to standard top-k and MoGE. MoGE (Mixture of Grouped Experts), the routing behind Huawei&#x27;s Pangu Pro MoE, splits the experts into one equal group per device and takes the same number of experts from each group<sup data-k=\"pangu-pro-moge\"><a href=\"#ref-2505.21411\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">213</a></sup>. Intern-S1-Pro, trained with expert parallelism over 8 GPUs, takes one expert from each of 8 groups in the same way<sup data-k=\"interns1pro-s21\"><a href=\"#ref-src-interns1pro-s21\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">214</a></sup>.</p>\n"} />

<Viz name="device-groups" height={714} caption="Figure 3.7.8" />

<Fragment set:html={"\n\n\n<p class=\"full\">Compared device by device, group-limited routing caps how many devices each token reaches, while MoGE loads every device evenly and sends each token to all of them.</p>\n<details><summary>Megatron source</summary><details class=\"src\"><summary><a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/moe_utils.py#L713-L718\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">moe_utils.py#L713-L718</span></code></a> (rank the groups, keep the best ones)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">713</span><span class=\"code\" torch.topk(group_scores, k=group_topk, dim=-<span class=\"hl-m\">1</span>, <span class=\"hl-b\">sorted</span>=<span class=\"hl-k\">False</span>)[<span class=\"hl-m\">1</span>]</span></span><span class=\"hl-m\">1</span>)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">723</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">.expand(num_tokens, num_groups, num_experts // style=\"--i:0ch\"></span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">727</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\">masked_scores = scores.masked_fill(~score_mask.bool(), <span class=\"hl-b\">float</span>(<span class=\"hl-s\">&#x27;</span><span class=\"hl-s\">-inf</span><span class=\"hl-s\">&#x27;</span>))</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">728</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\">probs, top_indices = torch.topk(masked_scores, k=topk, dim=-<span class=\"hl-m\">1</span>)</span></span></pre></details></details>\n<p class=\"full\">What the cap saves in traffic depends on the dispatcher. Megatron&#x27;s <code>alltoall</code> dispatcher sends one copy of the token per expert, while the <code>flex</code> dispatcher with <a href=\"https://github.com/deepseek-ai/DeepEP\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\">DeepEP</a>, whose kernels follow DeepSeek-V3&#x27;s group-limited routing, sends one copy per node and forwards it inside the node over NVLink<sup class=\"fn-multi\"><a data-ks=\"dsv3-s322 deepep-readme\" href=\"#ref-2412.19437\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">215,216</a></sup>. For a layer of our 7B-A1B model, 64 experts with top-8 and a 2,048-wide hidden state in bf16, spread over 2 to 8 nodes, the following figure counts what crosses the network with and without the cap, under both dispatchers.</p>"} />

<Viz name="group-limited-bytes" height={937} caption="Figure 3.7.9" />

<Fragment set:html={"<p class=\"full\">With <code>alltoall</code>, the cap only moves a token&#x27;s copies onto fewer nodes, since every selected expert outside the token&#x27;s own node still gets its own copy; the figure&#x27;s router scores experts at random, ignoring which node the token sits on, and then as many bytes cross the network on average as without the cap. With DeepEP, each node a token reaches costs one copy, so capping the nodes caps the bytes. Reaching fewer nodes also leaves each token fewer experts to choose from, a cost paid in quality rather than in time, though a small one: when Xue et al. trained a 13B model with the group limit, its loss came out only slightly higher<sup data-k=\"terrace-t8\"><a href=\"#ref-2608.27874\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">217</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">Recent MoEs tend to drop the cap. DeepSeek, whose V3 used it, dropped it in DeepSeek-V4<sup data-k=\"dsv4-s21\"><a href=\"#ref-2606.19348\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">27</a></sup>, and GLM, Kimi, Nemotron, MiMo and Hy4 also let each token reach any expert. InclusionAI&#x27;s Ling and Ring models still keep it in the same form as DeepSeek-V3, with 8 groups of which each token keeps 4, and SK Telecom&#x27;s A.X-K2 keeps a looser version of it.</p><p class=\"full watch\">When tokens fan out across many nodes the step gets slow, so watch two numbers. The first is nodes reached per token, computed offline from the routing trace and the map of which rank holds which expert (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>). The second is tokens received per EP rank, summed from the token counts that <code><span class=\"nw\">--save-tokens-per-expert-interval</span></code> writes (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>).</p><p class=\"full rec\">Try the recommendations above first, since they leave training unchanged, and turn to the cap only if the all-to-all across nodes still takes a large share of the step: the cap changes which experts a token can pick, and so what the model learns. It helps only when expert parallelism spans several nodes and the dispatcher sends a token once per node, as the <code>flex</code> dispatcher with DeepEP does. Then set <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-router-num-groups</span></code> to the number of nodes and <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-router-group-topk</span></code> to how many of them a token may reach, and pair it with a balancer other than <code>quantile_balancing</code>, which does not run with the cap. Otherwise leave it off: on one node it saves no traffic, and with <code>alltoall</code> it saves none on average.</p>\n<p class=\"full\">The node cap limits where a token&#x27;s copies go, but an expert the router favors still takes every token sent to it, and the rank that holds it finishes last. Should you turn that expert&#x27;s extra tokens away, or copy the expert onto a quieter rank, and what does each choice cost?</p>\n<h4 id=\"s-3-7-capacity-and-replicas\">Capacity and replicas</h4>\n<p class=\"full\">The rank that holds a hot expert finishes its share of the step last, and the other ranks wait for it at the next synchronization point<sup data-k=\"mcore-s437\"><a href=\"#ref-2603.07685\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">208</a></sup>. By default, Megatron accepts that wait and keeps every token: with <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-expert-capacity-factor</span></code> unset, routing is dropless (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/transformer_config.py#L1009-L1011\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">transformer_config.py#L1009-L1011</span></code></a>), and an expert takes however many tokens the router sends it.</p><p class=\"full\">A capacity factor caps that number. Each expert keeps its highest-probability tokens up to its capacity (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/transformer_config.py#L1024-L1028\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">transformer_config.py#L1024-L1028</span></code></a>), and the tokens past it skip the expert. That buys some speed, since no expert can hold up the step with more tokens than its capacity<sup data-k=\"pangu-s32\"><a href=\"#ref-2505.04519\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">218</a></sup>, and it caps memory too, since no expert ever holds activations for more tokens than its capacity<sup data-k=\"stmoe-cf-mem\"><a href=\"#ref-2202.08906\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">219</a></sup>. For static shapes, add <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-pad-expert-input-to-capacity</span></code> as well.</p><p class=\"full\">The memory a capacity factor frees can also buy a better parallel layout. Vavre et al. at NVIDIA upcycled Llama 3-8B into an MoE with 8 experts and top-2 routing, and on 128 GPUs capacity factor 1 ran without <a href=\"https://huggingface.co/spaces/nanotron/ultrascale-playbook?section=tensor_parallelism\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\">tensor parallelism</a> at 463 TFLOP/s per GPU, against 392 for dropless training with a tensor-parallel size of 2. The authors put the gain down to the smaller memory footprint, which let them use less model parallelism<sup data-k=\"vavre-t2\"><a href=\"#ref-2412.09952\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">220</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">The other answer keeps every token and copies the hot expert instead. Three ways to handle a hot expert appear in the figure below: dropless routing, a capacity factor, and a replica of the hot expert placed the way ECHO places it, a method from the Megatron Core team&#x27;s report that copies hot experts during training<sup data-k=\"mcore-s437\"><a href=\"#ref-2603.07685\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">208</a></sup>.</p>\n"} />

<Viz name="capacity-and-redundancy" height={821} caption="Figure 3.7.10" />

<Fragment set:html={"\n\n\n\n<p class=\"full\">On one skewed toy batch of 8 experts over 4 ranks, the capacity factor drops the selections that overflow an expert, mostly the hot expert&#x27;s, and drops them in arrival order, where Megatron&#x27;s default would keep the highest-probability ones. Each dropped selection skips its expert, so the drawn step gets shorter and the model&#x27;s output changes. A replica placed on the idlest rank brings the toy&#x27;s busiest rank closer to the mean with nothing dropped.</p>\n<details><summary>Megatron source</summary><details class=\"src\"><summary><a class=\"hl-s\">}</span><span class=\"hl-s\">&quot;</span>)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1101</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"></span></span><span class=\"ln\">1103</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">if</span> pad_to_capacity:</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1104</span><span class=\"code\" final_map</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1106</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">else</span>:</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1108</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">final_map = torch.logical_and(routing_map, capacity_mask)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1109</span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1111</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">return</span> final_probs, final_map</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">1112</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"></span></span></pre></details></details>\n<p class=\"full\">Real runs drop less than the toy: at a capacity factor of 1.5, Huawei&#x27;s Pangu team saw about 6% of tokens dropped in a 20B MoE and about 8% in their 718B<sup data-k=\"pangu-s32\"><a href=\"#ref-2505.04519\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">218</a></sup>. Replicas can balance the ranks almost exactly. Wei et al.&#x27;s UltraEP, which re-plans replicas for every micro-batch and layer, took the busiest rank of three training runs, DeepSeek-V3, Qwen3-235B and GLM-4.5-Air, from 1.30 to 2.86 times the mean load in plain Megatron-LM down to 1.01 to 1.03<sup data-k=\"ultraep-f11\"><a href=\"#ref-src-ultraep-f11\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">221</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">A copy costs more in training than in serving. Each one needs the expert&#x27;s weights sent to its rank in the forward pass and its gradients reduced back to the original in the backward pass, so ECHO&#x27;s planner makes as few copies as it can: it counts each expert&#x27;s tokens above the mean load per rank, finds the spare room on the quieter ranks, and packs the first into the second, then discards the copies once they have computed. Balanced ranks pay back twice, in less waiting and in smaller buffers, since CUDA graphs size their static buffers for the worst case, the same worst-case buffers the paged stash in <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-7-memory\">3.7 Memory</a> works around<sup data-k=\"mcore-echo\"><a href=\"#ref-2603.07685\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">222</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">Serving has the same problem without the gradients, so its copies stay in place far longer than ECHO&#x27;s, which are planned anew in every forward pass. DeepSeek-V3 re-chooses its redundant experts from the load of its online service at an interval of about 10 minutes<sup data-k=\"dsv3-redundant\"><a href=\"#ref-2412.19437\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">223</a></sup>. DeepInfer, the engine DeepSeek serves V4.1-Flash with, gives every GPU spare slots for copies of the busiest experts, counts each expert&#x27;s load over a recent window, and re-places the copies every 1,000 steps in prefill and every 10,000 in decode, and with it the slowest GPU stays within 1.09 times the mean, as quoted in 3.3<sup data-k=\"deepinfer-eplb\"><a href=\"#ref-doc-1\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">71</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">Does dropping cost quality? Published comparisons say it costs some loss. Huawei&#x27;s Pangu team trained a 20B MoE with a capacity factor of 1.5 and dropless, and the dropless run kept a lower loss throughout, by a gap that grew with the number of experts<sup data-k=\"pangu-s32\"><a href=\"#ref-2505.04519\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">218</a></sup>. Gale et al. found the same when they built MegaBlocks: the loss was lowest when no token was dropped<sup data-k=\"megablocks-f2\"><a href=\"#ref-2211.15841\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">224</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">A downstream benchmark can hide that gap: the upcycled Llama 3 MoE of Vavre et al. scored within a point on MMLU at every capacity factor they tried and with dropless routing<sup data-k=\"vavre-t4\"><a href=\"#ref-2412.09952\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">225</a></sup>. Where the dropped tokens fall matters too. Meta&#x27;s MobileMoE pretrained with a capacity factor and switched to dropless routing for fine-tuning, where dropping would cut tokens out of instruction-response pairs<sup data-k=\"mobilemoe-sft\"><a href=\"#ref-2605.27358\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">226</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">A model trained with a cap need not be served with one. When Zoph et al. evaluated their ST-MoE models at a higher capacity factor than the one they were trained with, every model in their comparison scored better<sup data-k=\"stmoe-t8\"><a href=\"#ref-2202.08906\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">227</a></sup>. DeepSeek-V2 was built for that switch: it dropped tokens on each device during training but never from about 10% of its training sequences, so that at inference it could choose whether to drop tokens at all and still match how it was trained<sup data-k=\"dsv2-s224\"><a href=\"#ref-2405.04434\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">228</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full watch\">With a capacity factor on, tokens are dropped silently, so watch how often they are dropped alongside the validation loss. The routing trace that <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-routing-trace-path</span></code> writes skips every record where a token was dropped (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/router_trace.py#L353-L360\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">router_trace.py#L353-L360</span></code></a>). To count the drops, compute the share of dropped token-expert assignments per layer with a forward hook on the router: <code>1 <span class=\"nw\">-</span> kept / (tokens * topk)</code>, where <code>kept</code> counts the assignments left in the routing map.</p><p class=\"full rec\">Keep dropless as the default. When one hot expert holds the step back and the quieter ranks have memory to spare, a replica of that expert would balance the ranks without touching the loss, although ECHO&#x27;s replication is still an open pull request<sup data-k=\"mcore-s437\"><a href=\"#ref-2603.07685\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">208</a></sup>. Use a capacity factor when memory is short and you can accept a slightly higher loss; static shapes also need <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-pad-expert-input-to-capacity</span></code>. Go back to dropless for fine-tuning, where dropped tokens fall inside instruction-response pairs.</p>\n<p class=\"full\">Whether you drop tokens or keep them all, every token an expert keeps runs through its GEMMs, which so far have run in bf16. Could a lower precision such as FP8 make that work cheaper, and the activations it saves smaller, without hurting the loss?</p>\n<h4 id=\"s-3-7-precision\">Precision</h4>\n<p class=\"full\">Most of an MoE layer&#x27;s computation sits in its matrix multiplications, and much of its activation memory in the inputs those multiplications save for the backward pass. FP8 halves the bytes of both against bf16, and its Tensor Core operations run faster on Hopper and Blackwell GPUs<sup data-k=\"mcore-fp8-why\"><a href=\"#ref-2603.07685\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">229</a></sup>. How much of that reaches the step depends on how much of the step the multiplications take and on what quantizing their tensors costs, and for large MoE training the Megatron Core team reports about 10 to 25% end to end<sup data-k=\"mcore-fp8-e2e\"><a href=\"#ref-2603.07685\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">229</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">Not every operation can take the lower precision. The figure below shows where FP8 runs in one layer, forward and backward, following the split DeepSeek-V3 trained with: each linear layer runs its forward multiplication and both of its backward ones in FP8, while the embedding, the output head, the norms, the attention core and the router keep BF16 or FP32<sup data-k=\"dsv3-fp8\"><a href=\"#ref-2412.19437\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">230</a></sup>.</p>\n"} />

<Viz name="fp8-layer" height={558} caption="Figure 3.7.11" />

<Fragment set:html={"\n\n\n\n<p class=\"full\">The memory saving comes from the backward pass. The weight-gradient multiplication reads the input the forward pass saved, so keeping that input in FP8 halves it, which for DeepSeek-V3 comes to about 16 GB of activations per GPU<sup data-k=\"mcore-fp8-mem\"><a href=\"#ref-2603.07685\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">231</a></sup>. The master weights, the weight gradients and the optimizer states stay in higher precision<sup data-k=\"dsv3-fp8\"><a href=\"#ref-2412.19437\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">230</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">The router is the part to protect. Its scores pick each token&#x27;s experts, so quantization noise there can change the selection and destabilize training, which is why the Megatron Core report keeps it in FP32<sup data-k=\"mcore-s51\"><a href=\"#ref-2603.07685\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">232</a></sup>. In Megatron the router is not a <a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/TransformerEngine\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\">Transformer Engine</a> layer, so FP8 never reaches it, and <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-router-dtype</span> fp32</code> sets the precision of its math (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/moe/router.py#L125-L130\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">router.py#L125-L130</span></code></a>, <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-3-1-precision\">3.1 Precision</a>). The quantized grouped GEMM needs each expert&#x27;s token count padded to an aligned size, which <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-router-padding-for-quantization</span></code> gives it (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/transformer_config.py#L851-L854\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">transformer_config.py#L851-L854</span></code></a>).</p>\n<details><summary>Megatron source</summary><details class=\"src\"><summary><a rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">token_dispatcher.py#L652-L657</span></code></a> (pad routing for quantization)</summary><pre class=\"src\"><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">652</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:0ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">if</span> <span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.config.moe_router_padding_for_quantization:</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">653</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\">pad_multiple = get_align_size_for_quantization(<span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.config)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">654</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">if</span> is_experimental_enabled() <span class=\"hl-k\">and</span> <span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.config.moe_permute_fusion:</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">655</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\"><span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.routing_map = fused_pad_routing_map(<span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.routing_map, pad_multiple)</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">656</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:4ch\"><span class=\"hl-k\">else</span>:</span></span><span class=\"line\"><span class=\"ln\">657</span><span class=\"code\" style=\"--i:8ch\"><span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.routing_map = pad_routing_map(<span class=\"hl-b\">self</span>.routing_map, pad_multiple)</span></span></pre></details></details>\n<p class=\"full\">The recipe sets how finely the scales follow the data. With one scale per tensor, the largest outlier sets the range for every other value, so DeepSeek-V3 and Ling 2.0 scale activations and gradients per tile of 1\u00d7128 elements and weights per block of 128\u00d7128<sup class=\"fn-multi\"><a data-ks=\"dsv3-fp8-tiles ling-fp8\" href=\"#ref-2412.19437\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">233,234</a></sup>. The Megatron Core report recommends this blockwise recipe on Hopper, and MXFP8, with one scale per 32 elements, on Blackwell<sup data-k=\"mcore-fp8-recipes\"><a href=\"#ref-2603.07685\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">235</a></sup>. You pick them with <code><span class=\"nw\">--fp8-format</span></code> and <code><span class=\"nw\">--fp8-recipe</span></code>, and the blockwise recipe needs Transformer Engine 2.3 or newer (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/fp8_utils.py#L799\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code>fp8_utils.py#L799</code></a>).</p><p class=\"full\">FP8 can also cross the network. DeepSeek-V3 quantizes the tokens to FP8 before dispatch, since the experts&#x27; first layer reads them in FP8 anyway; &quot;a similar strategy is applied to the activation gradient before MoE down-projections&quot;, while &quot;both the forward and backward combine components&quot; stay in BF16<sup data-k=\"dsv3-fp8-comm\"><a href=\"#ref-2412.19437\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">236</a></sup>. In Megatron, <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-dispatch-fwd-dtype</span> mxfp8</code> and <code><span class=\"nw\">--moe-combine-bwd-dtype</span> mxfp8</code> send the dispatch payload and the gradient going back through combine in MXFP8. Both need the <code>ncclep</code> backend of the flex dispatcher (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/transformer_config.py#L2150-L2158\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">transformer_config.py#L2150-L2158</span></code></a>), and MXFP8 itself needs Blackwell-class GPUs (<a href=\"https://github.com/NVIDIA/Megatron-LM/blob/d6316eb45bf1bfbb6f1f848c0b56e55166564d80/megatron/core/transformer/transformer_config.py#L656-L661\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"><code><span class=\"nw\">#L656-L661</span></code></a>).</p><p class=\"full\">Long runs report the loss holding. DeepSeek trained a 16B and a 230B MoE in FP8 for about a trillion tokens each, and both stayed within 0.25% relative error of their BF16 baselines<sup data-k=\"dsv3-fp8-val\"><a href=\"#ref-2412.19437\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">237</a></sup>. Ling-1T held within the same bound, about 0.1% on average, over 900B tokens, while FP8 raised its MFU by about 15%<sup data-k=\"ling-fp8\"><a href=\"#ref-2510.22115\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">234</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">Four bits is the next step down, and the first large MoEs have been pretrained there. NVIDIA trained Nemotron 3 Super, 120B parameters with 12B active, on 25T tokens in NVFP4<span class=\"sn\">A 4-bit floating-point format with one FP8 scale per block of 16 elements and a second, per-tensor FP32 scale.</span>, running all three multiplications of most linear layers in it. The exceptions show where 4 bits are too few: the last 15% of the network, the attention projections, the embeddings and the multi-token prediction layers stay in BF16, and the Mamba output projection runs in MXFP8 because it underflowed in NVFP4. The recipe also scales weights in two-dimensional blocks, so the forward and backward passes see the same quantized weights, applies random Hadamard transforms to the inputs of the weight-gradient multiplication and rounds gradients stochastically<sup data-k=\"nemotron3s-nvfp4\"><a href=\"#ref-2604.12374\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">238</a></sup>. Nemotron 3 Ultra, 550B with 55B active, followed on 20T tokens with the same recipe, and when NVIDIA branched BF16 runs off its checkpoints, the loss gap to them stayed below 0.4% on average<sup data-k=\"nemotron3u-nvfp4\"><a href=\"#ref-2606.15007\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">239</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full\">FP4 shrinks the saved inputs to a quarter of their BF16 size, against half for FP8<sup data-k=\"mcore-fp4-mem\"><a href=\"#ref-2603.07685\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">231</a></sup>, and native FP4 multiplications need Blackwell-class GPUs<sup data-k=\"nvfp4-hw\"><a href=\"#ref-2509.25149\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">240</a></sup>. Hopper has no 4-bit Tensor Core instructions, yet Zhang et al. at Zhejiang Lab stored the activations and the forward dispatch traffic of a 671B MoE in software-emulated MXFP4 while its multiplications stayed in FP8, and cut peak activation memory by 14.8% while matching the convergence of a strong FP8 baseline. The memory it freed let the run recompute less, and it ran 12.5% faster than the best FP8 setting that fit<sup data-k=\"fp4-hopper\"><a href=\"#ref-2603.02731\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">241</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full watch\">An FP8 run&#x27;s loss should stay close to BF16&#x27;s for the whole run, so compare it with a BF16 run of the same config as a relative difference. DeepSeek&#x27;s 16B and 230B runs and Ling-1T all stayed under 0.25%, so a gap well past that, or one that keeps growing, points at the recipe. Under NVFP4, also count the weight-gradient elements that are exactly zero in the expert layers. In Nemotron 3 Super they grew to 7% of all parameters by the end of pretraining, as low-norm channels in some experts shrank toward zero, and in a smaller ablation on Nemotron 3 Nano, switching back to BF16 brought the count back to its baseline<sup data-k=\"nemotron3s-zeros\"><a href=\"#ref-2604.12374\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">242</a></sup>.</p><p class=\"full rec\">Use FP8 when the linear layers take most of the step, with the blockwise recipe on Hopper and MXFP8 on Blackwell, and keep the router, the norms and the attention core in higher precision. On a large MoE, expect about 10 to 25% more throughput and a loss within a fraction of a percent of BF16. On Blackwell, NVFP4 is the next step down, with the Nemotron 3 recipe keeping the last layers, the attention projections and the embeddings in BF16.</p>\n<h4>What to watch</h4>\n<table class=\"glance\"><thead><tr><th>Part</th><th>Symptom</th><th>Signal to watch</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>Profiling</td><td><code>slow</code>: the step takes longer than it should.</td><td>Step time or TFLOP/s first. Then, from a trace, how much of the step the all-to-all leaves exposed.</td></tr><tr><td>Interconnect</td><td><code>slow</code> on several nodes: when NCCL falls back to TCP sockets, our EP16 proxy ran about 25 times slower, 1.7 against 42.0 TFLOP/s.</td><td>With <code>NCCL_DEBUG=INFO</code>, check &quot;Using network&quot; for the intended transport: IB for InfiniBand or Libfabric for the OFI plugin on EFA; Socket indicates fallback. Then compare nccl-tests alltoall bus bandwidth inside and across nodes (<a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-2-1\">2.1</a>), and TFLOP/s at node-local versus cross-node EP.</td></tr><tr><td>Overlap</td><td><code>slow</code>: the all-to-all is not hidden behind compute.</td><td>TFLOP/s per GPU (<code><span class=\"nw\">--log-throughput</span></code>) before and after. Use the exposed all-to-all share only to explain the change.</td></tr><tr><td>Memory</td><td><code>OOM</code>: one rank runs out of memory. <code>slow</code>: recomputation, or offload reloads that outlast compute, add step time.</td><td>Compare step time and peak memory after each change. Use the busiest rank&#x27;s peak: maximum <code>torch.cuda.max_memory_allocated()</code> across ranks or per-rank <code><span class=\"nw\">--record-memory-history</span></code> snapshots. Longer backward can expose offload reloads that outlast compute.</td></tr><tr><td>Group-limited routing</td><td><code>slow</code>: each token fans out across nodes.</td><td>How many nodes each token reaches (routing trace and the map of experts to ranks), and the tokens each EP rank receives (token counts), <a class=\"xref\" href=\"#s-a-1\">A.1</a>.</td></tr><tr><td>Capacity and replicas</td><td><code>learns badly</code>: tokens are dropped silently. <code>OOM</code>: one rank runs out of memory.</td><td>Share of dropped token-expert assignments per layer, <code>1 <span class=\"nw\">-</span> kept / (tokens * topk)</code>, from a router forward hook, alongside validation loss. The routing trace skips records with drops.</td></tr><tr><td>Precision</td><td><code>slow</code>: no speedup over bf16. <code>learns badly</code>: a higher loss than bf16.</td><td>Relative loss difference against a BF16 run of the same config across training; DeepSeek&#x27;s 16B and 230B runs and Ling-1T stayed below 0.25%<sup class=\"fn-multi\"><a data-ks=\"dsv3-fp8-val ling-fp8\" href=\"#ref-2412.19437\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">237,234</a></sup>. Under NVFP4, also count exactly zero expert weight-gradient elements: these reached 7% of all parameters in Nemotron 3 Super<sup data-k=\"nemotron3s-zeros\"><a href=\"#ref-2604.12374\" data-footnote-ref=\"\">242</a></sup>.</td></tr></tbody></table>\n<p class=\"full\">You now have every part of an MoE layer, from the router that picks the experts to the precision they compute in. Once training ends, which of those choices still matter when the model is served?</p>\n"} />
